SCHOTT Pharma · 1SXP-DE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 11 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: SCHOTT Pharma

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 47,745 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces support volume and undermine price. Demand is structurally strong (biologics, GLP-1 injectables, ready-to-use conversion, Annex 1, US onshoring), the destocking cycle has ended, and regulation favours approved incumbents. Against that, the three leaders are building syringe and ready-to-use capacity together, utilisation is already slipping, customers have shown they can cut volumes under "take-or-pay" contracts, and per-dose prices of obesity drugs are falling down the chain. Over the next 6 months the read-across from Stevanato dominates sentiment more than fundamentals. Over 6 to 18 months volume tailwinds dominate. From 2028 the overcapacity and orals debates decide the multiple. SCHOTT Pharma is better hedged than Stevanato across formats (vials, cartridges, syringes), which makes it a relative winner in the format-shift risk. It is not a winner in the pricing risk, which falls on every supplier with new capacity to fill.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0238C-0545C-0747C-0944C-3572C-3968
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F3841st of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: intensity is rising, mainly in glass and polymer syringes and in bulk glass. SCHOTT Pharma's strongest areas, vials and specialty containment, are less exposed. The direction is unfavourable for 2027 to 2030, partly offset by Gerresheimer's retreat.

  • Capacity is going in together. Stevanato (Fishers, Indiana, at about 50% occupancy; Latina), SCHOTT Pharma (Hungary syringes +60%, Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant over EUR 100m, St. Gallen cartridges, Lebanon vials, North Carolina syringes) and BD are all adding. Glass syringe utilisation fell from 100% to 90% in 2026 (Aptar). Experts project 10% to 20% excess syringe capacity within three to five years and 20% overcapacity by 2030, against an equilibrium utilisation of 92% to 95%.
  • The Stevanato shock of 3-Nov-2026 raises intensity now. One GLP-1 originator cut its Nexa syringe demand, turning Stevanato's 2027 growth guidance to low single digits. Stevanato says it has backfilled "a majority" of the lost revenue. That backfilling is volume it must win from somebody, in an industry where large buyers see SCHOTT Pharma and Stevanato syringes as "functionally equivalent" and award on proximity and supply availability (Eli Lilly engineering adviser). Expect sharper syringe tenders in 2027.
  • New contestants in SCHOTT Pharma's niches. Stevanato's Nexa Flex polymer syringe challenges SCHOTT Pharma's 60% to 70% polymer share. West's Synchrony prefilled syringe brand adds a premium entrant. Chinese and Indian makers take share in bulk vials and ampoules with shorter lead times and steep discounts, as they did earlier in heparin.
  • The offset. Gerresheimer is shrinking: leverage 4.7x to 4.95x, a BaFin bill-and-hold probe, the Chicago Heights moulded-glass plant closed in September 2026, and some customers have "lost trust". SCHOTT Pharma is the natural beneficiary in vials and molded glass. Gerresheimer's Centor sale (EUR 1.5bn) leaves it a slimmer syringe and tubular-glass rival, which could later turn more aggressive.
  • Net for the model. Assume bulk-glass price concessions of 1% to 3% a year at renewal, against the historical low-to-mid single-digit increases. Assume flat to slightly negative like-for-like syringe pricing from 2027. Ready-to-use premiums hold for now but narrow as capacity rises: the ready-to-use 6ml vial price fell from EUR 0.75 to EUR 0.35 to 0.40 in 2026, according to a Gerresheimer executive.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C4519th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: neutral to a slight headwind. Glass tubing supply is secure and, if anything, in surplus. Energy is partly shielded because the parent melts the glass. The Swiss franc cost base, freight, the single cyclic-olefin-copolymer resin supplier and the concentrated elastomer supply are mild, persistent headwinds. Raw-material index clauses limit the damage, with a lag.

  • Tubing is the largest cost: about 15% of revenue, bought mostly from SCHOTT AG under an arm's-length framework agreement fixed until December 2027, with automatic three-year renewal, volume discounts and first access to innovations. The parent says pharma tubing suffers "persistent market overcapacity", which favours the buyer. The parent controls 60% to 70% of global pharma tubing; Stevanato buys from SCHOTT and Nippon Electric Glass. Cost risk would arise only if the parent re-prices at the 2027 renewal. The electric melting tank (FIOLAX Pro, 2027, about 30% lower carbon footprint) is a cost and carbon positive for the medium term.
  • Energy: about 30% of a standard vial's cost is energy (expert). The energy-intensive melting sits at the parent; SCHOTT Pharma's forming and finishing sites remain exposed to utility prices. A Middle East escalation in 2026 raised freight (composite rates +111% year on year in late 2026) and energy risk.
  • Concentrated secondary inputs: cyclic olefin copolymer comes from Topas under a long-standing agreement; elastomer plungers and stoppers come mainly from West, which holds 95% of GLP-1 high-value elastomer components and keeps its own pricing power.
  • Currency of costs: Swiss operating costs exceed Swiss revenue, with a net short exposure of CHF 135m. A 10% franc rise cuts group EBITDA by about 3%. Serbia's net short exposure rose to EUR 15m as production moved there. These are structural cost-side currency headwinds while the franc stays strong.
  • Pass-through: contracts index commodity glass every six months and labour and overhead once a year (every February). Margins therefore lag inflation by one to two quarters but recover it.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

B4711th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro barely touches demand, which is non-cyclical. It bites hardest through currency, at the company level, and through the discount rate and risk appetite on the share price, at the sector and market level.

  • Company level, currency: about 50% of revenue is in non-euro currencies, with the US dollar about 30%. A 10% dollar move shifts revenue about 3%, EBITDA about 3.5% and EPS about 5%. Translation is unhedged; transaction hedges run at most 12 months (EUR 292m of forwards at September 2025); no hedge accounting is applied, so derivative swings run through the income statement. FX took 5.6 points off FY2024 growth and about 3 points off FY2025; in FY2026 it is slightly positive to neutral. House views on euro–dollar for mid-2027 split: Morgan Stanley 1.10 (a tailwind), Scotiabank 1.18 and Deutsche Bank 1.20 by late 2026 (a headwind), from a spot of 1.12. Model FX as a symmetrical ±3% EPS range for FY2027 with a slight headwind bias, plus the Swiss franc cost drag.
  • Sector and industry level, demand: none. The Q2 FY2026 interim report states the Middle East conflict and trade restrictions had "no relevant direct impact". Pharma capital-spending restraint slows some equipment-heavy decisions, such as line conversion to ready-to-use. That marginally slows the format shift, not container volumes.
  • Market level: the Fitness for Fighting regime is risk-off beneath an index at its high. Healthcare relative strength is graded A (bought as a defensive), but Fragility is Double Underweight and Momentum Underweight. SCHOTT Pharma is a EUR 3.1bn stock with an 18% to 23% free float and US$4m to 6m a day traded. That is the fragile end the market sells first, whatever the fundamentals.
  • Rates: low leverage means rates bite through the multiple, not the income statement. FY2025 interest of EUR 19.7m was reduced by refinancing within the group in FY2026.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–44

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: four further dynamics matter. One is negative and immediate (the read-across from Stevanato's customer cut). One is negative and slow (generic semaglutide driving per-dose price deflation down the chain). One is positive and slow (Gerresheimer's retreat and trust deficit). One is two-sided and dated (fill-finish capacity arriving in 2027 to 2028). Together they net slightly negative for the next 18 months.

  • Stevanato read-across (negative, now). Stevanato's shares fell 28% on 3-Nov-2026 and SCHOTT Pharma's fell from about EUR 25 to 26 in October to about EUR 21 (inferred timing from the 50-day average of EUR 24.0 and the P/E falling from 20.8x on 22-Oct-26 to 17.4x). Investors now question every GLP-1 contract. It also frees Stevanato syringe capacity to bid against SCHOTT Pharma (C-02).
  • Generic semaglutide (negative, slow; volume up, price down). In India the monthly price fell from 16,400 to 3,400 rupees within weeks of patent expiry, and Canadian Ozempic value sales fell 29% under 65% price cuts. Generics raise unit volumes (BD has signed over 80 GLP-1 biosimilar device deals), but the buyers are price-led, which favours lower-cost formats such as vials and pens and lower-price suppliers.
  • Gerresheimer's retreat (positive, slow). Customers "hesitant to award new business" to Gerresheimer (former Stevanato manager), Chicago Heights closed, type 1 glass customers being transferred. SCHOTT Pharma's clean audit record is a commercial asset in this contest.
  • Fill-finish capacity (two-sided, 2027 to 2028). It adds container demand as new lines fill; but if the obesity boom slows, the same overbuild produces the pandemic-style overhang that a former Thermo Fisher project manager warns of.
  • Patient attrition (65% stop within a year; 8% persist at three years) caps the steady-state volume per patient that capacity plans assume.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

B+727th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the threat from entrants is low in high-value Western products and moderate in bulk and emerging markets. Adjacent entrants (West in syringes; device makers) are the most credible. Standard low-cost entrants (Chinese and Indian makers) are real in bulk vials and ampoules but are blocked in the United States by validation and, soon, the BIOSECURE Act. Orthogonal entrants (needle-free injectors, patch pumps, oral drugs) are a slow, long-term threat to unit volume.

  • Adjacent: West's Synchrony syringe applies pricing pressure (expert); Stevanato's polymer entry; device makers integrate primary containers into platforms. Threat: moderate.
  • Standard: Chinese and Indian container makers with shorter lead times and big discounts in commoditised vials; bulk is growing only 1% to 3% a year anyway. Threat: moderate in bulk (about 40% of SCHOTT Pharma's sales are not high-value), low in high-value.
  • Orthogonal or innovative: Crossject's needle-free Zeneo, insulin patch pumps (Omnipod), reusable electromechanical injectors (SHL's Elexy), oral peptides. Threat: low within four years, rising thereafter.
  • Entry barriers remain high: cleanroom capital, ISO 15378, 9- to 12-month qualification, multi-year stability data, and, from 2026, the FDA's tougher container-closure guidance.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

B+686th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are a support. The parent supplies most tubing on preferential, arm's-length terms in a tubing market with spare capacity, which gives SCHOTT Pharma a cost and security edge over Stevanato and Gerresheimer, who buy tubing from SCHOTT AG, Corning or Nippon Electric Glass. Risks are concentrated in single-source resins and elastomers and in the parent's December 2027 renewal.

  • Tubing: about 15% of revenue; volume discounts and first access to innovations; parent's electric melting tank from 2027 (lower carbon footprint). Experts see "no viable disruption from cheaper Chinese glass".
  • Single-source items: cyclic olefin copolymer from Topas (binding volume and capacity agreements); elastomer components from West (a 95% share in GLP-1 high-value parts, with its own pricing power). Dual-sourcing is pursued where regulation allows; supplier risk assessments run twice a year.
  • Structural ceiling: dependence on one tubing source limits SCHOTT Pharma's ability to play suppliers against each other if the parent re-prices on energy costs.
1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0170C-0355C-0464C-0868C-3255C-3772C-4364
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

C+7013th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand is improving on both counts. Structurally, biologics, obesity drugs (GLP-1s), self-administration and the move to ready-to-use formats keep the addressable market growing about 9% a year. Cyclically, the 2022 to 2025 destocking is over. Visibility is good on volume floors and poor on the size of individual customers' orders. The one cyclical swing that is not visible is vaccines, still falling.

  • Structural, accelerating in value more than in units. The addressable injectables packaging market is EUR 3.9bn, growing 9% a year. Biologics are 78% of injectable approvals. Injections were 89.9% of United States prescriptions for the new obesity drugs in September 2026. Ready-to-use cartridges are under 5% penetrated, against a forecast 66% to 70% within about ten years, and a ready-to-use cartridge sells for 5 to 10 times a bulk one. This is a value-mix tailwind that runs for years.
  • Cyclical, now turning up. Destocking from 2022 to 2025 has ended: West's chief executive said "destocking is pretty much over", Merck & Co confirmed normal ordering, and book-to-bill has been above 100% "for several months" (EQUI.TS). SCHOTT Pharma's DCS grew 11% organically in the third quarter. Customers' safety stocks are now 3 to 4 months, against 9 months before. That is the permanent part of the change: purchasing is now hand-to-mouth, so orders track use more closely and visibility per quarter is shorter.
  • The negative swing still running is vaccines. mRNA vaccine work fell from about 10% of sales (FY2024) to 8% (FY2025) and about 5% (FY2026). Vaccine packaging is 15% to 20% of sales (Jefferies), and some vaccines such as Gardasil face sharp cuts. This drag explains most of the gap between the market's 9% growth and the company's 4% to 6% organic growth in FY2025 and FY2026.
  • Visibility. Contracts run 3 to 5 years, with prices locked for 3 years and minimum volumes; there are over EUR 1bn of GLP-1 contracts through 2030 with the two obesity-drug leaders, and a EUR 700m lifetime glass-syringe GLP-1 contract. The 2025 to 2026 episodes (SCHOTT Pharma's syringe customer; Stevanato's GLP-1 originator, which removed about 10 points of Stevanato's growth) show the limit. Contracts protect against a customer switching supplier, not against a customer ordering less; the compensation then partly offsets the lost volume. Prepayments of about EUR 35m against over EUR 1bn of contracts (3.5%) show how thin the financial lock is.
  • For the model. Underlying end-market growth for SCHOTT Pharma's mix is about 7% to 8% a year. That is 9% for injectables, less about 1 to 2 points for vaccine and polymer drag, which fades as those lines shrink. The drag fades through FY2027 and FY2028, so the company's organic growth should converge on the market's from below rather than above, provided the obesity format decisions (below) do not go against it.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C-5521st of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: two disruption forces act on the company. Ready-to-use formats under Annex 1 are strengthening, and they help SCHOTT Pharma. Oral obesity drugs, multi-dose formats and polymer substitution are strengthening at the margin but not yet proven against injectables. Polymer as a mass-market material has effectively been disproved, which hurts SCHOTT Pharma's own polymer investment. Over 18 months the net direction is mildly positive; beyond 2028 it is uncertain and skewed negative.

  • Ready-to-use conversion (positive, strengthening). Annex 1 shifts sterilisation liability to the supplier. The industry alliance for ready-to-use formats (SCHOTT Pharma, Gerresheimer and Stevanato, launched in September 2024) is promoting conversion. A US$2m-plus bulk filling line takes 20 years to depreciate, so conversion runs at the pace of new drug launches and line replacements, not at the pace of the regulation. It is a decade-long tailwind worth perhaps 1 to 2 points of value growth a year, not a step change.
  • Oral GLP-1s (negative, strengthening in forecasts, unproven in data). Forecasts range from 30% of the GLP-1 market moving to orals (Stevanato) to about 40% by 2030 (Deutsche Bank) and 50% by 2028 (Bernstein). Eli Lilly is building a US$6bn orforglipron plant. The evidence so far points the other way: Foundayo took about 5% of Lilly's obesity scripts in four months, about 80% of its patients were new to the class, and Rothschild & Co Redburn finds no sign of the Wegovy pill cannibalising pens. The daily pill needs about 73 times the semaglutide per patient, which keeps injections cheaper to supply. Orals currently expand the market more than they substitute.
  • Format shifts within injectables (mixed, and better for SCHOTT Pharma than for Stevanato). A monthly multi-dose pen replaces four weekly prefilled syringes with one cartridge. Lilly and Novo have pushed cash-pay vials, which drove West's stopper growth. SCHOTT Pharma is first in vials (about 50% share per Deutsche Bank, over 20% per an expert), second in cartridges (34%) and third in glass syringes. It is therefore hedged across formats. A shift away from syringes hurts the syringe leader most, which is what hit Stevanato.
  • Polymer (negative for SCHOTT Pharma's own assets, a disruption disproved). Polymer syringes cost 2.5 to 3 times glass, are more permeable to oxygen and have a 1% cosmetic reject rate. Customers use them only where glass fails, for example ultra-cold storage at minus 100 degrees Celsius. SCHOTT Pharma built automated polymer lines for the mRNA boom and leads the niche with 60% to 70% share, but those lines are now under-used.
  • Less discussed but more immediate: a clinical move from weekly to monthly injections would cut unit demand directly, and patients drop out heavily (65% stop within a year).
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C+6415th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: substantial change is under way, and almost all of it favours an incumbent with approved capacity in Europe and the United States. Regulation is a net tailwind of moderate size. The main political risk is a US tariff on European glass inputs, which local finishing plants do not fully hedge.

  • Annex 1 (EU sterile manufacturing rule): pushes customers to buy ready-to-use formats and obliges upgrades across about 1,200 fill-finish suites (BEW Engineering).
  • US FDA draft container-closure guidance (August 2026): replaces a 25-year-old framework and requires full extractables-and-leachables testing. That raises the qualification bar and favours suppliers with long testing histories. Morgan Stanley expects an "upgrade cycle", and biosimilars face the same bar as innovators.
  • US onshoring and tariffs: a tariff proclamation of 2-Apr-2026 imposes duties of up to 100% on patented drugs and active ingredients, and customers demand local supply. SCHOTT Pharma's Lebanon, Pennsylvania plant (US$60m, BARDA-supported, tripled ready-to-use vial capacity) and the planned North Carolina syringe plant (US$371m, announced 2024 for commercial start in FY2027; the files hold no 2026 update on its progress) fit this. Gerresheimer's tariff experience shows the risk. SCHOTT Pharma's own risk is that its glass tubing still comes from European parent furnaces.
  • The BIOSECURE Act (list of companies of concern due 18-Dec-2026): restricts Chinese suppliers in US pharma supply chains, which protects Western containment makers from the low-cost Chinese threat in the United States.
  • The Inflation Reduction Act's "pill penalty" (9 years before price negotiation for small molecules against 13 for biologics): tilts research toward injectable biologics.
  • Drug-price policy cuts per-dose prices, for example Novo Nordisk's lower US prices from January 2027. It widens patient access (volume up) while putting pressure on the supply chain's prices over time.
  • Smaller costs: packaging recyclability rules (PPWR) and the science-based climate targets add compliance costs.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C6819th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, two industry drivers are accelerating demand: GLP-1 and biologic injectables (volume), and ready-to-use and Annex 1 conversion (value per unit). SCHOTT Pharma captures less of the GLP-1 syringe acceleration than Stevanato. It captures more of the vial and cartridge side of the same trends, and that side looks less exposed to the format risk now hitting syringes.

  • GLP-1: a low-teens share of group sales (Deutsche Bank), so about EUR 120m to 135m (derived), with Eli Lilly about two-thirds of it. Growth was "clear double digit" in the first half. Over EUR 1bn of contracts run through 2030, which implies annual GLP-1 revenue rising toward EUR 170m to 200m by 2028 to 2030 (derived: contract value spread over the period). That is worth about 1 to 1.5 points of group growth a year.
  • Ready-to-use and Annex 1: high-value products rose from 55% of sales (FY2024) to 57% (FY2025 and nine months FY2026), against a target above 60%. The ready-to-use cartridge market grows about 20% a year (expert).
  • Biologics and filling capacity: fill-finish capacity additions by Lonza (Stein, from 2028), Rovi (625m to 810m syringes by end-2026), Thermo Fisher (+40% to 60%) and Novo's Catalent sites mean more containers are filled from 2027 to 2028, a demand pull for primary packaging.
  • Where SCHOTT Pharma captures less: in GLP-1 syringes, the largest new contracts went to Stevanato, which is primary syringe supplier for two Lilly and two Novo products and makes about 800m glass syringes a year against SCHOTT Pharma's 400m. The acceleration in syringes now carries format risk, as Stevanato's cut shows.
  • Net: industry drivers add about 2 to 3 points of growth a year above a no-driver base of about 4% to 5%. That is consistent with the 6% to 8% medium-term target, not above it.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5533rd of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: demand supports the volume thesis and only partly supports the margin thesis. Volumes are growing in GLP-1 glass syringes, cartridges and specialty vials, and destocking is over. But customers are more price-conscious and more willing to renegotiate, and in obesity drugs two buyers control format choice. Support is fragile in the 10% to 15% of sales tied to GLP-1 and in under-used polymer capacity; it is robust in the core.

  • Supportive: DCS growth of 9.2% in the nine months and 11% in the third quarter; GLP-1 orders "continuous and strong growth in all key regions"; "increasing dynamics from non-mRNA" polymer use; book-to-bill above 100%; biologics approvals rising. Packaging is under 1% of drug cost, so quality and supply security dominate purchase decisions (MSD procurement).
  • Fragile: customers now hold three to four months of stock, not nine, so order swings pass straight through. They renegotiate take-or-pay terms when supply loosens (Jefferies: customers "possess greater negotiating power than previously assumed"). Volume can be cut even under contract (SCHOTT Pharma 2025; Stevanato 2026). Within obesity drugs, cash-pay vial pushes, multi-dose pens and pills all change unit demand.
  • The "use, refill or price" split: in FY2026, use (GLP-1, biologics) is perhaps 5 to 6 points of the recovery, refill (end of vial destocking, extra shifts) 1 to 2 points, and price about zero. The model should not extrapolate refill.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

B+726th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators help, and the help is durable because each rule raises the qualification bar and the value of approved capacity. Political actors help through US onshoring and anti-China supply rules, and could hurt through tariffs on European glass inputs or drug-price policy that squeezes the supply chain. The regulatory position is robust; the political one is moderately fragile.

  • Helping, robust: Annex 1 (ready-to-use formats), the FDA draft container-closure guidance (testing burden favours incumbents), the Inflation Reduction Act's tilt to biologics, and the switching rules that bind containment to the drug's registration. Recent rule changes giving drug makers "slightly more flexibility" on tubing supplier changes are a mild loosening at the margin, still a "controlled process".
  • Helping, more fragile: US tariffs and onshoring push local-for-local (Lebanon, North Carolina); BIOSECURE limits Chinese rivals; BARDA co-funding.
  • Hurting potentially: a broad US tariff on European medical glass components would hit inputs that come from European parent furnaces. US drug-price agreements and generic entry compress per-dose economics, which eventually reach packaging prices. The EU packaging recyclability rules (2030) add costs.
C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

D6429th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic addressable market is about EUR 3.9bn today (injectable primary packaging), of which SCHOTT Pharma holds 21%. It grows about 9% a year in value, faster in ready-to-use formats. With mix headwinds fading and share roughly stable, it allows 6% to 8% organic growth for SCHOTT Pharma over FY2027 to FY2029, and 8% or more only if the company wins a fair share of new GLP-1 and ready-to-use cartridge volume.

  • Market growth inputs: biologics about 9% a year; injectables packaging 9%; the GLP-1 market expected above US$100bn by 2030; ready-to-use cartridges about 20% a year; bulk glass 1% to 3% a year.
  • Format runway: cartridges about 1% to 5% ready-to-use today, 66% to 70% expected in about ten years; vials under 5%, against 60% expected. The value pool expands several times as units convert at 5 to 10 times the price.
  • Drags: vaccines (15% to 20% of sales, falling) and polymer mRNA (5% of sales, after falling from 10%) take 1 to 2 points off growth until they stop falling (likely by FY2028).
  • Consensus fits this: 5.6% (FY2026, inflated by the payment), 6.0%, 6.9% and 6.3% for FY2027 to FY2029. That is the low end of the 6% to 8% target and does not need share gains.
1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0666C-1058
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C+6612th of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the cycle troughed in the first half of FY2026 and turned in the third quarter. Organic growth went from 2.3% in the first half to 8.3% in the third quarter, with book-to-bill above one. But the turn is in volume, not yet in margin. The FY2027 comparison will look like a deceleration even if underlying demand holds.

  • Trough: second-quarter FY2026 organic growth was flat (DDS -9.8%), the EBITDA margin was 26.0% and there was the syringe write-down. Deutsche Bank called the first half "the absolute bottom of the cycle".
  • Turn: in the third quarter, revenue was EUR 281.8m, +8.3% organic against consensus of about 3.5%. DCS grew 11% (cartridges, specialty vials) and DDS 5% (GLP-1 glass syringes up, non-mRNA polymer improving). Order visibility extends into FY2027 in both segments (second-quarter call).
  • What has not turned: the third-quarter EBITDA margin fell to 26.8% from 32.3%. Gross margin for the nine months was 31.3% against 34.7%, and cost of sales grew 9.6% against revenue up 4.1%. High-value products were 59% of third-quarter sales against 60% a year earlier, so the mix shift has stalled. Volume is recovering into a cost base that has grown ahead of it (new sites, depreciation, polymer under-absorption, "production optimisation costs").
  • "Use, refill or price" (Wisdom Library): part of the turn is refill, meaning destocking ending in vials and first-quarter "supplemental orders and extra factory shifts" (the finance director's words). Part is use (GLP-1, cartridges). Very little is price. The refill part does not repeat. Underlying use-driven growth is closer to 6% to 7% than to 8.3%.
  • Next six months: the fourth quarter, at about 9% reported growth including the EUR 15m payment and about 3% to 5% without it, will land FY2026 at or above the top of the 5% to 6% guidance. On reported numbers, the first half of FY2027 faces easy comparisons (weak first half of FY2026) and the second half hard ones (strong third quarter and the fourth-quarter payment). Reported growth should therefore look strong in the first half of FY2027 and weaker in the second.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C-5823rd of 54

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the tailwinds are structural, broad and durable. The headwinds are concentrated in one place, syringe and bulk-glass pricing as capacity outruns demand, and they arrive from 2027. The overall external picture is moderately positive for volume through FY2028 and neutral to negative for price. For the model: volume growth in the 7% to 8% range, like-for-like price flat to slightly negative. The external environment alone does not deliver the margin expansion to 30%; that must come from inside.

Driver by driver (the Sector Digest's four stories, then the Industry's forces):

  • Ready-to-fill cartridges and syringes ("big for SCHOTT Pharma, the format shift early"): a strong, long tailwind in value per unit; slow, because bulk lines take 20 years to depreciate.
  • Approved capacity as the scarce asset: a tailwind while utilisation stays near 92% to 95%. It is turning into a headwind in syringes (90% already) and then more broadly by 2028 to 2030. The Sector Digest's own test is that the shortage premium ends around end-2027.
  • Consumables restocking and sterile-filling upgrades (Industry force): a tailwind in FY2026 and FY2027 that has largely played out by FY2028; part of it is refill.
  • Obesity medicine as pills and injections (Industry force): a volume tailwind for now (pills add patients); price per dose falls (Novo's lower US prices from January 2027; autoinjector prices expected to fall up to 50% over five years). It turns into a headwind only if orals take share faster than the current data show.
  • US manufacturing relocation (Industry force): a tailwind to plant owners with US capacity, with a lag, as plants ramp. SCHOTT Pharma has Lebanon running and North Carolina pending.
  • Regulation (C-04): a tailwind.
  • Inputs and macro (C-05, C-07): neutral, with currency the swing factor (±3% EPS).

Taken together: value-chain level positive (customers' demand, filling capacity, regulation); industry level turning mixed (synchronised capacity, customers' negotiating power, the Stevanato precedent); sector level neutral (the market prices pharma-packaging stocks on contracts and plant news, and has just repriced the group lower). External change, then, gives SCHOTT Pharma a growing market but no pricing help. Every point of margin from here must come from filling its own lines and cutting its own costs.

Internal change forces/risks

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal picture is a new, financially disciplined team running an unchanged strategy, with lowered ambitions, on a capital base built for more volume than it now carries. The strongest signal is the shift of pay toward operating free cash flow; the weakest is the commercial leadership loss at a moment when every large syringe contract is contested. The July 2026 guidance raise and the customer settlement repaired credibility in headline terms but not in underlying margin terms, so the new team has not yet shown it can move the margin. The changes create the conditions for a margin and cash recovery in FY2027 to FY2029 rather than causing one, and they leave the outcome dependent on utilisation, which management controls only partly. For the model: no strategic step-change, a cost programme worth about 100 basis points net, capital spending that stays high in FY2027 before tapering, and no capital-return or M&A lever.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement40C-1457C-8636C-5950C-8146D-0368
Group judgement

Risks

D4029th of 54

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is moderate on survival and high on earnings and multiple volatility. The business will not break: lock-in, low leverage and the parent's backing see to that. But the earnings path rests on filling capacity in a market where customers have shown they can cut volume, competitors have capacity to fill, and prices on new volume are starting to slip. The specific risks cluster. A GLP-1 customer cut, lower utilisation, higher unit costs and price concessions reinforce each other; currency adds a ±5% band. Low liquidity turns sector news into 10% to 20% price moves. The trough-multiple arithmetic gives roughly 25% fair downside against about 18% upside to consensus targets. For the model, run three cases around consensus FY2028 EPS of EUR 1.22: bear about EUR 1.02 (price -2% a year from 2027 plus a GLP-1 volume cut); base about EUR 1.15 to 1.20; bull about EUR 1.35 (utilisation and mix land a year early).

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

D5731st of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are improving where the finance director acts directly: financing cost, receivables, free cash flow, cost programme, cash-linked pay. They are not yet improving where the operation acts: margins, plant absorption, reject rates. Governance standards are high on reporting quality and low on minority influence, and unchanged.

  • Improving: nine-month free cash flow rose 49% to EUR 58.9m (lower tax payments, collections, financing). The intra-group debt optimisation in the first quarter of FY2026 improved the financial result (EUR 3.4m in the first quarter; financial result 35.6% better). An efficiency programme targets a "double-digit million euro" sum through digitalising administration and manufacturing. Incentives now include operating free cash flow. The capital projects have historically run on schedule: an ex-head of process engineering says programmes above US$100m ran with no delays.
  • Not improving yet: the nine-month gross margin fell 340 basis points. The DDS EBITDA margin fell from over 36% to 29.4% in the third quarter, with "production optimisation costs" newly incurred. The nine-month EBIT margin fell to 18.2% from 21.1%. Retention missed targets: senior exit rate 4.5% against a target below 3%; graduate retention 65% against 75%.
  • Governance: clean, unqualified audits; no EBITDA adjustments; research and development expensed; no restatements; arm's-length related-party dealings. Against that, there are no independent pay or nomination committees (because of the KGaA form), director shareholdings are negligible and there has been no insider buying. These are unchanged.
  • For the model: credit the efficiency programme with EUR 10m to 20m of gross savings phased over FY2027 and FY2028 (100 to 200 basis points). Assume about half is reinvested or absorbed by wage inflation and ramp costs, consistent with how little of FY2026's efficiency reached the margin.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

D3634th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negatives are real and specific. Underlying margins have fallen for two years while capital spending stayed near its peak. The commercial leader left for a rival. The culture has tilted toward quarterly pressure, with retention missing targets. Two parent contracts reach their fixed terms in December 2027. Customer concentration in obesity drugs sits with one buyer (Eli Lilly). Any of these could turn the 2027 to 2028 margin recovery into a further delay.

  • Margin erosion that a turn in volume has not reversed: third-quarter FY2026 EBITDA margin 26.8% against 32.3%; third-quarter EBIT down 15.6% to EUR 53.3m; quarterly EPS EUR 0.26 against 0.30. Start-up costs at Hungarian, Serbian and Swiss sites stay in the "high single-digit millions" a year.
  • Commercial continuity: Mario Haas left for BD; the new chief commercial officer also runs DCS. Expert comment calls the departure a "significant loss of commercial leadership and industry relationships".
  • Culture: former staff describe the post-listing culture as "highly finance-driven and short-term", with round-the-clock shifts during ramp-ups. Employees reportedly reject outside ideas ("this is not the way"). The senior exit rate is 4.5% against a target below 3%, and graduate retention 65% against 75%. Engineering staff are the scarce input for the qualification-heavy ramp-ups the case depends on.
  • Concentration: GLP-1 work is a low-teens share of sales, two-thirds of it with Eli Lilly (about 8% of group sales, derived). Lilly has made Stevanato its primary syringe supplier for two products. A Lilly format or supplier decision is the largest single customer risk.
  • Contract dates: the tubing framework agreement (fixed until December 2027, three-year automatic renewal) and the parent revolving facilities (EUR 412m, ending 31-Dec-2027) both fall inside the forecast horizon. A re-pricing by the parent, plausible if its own energy costs rise, would hit a cost line worth about 15% of revenue.
  • Capital spending: the finance director says investment peaks in FY2027, while consensus has FY2027 capital spending below FY2026 (EUR 149m against EUR 156m). Consensus FY2027 free cash flow (EUR 95m) is therefore at risk.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

D5032nd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: partly congruent. Long-term owner logic, quality culture, engineering excellence and capacity strategy fit together well: that is the inherited SCHOTT system. Since the 2023 listing a quarterly, finance-driven layer has been placed on top. It sits awkwardly with the long-cycle capacity strategy and with the engineering staff the ramp-ups depend on. The new pay mix (free cash flow, margin, growth) aligns the top; the evidence from the shop floor is mixed.

  • Congruent: quality first ("zero-defect", the integrity helpline, clean audits); a long horizon (foundation ownership; economic-value-added long-term incentives); capital project execution "perfectly scheduled" (expert); a Culture Index of 83 and a leadership score of 85.
  • Incongruent: "highly finance-driven and short-term quarterly mindset" since listing; round-the-clock shifts during ramp-ups; missed retention targets; "this is not the way" rigidity toward outside ideas; former product managers saying the team "carried on behaving like they were in private ownership", failing to manage market expectations.
  • Contrast: Stevanato is family-driven and long-term (with governance weaknesses); Lifco- or Ryanair-style system congruence is absent.
C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

C4620th of 54

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: six fundamental risks matter. Plausibility (18 months) and the EPS impact on FY2027 and FY2028, before mitigation, are my estimates. They cluster: a customer cut lowers utilisation, which raises unit costs while competitors hunt volume. The two largest combined (a Lilly-related cut plus price concessions) could take 15% to 20% off FY2028 EPS.

  1. A GLP-1 customer volume, format or supplier change, chiefly at Eli Lilly (two-thirds of GLP-1 exposure, about 8% of group sales). A 30% to 50% cut is about 2.5% to 4% of revenue and about -6% to -10% EPS before compensation. Plausibility 15% to 20%: the Stevanato precedent of November 2026; Lilly already "pivoting to Stevanato" for syringes; the push into oral drugs and vials. Mitigants: SCHOTT Pharma supplies Lilly vials and cartridges as well as syringes; take-or-pay compensation.
  2. Price concessions at renewal under overcapacity: 5% to 7.5% on multi-year renewals across the roughly one-third of contracts renewing each year. That is about 1.5 to 2.5 points of revenue a year, at close to 100% drop-through, about -8% to -12% EPS cumulatively over two years. Plausibility 25% to 30% for 2027 to 2028.
  3. A quality or sterility failure at a ready-to-use or syringe line (a recall; a lost qualification). EPS -5% to -15% plus a multiple hit. Plausibility about 5%. The quality record is strong, but polymer shows 1% cosmetic rejects and new lines carry ramp risk.
  4. A further vaccine collapse (vaccines 15% to 20% of sales; mRNA already down to 5%). A further 10% vaccine decline is about -1.5% to -2% of revenue and -4% EPS. Plausibility 20% to 25%.
  5. US tariffs on EU-made glass or tubing (Americas 29% of sales; tubing from European parent furnaces). EPS -3% to -6% if not passed through. Plausibility 10% to 15%.
  6. Parent re-pricing of tubing or financing at the December 2027 renewals: a 10% tubing rise is about 1.5% of sales, about -7% EPS. Plausibility about 10%. Mitigant: tubing overcapacity. - Also relevant: a 10% US dollar fall (about -5% EPS; plausibility 20%; already partly in the forward-rate debate) and a Swiss franc surge (10% means about -4% EPS).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

B+685th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the answer differs by horizon, and it differs from the second set's. Over the next 18 months, what makes the forecast unlike the last three years is diffuse and mostly mechanical. EBITDA does not have to grow faster than it has. What changes is that the three lines that consumed EBITDA growth (depreciation, tax and the financial result) stop consuming it. About three-quarters of the swing from flat EPS to +8% in FY2027 comes from those lines. From 18 months to four years the drivers concentrate into one: the incremental EBITDA margin, which must rise above anything delivered since the pandemic. For free cash flow there is a single driver throughout, capital spending, and it is a decision, not an outcome. So the drivers of the change are diffuse where it matters most for the next 18 months. The drivers of the risk are concentrated throughout: one margin line, moved by utilisation, price and one or two customers.

The bridge, year by year (company basis; EUR m; my derivation from the company's margins, FactSet's depreciation and the model).

FY2024 FY2025 FY2026E FY2027E FY2028E to FY2030E, a year
Change in EBITDA +18.5 +22.8 +6.3 +20.1 +26 to +28
Change in depreciation +18.4 +14.4 +9.1 +7.5 +5 to +7
Change in EBIT about 0 +8.4 -2.8 +12.6 +19 to +22
Financial result about -8.5 about -13 -8.5 -7.5 improving by 2 to 2.5 a year
Tax rate (on pre-tax profit including joint ventures) about 18% 21.8% 23.0% 22.5% 22.5%
EPS (EUR) 0.99 0.97 0.97 1.05 1.15, 1.28, 1.40

Reading the bridge for the next 18 months.

  • From FY2023 to FY2026, EBITDA rose by EUR 47.6m (worth +EUR 0.24 of EPS after tax). Depreciation rose by EUR 41.9m (-EUR 0.21). The tax rate rose by nearly five points (-EUR 0.06). The financial result cost a further EUR 0.01. The sum is EPS down from EUR 1.01 to EUR 0.97. This was not a company whose operations failed to grow; it was one whose growth was absorbed below the operating line.
  • In FY2027 the forecast EBITDA increase (+EUR 20m) is the size the company delivered in FY2024 and FY2025. The difference from history lies elsewhere. Depreciation adds EUR 7.5m instead of EUR 14m to 18m. The tax rate eases by half a point instead of rising. The financial result improves by EUR 1m instead of worsening.
  • Measured as the swing in the yearly change in EPS (from about -EUR 0.014 a year over FY2023 to FY2026, to about +EUR 0.077 in FY2027): slower depreciation growth gives about 36% of the swing, the stable tax rate about 30%, the financial result about 10%, and EBITDA growing a little faster than its three-year average only about 24%.
  • Each of the three lines below EBITDA is mechanical or already in hand. Depreciation is running at EUR 22m to 23m a quarter (EUR 22.2m in the third quarter), and EUR 96m for FY2027 allows that rate to step up by about EUR 1m each quarter. Capital spending has been flat at about EUR 145m for three years, so the asset base is no longer accelerating. The tax step (Pillar Two and country mix) has happened. The financing saving is in the nine-month numbers. The one identifiable risk on these lines is the Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant entering depreciation inside FY2027: each EUR 2m is 1% of EPS.

Reading the bridge for 18 months to four years. From FY2028 the mechanical help is smaller (depreciation still rises EUR 5m to 7m a year; the financial result gives about EUR 0.01 a year) and EBITDA must grow by EUR 26m to 28m a year, more than in any year since FY2022. That needs the incremental margin to rise from about 30% to 36% or 37%. Here the second set's reading holds: the forecasts rest on two or three linked drivers (absorption of built capacity, like-for-like price, the DDS margin), which are one driver seen from three sides. Each 5 points of incremental margin on the extra revenue to FY2030 is about EUR 0.09 of FY2030 EPS. Each 1% of price is EUR 0.055.

Free cash flow has one driver at every horizon. Free cash flow in FY2027 (about EUR 54m) is no different from FY2026 (EUR 58m) or from the history. The difference from history arrives in FY2028 and FY2029 and is one thing: capital spending falling from EUR 165m to EUR 135m while EBITDA rises. One decision, on North Carolina (US$371m; no final investment decision), would remove EUR 40m to 60m a year of it.

Concentration of risk, as distinct from concentration of drivers. The risks to the EBITDA line are concentrated. One customer is 10.3% of sales. GLP-1 work is a low-teens share of sales, two-thirds of it with Eli Lilly. The top five customers are about 30% of sales. One segment (DDS, 43% of sales and nearly half of EBITDA) carries the polymer problem and the syringe price risk. A forecast whose improvement comes from several mechanical lines but whose downside comes from one operating line is predictable in its central case and exposed in its tail, which is what D-04 finds.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

D5035th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there has been a near-complete change at the top within nine months: a new finance director (1-Aug-2025), a new chief executive (1-May-2026), a new chief commercial officer role, and the loss of the most senior commercial executive to a direct competitor. The change signals continuity of strategy with more financial discipline. It is not a reset of ambition, and it is not yet proven.

  • Chief executive Christian Mias (18 years inside SCHOTT Group; most recently ran Electronic Packaging, the group's fastest-growing unit in 2025) is an insider, appointed in November 2025 with a six-month handover. His early actions: closing the customer dispute in July 2026 (the negotiations were already "constructive" under him at the second-quarter call, two weeks into the job), confirming the 6% to 8% and towards-30% targets, and framing execution around three pillars (innovation, capacity, partnerships). Experts describe him as direct, hands-on and someone who "loves to challenge". His profit-and-loss record cannot be verified, because no SCHOTT division publishes accounts.
  • Finance director Reinhard Mayer (ex-Getinge and Nilfisk, both listed): restructured group financing (lower cash-pool interest), more than doubled first-half free cash flow through receivables collection, and launched a "double-digit million euro" efficiency programme. The 2026 short-term incentive now weights operating free cash flow at 30% (replacing return on capital employed), alongside revenue growth 30%, EBITDA margin 30% and sustainability 10%. That is a deliberate signal that cash conversion is now a paid-for target, which matters in the year capital spending peaks.
  • The negative change: Mario Haas, the chief commercial executive, left for BD as its chief commercial officer after being passed over. That loss of customer relationships comes exactly when multi-year GLP-1 and syringe contracts must be renewed and defended. Fabian Stöcker (14 years at SCHOTT) now combines the new chief commercial officer role with running DCS. That is one person on two big jobs.
  • Key actors: the parent (SCHOTT AG, owned by the Carl Zeiss Foundation, 77% of votes) is unchanged. There is no activist and no insider share dealing in twelve months.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

F4851st of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy itself has hardly changed. It remains to convert bulk to high-value and ready-to-use formats, build local-for-local capacity, and partner on devices rather than make them. What changed are the ambitions and the commercial emphasis: lower medium-term targets, a commercial officer, and tighter cash focus. The strategy is clearly articulated and well resourced in capital. It is not well differentiated from Stevanato's, and it carries no bold move.

  • Articulation: high-value products to above 60% of sales; the 6% to 8% organic growth target for FY2027 to FY2029; the EBITDA margin "increasing towards 30%" by FY2029; capital spending peaking in FY2027 and then tapering.
  • Resourcing: over EUR 800m invested since 2020, more than 80% on capacity, self-funded through operating cash flow plus the parent's cash pool. FY2026 capital spending is guided at EUR 140m to 160m.
  • New choices under the new team are modest: the chief commercial officer role for "customer centricity"; partnerships (Nemera pen-injector compatibility verified July 2026; Ypsomed collaboration); product launches (cartriQ BioPure cartridge, late 2026; ISO-compliant ready-to-use polymer cartridge; 5.5ml staked-needle syringe); and continuing joint ventures (India renamed SCHOTT Pharma India in October 2026, with TPG holding 35%).
  • What is missing: a deliberate answer to the two forces now pressing on the case. One is a plan for the under-used polymer lines beyond "non-mRNA applications are developing positively". The other is a pricing or capacity-discipline stance for 2027 to 2030 syringe overcapacity. Stevanato competes with devices and engineering; SCHOTT Pharma has chosen not to (a prudent choice given device execution risk, as West's SmartDose exit shows). That narrows its tools to materials science, tubing integration and location.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

F2851st of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: net over 12 to 18 months, guidance has been lowered, not raised. In December 2025 the medium-term targets were cut hard, from at least 10% growth to 6% to 8%, and from low-30s margins to towards 30%. The July 2026 raise of FY2026 guidance was mostly mechanical. The next event (10-Dec-2026) more likely brings FY2027 guidance at or slightly below the bottom of the 6% to 8% range than above it.

  • The December 2025 reset: FY2026 guidance of 2% to 5% organic growth and an EBITDA margin of about 27% was below estimates. The medium-term targets were cut as above. Consensus earnings fell about 13%, and Jefferies cut FY2026 EPS 28%.
  • The July 2026 raise, to 5% to 6% and 27% to 28%, decomposed: the EUR 15m compensation adds about 1.5 points of revenue growth and about 1.4 points of margin. So about three-quarters of the 2-point rise in mid-point revenue growth (3.5% to 5.5%) is the payment. Without the payment, the margin guide is effectively about 26% to 27% (mid-point about 26.4%), below the original "about 27%". Adding back the first-half write-down (about 1.2 points of margin, which arose after the December guidance) gives about 27.1% to 28.1%. So underlying margin guidance was barely raised, by about half a point at most. The genuine news in July was the third-quarter volume beat.
  • What it implies for FY2026 delivery: with nine-month organic growth at 4.4%, the guide implies fourth-quarter underlying growth of only about 1.5% to 5% after the payment, well below the third quarter's 8.3%. FY2026 should land at the top of the range (about 6% reported organic and about 27.5% to 28% margin).
  • FY2027: Deutsche Bank expects a guide below 6% to 8% because of the high base (it models 5.5%). The chief executive's message ("propel positive business momentum into the upcoming fiscal year") and the finance director's defence of 6% to 8% suggest the company will try to guide around 6% on the reported base. That is about 7.5% underlying, and it would be read as a soft guide.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

D5025th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: barely, and it does not need to much. The perimeter is already a narrow pure-play. The simplification under way is footprint consolidation (ampoules moved to one hub at Jagodina, Serbia, operational since April 2025) and moving standard glass to best-cost sites. There is no exit from the low-return polymer niche, which is the one unit where an exit or a sharp redeployment would raise returns.

  • Bulk containment is being "phased out" in emphasis rather than sold. It still funds the business and anchors vial leadership.
  • Polymer lines (heavy, automated, under-used) are being redeployed to non-mRNA uses (aesthetics, mental health, antibody-drug conjugates, large-volume syringes at Müllheim), not closed.
  • Peer contrast: Gerresheimer sold Centor and its plastics division for EUR 1.5bn under duress; Stevanato sold Balda; West sold SmartDose to AbbVie. SCHOTT Pharma has no forced-sale need, but also no portfolio lever to pull.
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C+6016th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: steadily and sensibly. It follows customers into the United States and India with approved local capacity, and it extends the product range at the high-value end. The one question mark is execution timing on the largest US project (North Carolina), on which the files carry no 2026 evidence.

  • United States: Lebanon, Pennsylvania (US$60m, BARDA-supported, inaugurated June 2026; the only US site making adaptiQ ready-to-use vials, one of two global sites for EVERIC vials). North Carolina: US$371m, designed to triple US prefilled-syringe supply by 2030, with commercial start pencilled for FY2027 (Jefferies, March 2024). Locating near customers' US filling plants answers what an Eli Lilly adviser says decides syringe awards: proximity and supply availability. Customers have perceived SCHOTT Pharma's lack of US manufacturing history as meaning longer delivery times and higher risk.
  • Europe: Lukácsháza, Hungary (glass syringes ramping after customer qualification; a ready-to-use cartridge plant over EUR 100m, ground broken June 2025); St. Gallen ready-to-use cartridges (demand exceeds capacity); Jagodina ampoules.
  • India: SCHOTT Pharma India (renamed October 2026), with TPG holding 35% (bought from the Serum Institute), preparing prefillable glass syringe capacity. It reports through the equity line.
  • Products: cartriQ BioPure (late 2026), the first ISO-compliant ready-to-use polymer cartridge, 1.5ml sterile cartridge (67% filling efficiency gain), 5.5ml staked-needle syringe for home-use biologics, Nemera pen compatibility.
  • Sensibleness: high. Each move is customer-led and inside the circle of competence. The risk is that adding US syringe capacity in 2027 coincides with Stevanato's Fishers plant at 50% occupancy and freed capacity after its customer cut.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F3653rd of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is no evidence of real price rises in FY2025 or FY2026. Pricing is at best nominal pass-through via index clauses, and in bulk glass and ready-to-use vials it is falling. The pricing power that exists is contractual and latent: the volume-based price mechanisms and the take-or-pay compensation. Management does not talk about price increases.

  • Evidence against real rises: gross margin fell from 36.0% (FY2022) to 33.7% (FY2024 and FY2025) and 31.3% (nine months FY2026), while revenue grew. Glass makers have conceded 5% to 10% in recent negotiations (former SGD Pharma director). Customers demand 5% to 7.5% on multi-year renewals. Ready-to-use 6ml vial prices halved in 2026. The 2021 to 2022 pandemic price rises of 25% to 40% were clawed back by 2025.
  • Evidence of power that exists but is used defensively: contracts index glass every six months and labour annually. Volume-based pricing lets lower volumes command higher unit prices (Deutsche Bank). The July 2026 settlement extracted about EUR 15m of compensation. Experts cite a 10% to 30% premium over peers on quality and "low-to-mid single-digit" annual rises for established suppliers. These are claims about the industry in general, not observed company pricing.
  • Peer benchmark: GVS discloses price contributions of +1.4% to +1.5% a year (FY2024 to Q1 2026); West targets 2% to 3% a year. SCHOTT Pharma discloses no price/volume split. That non-disclosure is itself a signal that price is not a lever it is pulling.
  • For the model: a blended like-for-like price of 0% to +1% a year (index pass-through less renewal concessions), with value growth coming from mix (ready-to-use at 5 to 10 times the bulk price), not from price on the same product.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

F4844th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: not at all. There has been no material acquisition or disposal since the 2023 listing, management prefers organic expansion and partnerships, and the finance director now owns the M&A function. This is disciplined but leaves a lever unused. For the model, assume zero M&A spend.

  • The stated stance: "open to targeted acquisitions", with priority on organic platform expansion and commercial partnerships, and an explicit choice not to enter devices through acquisition.
  • Peer contrast: Gerresheimer's Bormioli acquisition is judged overpriced and a poor fit, and it fed the leverage crisis. That supports SCHOTT Pharma's restraint.
  • Constraint: the parent's cash pool and the low free float make share-funded or large debt-funded deals unlikely in any case.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

D5030th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the balance sheet is used for organic capital spending and nothing else. Shareholder returns are modest and steady (a 10% to 20% payout, dividend raised 12.5% to EUR 0.18 for FY2025). No buyback is mooted, and the structure makes one unlikely: a buyback would shrink an already-thin free float.

  • Leverage: about 0.4x to 0.6x net debt to EBITDA on the company basis, financed by the parent's cash pool and revolving facilities (EUR 412m, of which EUR 224m was drawn in March 2026), with "liquid corporate assets" swept into the parent's treasury.
  • Consensus dividends: EUR 0.18 (FY2026), 0.20 (FY2027), 0.22 (FY2028 and FY2029), a payout of about 18%.
  • The real balance-sheet question is a refinancing date: the parent facilities terminate on 31-Dec-2027, the same month the tubing framework agreement reaches its fixed term. Both are likely to roll, but they are the two contractual dates inside the forecast horizon.
  • Peer contrast: GVS (a 63% family-controlled peer) launched a EUR 100m tender buyback for 12.29% of its capital, with the controlling shareholder not tendering, a model that adds to EPS without cutting the float held by others. Nothing similar is in view at SCHOTT Pharma.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–55

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the main plausible positive is a cash-flow inflection after FY2027, as capital spending peaks and new lines fill, which management is now paid to deliver. A secondary one is share gains from a weakened Gerresheimer. A tertiary one, plausible but unsignalled, is the parent addressing the free float, which would widen the investor base but carry a placement overhang.

  • Free cash flow: capital spending of about 15% of sales (FY2025 and FY2026) is guided to peak in FY2027 and then taper; Deutsche Bank sees capital intensity in the "low 10s %" within three years. A drop to 11% to 12% of sales on EUR 1.2bn of revenue, with operating cash flow of about EUR 270m to 300m, gives FY2029 free cash flow of EUR 130m to 160m against EUR 37m (company basis) in FY2025.
  • Joint-venture contribution: rising syringe output in India raises equity-accounted EBITDA with no capital spending in the consolidated accounts.
  • Gerresheimer's customers: the files show customers hesitant to award new work to Gerresheimer; SCHOTT Pharma's vial and molded-glass customers could come from there.
  • Float: Deutsche Bank says the parent "must eventually address" the restricted float. A placement by the parent would add liquidity and eventually lift the multiple, but in the short term it is an overhang risk.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4841st of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: none have. SCHOTT AG (77% of votes, ultimately owned by the Carl Zeiss Foundation) controls management through the general partner and has signalled no change in strategy, ownership or float. No activist exists, and none can act under the KGaA form. The only external pressure is analysts' comments about liquidity.

  • The structure: the general partner, SCHOTT Pharma Management AG, has exclusive authority to run the business and appoint executives. The company would convert automatically to a standard stock corporation if the parent fell below 30%. Change-of-control clauses sit in key contracts.
  • The parent's influence on economics is ongoing and one-directional: a 1.5% brand royalty (about EUR 15m a year), tubing purchases (related-party purchases EUR 112m in FY2025; EUR 81m in the first half of FY2026), the cash pool and the revolving facilities. All are at arm's length per the auditors, but they are permanent leakages and dependencies.
  • The foundation's owner logic favours long-term capacity investment over short-term earnings, which explains the capital-spending posture and the low payout.
  • Read for the model: no catalyst from controlling actors within 18 months; the parent's stance is a constant, not a variable.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F5249th of 54

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: moderate in strength, narrow in breadth, well resourced in capital, thin in people. The positive changes are financial housekeeping (financing, collections, cost programme, cash-linked pay) and commercial focus (chief commercial officer, partnerships, launches). They are real but small against the drag of under-absorbed capacity. The large positive, filling the new lines, is less a corporate change than the payoff of decisions taken in 2021 to 2024. The new team will be judged on it from FY2027.

  • Strength: the efficiency programme (about EUR 10m to 20m) and the financing savings (a few EUR m) are worth about 1 to 2 margin points gross, against about 4 points of EBITDA margin lost on a quarterly comparison (32.3% to 26.8% in the third quarter).
  • Breadth: no change in strategy, perimeter, M&A or capital returns; changes are concentrated in finance and commercial organisation.
  • Resourcing: about EUR 150m a year of capital spending and parent funding behind it, with the remuneration system now including free cash flow. The people side is thinner: one commercial leader lost and retention targets missed.
  • What would make it strong: evidence in the first half of FY2027 that polymer utilisation and Hungarian syringe and cartridge absorption are lifting DDS margins back above 31% to 32% without one-off help, together with FY2027 capital spending at or below EUR 150m to 160m.
C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

F3644th of 54

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the staircase is thin on novelty and two-sided. Most dated events are execution milestones (results, plant starts, capital-spending taper) rather than new stories. The two genuinely new positives, a free-cash-flow inflection and a re-rating as "proven operating leverage", sit 12 to 24 months out and depend on the same utilisation evidence. The most dated near-term event, the FY2027 guide, is more likely to read soft than strong.

Next 6 months (to May 2027):

  • 10-Dec-2026, FY2026 results and FY2027 guidance. Likely FY2026 at the top of guidance (about 6% growth, about 27.5% to 28% margin), but an FY2027 guide of about 5% to 7% growth and about 28% margin reads as "below 6% to 8%". Novelty low; well-founded; net neutral to slightly negative.
  • Late 2026, cartriQ BioPure cartridge launch. Novelty modest; small revenue in the window.
  • 18-Dec-2026, BIOSECURE list. An indirect positive (limits Chinese rivals in the United States). Novelty low.
  • February 2027, first-quarter FY2027 results and the AGM (dividend EUR 0.18 to 0.20). The first test of DDS margin recovery against an easy comparison (first-quarter FY2026 margin about 25%). Potentially positive; well-founded only if polymer improves.
  • January 2027, Novo Nordisk's lower US prices. Volume up, price per dose down; neutral.

Months 6 to 18 (mid-2027 to mid-2028):

  • Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant commercial start (over EUR 100m; ground broken June 2025). Novel for cartridges share; well-founded on the timeline but carrying validation risk.
  • North Carolina syringe plant commercial start (US$371m; planned for FY2027 in 2024; no 2026 confirmation in the files). Highly novel if it happens (US syringes near Lilly's US plants); poorly founded on current evidence. A key item to verify.
  • Polymer recovery evidence (management: from FY2027). Novel if real; weakly founded (two years of delay).
  • December 2027: FY2027 results with the capital-spending peak passed and FY2028 guidance with a taper. Free-cash-flow inflection story; well-founded on management statements. The same month brings the parent tubing agreement and revolving-facility renewals (expected to roll; low novelty, small risk).
  • The negatives in the same window: orforglipron approval and launch, the end of the containment shortage premium about end-2027 (Sector Digest), Stevanato's backfilled capacity.

Months 18 to 36 (mid-2028 to late 2029):

  • FY2028 and FY2029: margin near 29% to 30% and high-value products above 60%. The medium-term target delivered (or not). Novel as a narrative ("proven operating leverage"); moderately founded.
  • Free cash flow of EUR 130m to 160m a year: room to raise the payout. Novel; well-founded if capital spending tapers.
  • Possible free-float action by the parent: novel and unsignalled; it would broaden ownership after a placement overhang.
  • Against these: 2028 to 2030 syringe overcapacity and oral share data (Bernstein's 50% by 2028 call tested).
