Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

C+6814th of 54

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

C+6814th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The industry structure is that of an attractive oligopoly whose economics are about to be tested. Concentration, regulatory lock-in, integrated tubing and a cooperative history protect the installed base, which is close to an annuity for SCHOTT Pharma (97% repeat revenue). The test comes from four directions at once: synchronised capacity additions in syringes and ready-to-use formats; two obesity-drug originators with power to move large volumes between formats and suppliers; customers that have learned to renegotiate take-or-pay terms; and a weakened competitor with capacity to fill (Stevanato after November 2026). SCHOTT Pharma is well placed on the share of value (first in vials, second in cartridges, cleanest governance, owner of the tubing) and less well placed on the share of new syringe volume (third, half Stevanato's capacity). For the model: high volume visibility, stable shares on existing drugs, falling share of new GLP-1 syringe volume, flat like-for-like prices in the base case and a 25% to 30% chance of falling prices in 2028 to 2029.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3255C-3378C-3442C-3772C-3844C-3968
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5533rd of 54full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

A785th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market is direct and helpful. The company sells straight to drug makers and their contract fill-finish partners, with no wholesalers, and the containment specification is locked into the drug's file. The intermediary that matters, the contract filler, is growing capacity, which pulls containers through. The fragility is that drug makers are internalising filling (Novo's purchase of three Catalent sites) and concentrating volume with fewer, larger fillers, which shifts negotiating power toward large buyers.

  • Direct selling gives technical and regulatory support and keeps margin; advance payments are offset against deliveries where customers meet minimums.
  • Contract fillers: Lonza (Stein from 2028), Rovi (625m to 810m syringes; Phoenix 65m to 70m by 2027), Thermo Fisher (+40% to 60%) all add filling capacity, which needs containers. Rovi and Lonza are customers, not competitors.
  • Risk: large fillers and originators consolidate purchasing and can push ready-to-use requirements, and prices, onto suppliers. Some large producers choose isolators and in-house washing instead of ready-to-use formats to meet Annex 1, which slows the format shift.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

D4235th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: on balance competitors' behaviour now hurts slightly. Stevanato and BD are winning the largest new syringe volumes and launching rival products in SCHOTT Pharma's niches, while Gerresheimer's retreat helps at the margin. SCHOTT Pharma's position is secure on installed volume and contestable on every new award.

  • Hurting: Stevanato (primary GLP-1 syringe supplier at Lilly and Novo; Nexa Flex polymer syringe in COC and copolymer; Latina vial line converted to cartridges for an anchor customer; now with capacity to refill after its cut). West (Synchrony syringe brand; GLP-1 high-value elastomers +20% to +30% organic). BD (over 80 GLP-1 biosimilar device deals; scale pricing).
  • Helping: Gerresheimer (BaFin probe, trust deficit, Chicago Heights closure, a perimeter reshaped around syringes and tubular glass while its management is distracted).
  • Fragility: buyers see top-tier syringes as "functionally equivalent" and award on proximity and availability. SCHOTT Pharma's quality premium therefore matters most in vials, cartridges and specialty products, less in syringes.
C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?B+726th of 54full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

D4438th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption acts mostly on customers' demand and on competitors' capacity, less on regulators and suppliers. Demand-side disruption (formats, orals, prices per dose) is changing which containers are bought. Competitive disruption (synchronised capacity) is changing what they cost. The company's position is robust in vials and cartridges, exposed in syringes and polymer, and overall moderately fragile on margin, not on survival or share.

  • Demand: originators (format choices: vials, pens, pills; supplier choice), patients (attrition, cash pay) and generics (lower prices per dose). Effect: volume reshuffled toward vials and cartridges; per-unit price pressure over time.
  • Competition: Stevanato, BD and SCHOTT Pharma itself adding capacity; Gerresheimer retreating. Effect: price pressure on new awards from 2027.
  • Regulators and political actors: Annex 1 and the FDA raise barriers; US tariffs relocate supply. Effect: positive for incumbents with approved US and EU capacity.
  • Suppliers: stable (tubing surplus; parent integration). COC and elastomers are concentrated but secure.
  • Contract fillers: consolidation and internalisation (Novo–Catalent) raise buyer power; new filling capacity raises container demand.

3b

Industry structure

Judgement68C-2472C-2585C-2670C-2755C-2860C-2936C-3168C-3572C-3648
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)C+6814th of 54full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C+7216th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: SCHOTT Pharma is number one or two in every containment category except glass prefillable syringes, where it is third behind BD and Stevanato, with about half Stevanato's capacity. It is the largest single player in the EUR 3.9bn addressable injectable-packaging market (21% share), but in the fastest-growing high-value product, GLP-1 syringes, it is not the leader.

  • Number one: vials (about 50% per Deutsche Bank's October 2026 model; "over 20%" per an expert, the difference likely being the definition of the market), ampoules, and prefillable polymer syringes (60% to 70%; 70% per Deutsche Bank).
  • Number two: cartridges, 34% against Stevanato's 60%. Stevanato leads ready-to-use cartridges and vials.
  • Number three: glass prefillable syringes, behind BD (the dominant player) and Stevanato. Stevanato makes about 800m glass syringes a year against SCHOTT Pharma's 400m and takes a disproportionate share of Eli Lilly's syringe demand.
  • Overall: 21% of the EUR 3.9bn addressable market and 33% of the EUR 1.8bn containment market. In tubing, the parent leads with 60% to 70%.
  • Implication: SCHOTT Pharma's position is strongest where growth is moderate (vials, ampoules) or where the niche is shrinking (polymer), and weaker where growth is fastest (GLP-1 syringes). Its fastest high-share growth area is cartridges, where it is the challenger to Stevanato's lead.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

A854th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: very concentrated and steep. The top three in core containment (SCHOTT Pharma, Stevanato, Gerresheimer) hold over 60%. With BD in syringes and a fifth player (Nipro, SGD or Corning, depending on the product), the top five plausibly hold 75% to 85% of each Western category. That is many times what numbers six to ten hold; those are regional and Chinese bulk makers.

  • The files name a "leader tier" of four (BD, SCHOTT, Stevanato, Gerresheimer) and a second tier of SGD Pharma, Nipro and Chinese makers in standard vials and ampoules.
  • Upstream, tubing is even more concentrated: SCHOTT AG 60% to 70%, then Corning and Nippon Electric Glass/Nipro.
  • Elastomer components are near-monopoly: West has 95% of GLP-1 high-value components.
  • Concentration supports rational pricing, but it also means one leader's capacity decision moves the whole market's utilisation. The current synchronised build-out is exactly that risk.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

C+7014th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: shares on existing drugs are very stable, because containment is written into the drug's registration and changing it takes 6 to 9 months to up to five years of testing and refiling. Shares on new volume (new drugs, new formats, new capacity) move quickly, and over the last three years they have moved toward Stevanato in syringes and ready-to-use cartridges. New entrants have taken little share in Western high-value products and some share in bulk vials, ampoules and emerging markets.

  • Repeat customers give 97% of SCHOTT Pharma's revenue; relationships with the top 25 customers average over 10 years; the loyalty index is 90.
  • Shifts in new business: Lilly "pivoting to Stevanato as the primary" syringe supplier for two GLP-1 products, Stevanato main syringe supplier for two Novo products and first to convert customers (Novo) to ready-to-use cartridges. The Sector Digest notes that "the largest new syringe contracts are going to Stevanato".
  • Entrants: Chinese and Indian makers in standard vials (shorter lead times, discounts; they disrupted heparin earlier); West's Synchrony syringe; Stevanato's Nexa Flex polymer syringe.
  • Implication: SCHOTT Pharma's installed base is close to an annuity, but its share of growth is decided contract by contract. Its share of new GLP-1 syringe volume appears below its overall market share.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5530th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: share consolidates toward the most advantaged, not simply the largest, and the advantages differ by product. In ready-to-use cartridges and GLP-1 syringes the advantaged player has been Stevanato: it builds its own forming machines, bundles devices and engineering, and has US capacity first. In vials, specialty glass and integrated tubing supply it is SCHOTT Pharma. Gerresheimer is the share donor. SCHOTT Pharma is among the advantaged, but not in the segment growing fastest.

  • SCHOTT Pharma's advantages: owner-level access to tubing (60% to 70% world share; preferential allocation in shortages), the quality reputation ("SCHOTT leads in quality", former Gerresheimer director), and clean governance in a sector with two governance scandals (Gerresheimer, BaFin; Stevanato, material weaknesses, controlled by a family with 84% of votes).
  • Stevanato's advantages: in-house glass-forming machinery (no equipment premium), auto-injector and pen contract manufacturing, Fishers already running. Its weakness is buying tubing from SCHOTT AG and Nippon Electric Glass.
  • BD: scale and lower unit prices (Stevanato's container pricing is about 1.5 times BD's standard rates), over 80 GLP-1 biosimilar device deals.
  • Consolidation is visible in Gerresheimer's retreat: debt-driven divestments, plant closures, lost customer trust. Its volumes go to the two European leaders.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C-6026th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: historically margin-first and cooperative. The three European leaders founded a joint alliance to promote ready-to-use formats, and established suppliers took low-to-mid single-digit price rises for years without losing volume. But prices do fall when volume falls, in bulk glass at least. Post-pandemic overcapacity produced 5% to 10% concessions, clawing back the 25% to 40% pandemic rises.

  • Cooperative evidence: the Alliance for RTU (SCHOTT Pharma, Gerresheimer, Stevanato, September 2024) to standardise configurations and teach the total-cost case; segmented leadership (BD syringes, Stevanato cartridges, SCHOTT vials) that "reduces direct head-to-head pricing fights" (expert).
  • Price-follows-volume evidence: glass prices rose 25% to 40% in the pandemic, then customers clawed them back as energy and transport normalised; 5% to 10% cuts in recent glass negotiations; ready-to-use vial prices halved in 2026 as capacity grew.
  • Willingness to suffer losses: no leader is pricing below cost yet. Stevanato ran negative free cash flow for years to build capacity. That is a volume-first capital posture, though not volume-first pricing.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F3642nd of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the attitude is becoming less stable, and the conditions for a price attack in syringes are forming for 2027 to 2028. No competitor shows O'Leary-style volatility today. But two of the four leaders are under pressure: Stevanato with freed capacity and a 28% share-price fall, and Gerresheimer with a covenant reset to 4.75x and a need for cash. All three European leaders are adding capacity that only fills at high utilisation, and specialised lines run 20 years with high exit barriers. The likeliest form is not a shark attack but tender-by-tender discounting on new syringe and ready-to-use volume.

  • Eighteen months to three years ago: tight supply and pandemic pricing; a benign attitude was easy.
  • Now: buyers "more aggressive" (former SGD director); take-or-pay terms renegotiated (SCHOTT Pharma's own settlement); a Vice President at Aptar predicts that pricing power moves to the drug makers within three years; experts warn both leaders "could sacrifice pricing power to absorb peak capital expenditures".
  • Precedents in the files: the pandemic vaccine-capacity build left contract manufacturers with under-used assets and price pressure (former Thermo Fisher manager). "Competitive intensity is entry minus exit" (Wisdom Library): entry has been heavy and exit is close to impossible for 20-year sterile lines. The Wisdom Library's "money war and last man standing" fits a market with indivisible capacity and well-funded rivals.
  • What restrains an attack: each leader's own new lines need validated customers, not just low prices; qualification takes 6 to 24 months; buyers dual-source for supply security; and low prices do not win existing registered volume.
  • For the model: a downside scenario in which syringe and ready-to-use vial pricing falls 3% to 5% a year in 2028 to 2029 deserves about a 25% to 30% probability. The base case should assume flat like-for-like prices from 2027.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

C+6814th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the structure is supportive: a concentrated oligopoly, regulatory lock-in on every approved drug, an integrated tubing supply in the parent's hands, and a cooperative history. That support is fragile in one specific place, the price of new volume in syringes and ready-to-use formats, while 2025 to 2028 capacity additions outpace demand. It is robust on installed volume.

  • Robust: the installed base (97% repeat revenue; registration lock-in; packaging under 1% of drug cost and 3% to 5% for high-value biologics, so price is a "secondary consideration" for buyers of approved drugs). Exit barriers mean the market will not lose players: Gerresheimer is shrinking, not leaving.
  • Fragile: the share of new volume, and its price. Utilisation in syringes is about 90% against a 92% to 95% equilibrium, and new lines arrive (Stevanato Fishers; SCHOTT Pharma Hungary and North Carolina; BD). The sector's own judgement is that the shortage premium ends around end-2027.
  • Interaction with customers: two obesity-drug originators decide a large share of incremental demand, and both have shown they will change format and supplier. Concentration on the customer side (monopsony at the margin) is rising as the supply side loosens.
  • Implication for the model: volume visibility is high, like-for-like price visibility is low, and the margin bridge must rest on absorption (utilisation) and mix (ready-to-use), not on price.
C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?B+727th of 54full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D4835th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a growing but not yet active threat. The live substitutes are other injectable formats (vials, multi-dose cartridges, longer dosing intervals), which reshuffle volume among SCHOTT Pharma's own products rather than remove it. The external substitute, oral drugs, is the bigger long-term threat but is so far adding patients rather than replacing injections.

  • Within injectables: one monthly cartridge replaces four weekly syringes; Lilly and Novo push cash-pay vials; a weekly-to-monthly shift in dosing cuts units. SCHOTT Pharma is relatively hedged (first in vials, second in cartridges).
  • Orals: forecasts of 30% (Stevanato) to 50% (Bernstein, by 2028) of GLP-1 volume; SHL Medical sees prefilled-syringe demand falling up to 20% by 2028. Against that: low bioavailability, about five times (expert) to 73 times (Industry Digest) more active ingredient per patient, GI side effects, injections at 89.9% of US prescriptions, and 80% of Foundayo patients new to the class.
  • Polymer against glass: the substitute that failed. Polymer stays a niche because of cost (2.5 to 3 times glass), oxygen permeability and scratches.
  • Change: the threat rises from 2028, when Lilly's orforglipron capacity is built and semaglutide patents expire more widely. For FY2027 and FY2028, the effect on SCHOTT Pharma is small (GLP-1 is about 12% of sales and mostly vials, cartridges and syringes for Lilly's injectables).

4a

Business quality

C-5724th of 548 answers
Judgement57C-4162C-4258C-4364C-4466C-4532C-4648C-4760
Group judgement

Company: Competitive advantage

C-5724th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

SCHOTT Pharma's competitive advantage is real, durable and mostly defensive. Registration lock-in, integrated tubing supply, vial scale and a quality and governance reputation make its installed revenue among the safest in industrial healthcare. That explains why its revenue fell only modestly through the vaccine and destocking reversal of 2023 to 2026. The advantage is weaker in the two places that decide the next four years' margins: winning new GLP-1 syringe volume (at parity with Stevanato, which has twice its syringe capacity and the edge in equipment, devices and US presence), and pricing (no demonstrated real price rises; gross margin down 470 basis points from the FY2022 peak). The moat is widening slowly through regulation and format conversion and narrowing in polymer. For the model, the advantage justifies high revenue visibility and a low probability of share loss on existing volume. It does not justify assuming price-driven margin expansion; the margin must come from filling capacity and from mix.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C+6215th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: SCHOTT Pharma has four real moats. They are strong at keeping existing business and weaker at winning new business or setting price. Regulatory lock-in is widening. The rest are stable. The polymer niche moat is narrowing. Overall the moat protects revenue more than it protects margin.

  1. Regulatory and registration lock-in (customer-related; strong; widening). Containment is written into drug files; requalification takes 6 to 9 months for minor changes, 18 to 24 months for complex drug-device combinations, and up to five years of stability testing for a primary container switch. Annex 1 and the new FDA guidance raise the bar further. Every new drug approved in SCHOTT Pharma containment adds another 10- to 30-year annuity. The limit: the lock-in covers the supplier, not the volume.
  2. Integrated tubing supply (production; moderately strong; stable). The parent's 60% to 70% world share in pharma tubing, preferential allocation in shortages, first access to innovations (FIOLAX Pro), and a cost edge over non-integrated rivals. It matters most in shortages; in today's tubing surplus the edge is smaller.
  3. Scale and quality leadership in vials, ampoules and cartridges (scale; strong in vials; stable). About 13 billion units a year, 17 sites; the number-one vial position and a quality reputation that wins business as Gerresheimer stumbles. The US Lebanon site (the only domestic adaptiQ ready-to-use vial producer) adds a local-for-local edge.
  4. Materials science and polymer know-how (knowledge; strong but in a shrinking niche; narrowing). 60% to 70% of polymer syringes, essential for minus 100 degrees Celsius drugs; Stevanato's Nexa Flex is challenging it, and demand has shrunk with mRNA. - Not a moat: a pricing premium in syringes (buyers see SCHOTT Pharma and Stevanato as interchangeable); "relationships" as such (97% retention reflects lock-in, and it also masks weak new-customer acquisition). - Wisdom Library check ("barriers block entrants, advantages beat rivals"): SCHOTT Pharma's moats are mostly barriers, which keep out entrants and lock in existing volume. They are less advantages over Stevanato and BD, which are needed to win growth contracts.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C5819th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: SCHOTT Pharma is mid-sized in revenue, about EUR 1.0bn against Stevanato's EUR 1.19bn, Gerresheimer's EUR 2.3bn before its sales of businesses, and West's US$3.1bn. It is the largest pure containment maker by units. Scale confers the lowest unit cost and the broadest portfolio in vials, but no scale advantage in syringes, where Stevanato has twice its capacity and BD the lowest prices.

  • About 4,800 employees, 17 sites, about 13 billion units a year; it supplies all of the top 30 injectable drug makers and over 1,800 customers.
  • Scale against customers is small: its largest obesity customer is a top-five world drug company, and packaging is a tiny share of the customer's cost. Scale gives supply security, not bargaining power.
  • "Scale needs a bounded market" (Wisdom Library): vials are effectively bounded (slow growth, entrenched filing lines), so scale advantage holds there. Syringes are not bounded (rivals have grown capacity to match), so there is no lasting scale moat there.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

B6612th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior, and leading in quality, breadth and governance. Not leading in syringes, where it is at parity. The surplus utility is real for drug makers (quality, regulatory track record, tubing security, breadth from vial to cartridge to syringe, US and EU local supply). It is too small in syringes to command a premium against Stevanato.

  • For not inferior and leading: customer loyalty index of 90 (168 customers, 37 countries); "SCHOTT leads in quality" (former Gerresheimer director); a 5% reject-rate reduction delivered to a top account; first-of-kind products (ISO-compliant ready-to-use polymer cartridge; cartriQ BioPure; a diamond-shaped nest giving 67% filling efficiency); clean governance at a time when customers have "lost trust" in Gerresheimer.
  • Parity, not superiority, in syringes: "functionally equivalent" to Stevanato in Lilly's qualification teams; awards decided on proximity and availability; Stevanato's equipment, device and US capacity edge.
  • Inferior points: polymer has a 1% cosmetic reject rate that high-value fillers resist, and no North American manufacturing history in syringes (customers perceive longer lead times and higher risk).
C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D3235th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: it has structural pricing power in theory and does not demonstrably use it. The evidence of application is contractual (index clauses; volume-based pricing; the EUR 15m compensation), not real price rises. The gross margin trend and renewal concessions point the other way. This is "pricing power breaks before demand does" (Wisdom Library, 30-Oct-2026) playing out in bulk glass and ready-to-use vials.

  • The theory: packaging is under 1% of drug cost (3% to 5% for high-value biologics); switching costs are very high; a 10% to 30% premium over peers (Deutsche Bank and experts); "low-to-mid single-digit" annual rises across the industry; ready-to-use at 5 to 15 times the bulk price.
  • The application: gross margin down from 36.0% (FY2022) to 31.3% (nine months FY2026); 5% to 10% concessions in recent glass negotiations; renewals at -5% to -7.5% (SGD procurement); ready-to-use vial prices down about 50% in 2026; no price/volume disclosure from the company.
  • Where it was applied: the July 2026 customer agreement converted a contractual volume floor into about EUR 15m of compensation plus revised volumes. That shows contractual teeth, but also that the volume floor is negotiable.
  • For the model: like-for-like price is a 0% to +1% contribution, with mix doing the value work.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C+4816th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: only partly, and not as a stated value. The company shares process gains (reject-rate improvements, filling-line efficiency, total-cost-of-ownership arguments for ready-to-use formats, volume discounts on bundles) because they help sell high-value products. It does not pass scale savings through as price cuts. That is a margin-first stance typical of the oligopoly, not a "scale economies shared" flywheel.

  • Evidence of sharing: a 5% reject-rate reduction for a top account; 1.5ml cartridge nests improving filling efficiency 67%; bundled vial and syringe offers; Alliance for RTU teaching cost-of-ownership benefits; Stevanato reports ready-to-use cutting customers' cost of ownership up to 40% for vials.
  • No evidence of systematic price-down sharing; the Supreme Court of India confirmed that volume discounts are lawful, which shows they exist mainly as a tool of loyalty, not generosity.
C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

C+6015th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: gravitational forces work for SCHOTT Pharma on revenue and moat, and against it on margins in the short term. Each year, more drugs are registered in its containment (the annuity grows) and more volume converts to ready-to-use formats (value per unit rises), with no need for heroic effort. Those forces are slow, worth perhaps 2 to 3 points of growth a year, and for the next two years capacity costs work against them.

  • Working for it: each approved molecule locks a supplier for 10 to 30 years; Annex 1 and the FDA guidance tighten every year; biologics are 78% of approvals; vial and cartridge conversion to ready-to-use proceeds as old lines retire on 20-year cycles; tubing integration matters in every shortage.
  • Working against it, mechanically: depreciation rises with new plants regardless of use (EUR 79m FY2025 to EUR 112m FY2029, consensus); vaccine and polymer shrinkage; renewal concessions every three years on locked prices; the synchronised capacity in the industry.
  • Against peers: the gravitational gain is larger for SCHOTT Pharma than for Gerresheimer (less high-value exposure, leverage) and similar to Stevanato's (whose larger syringe exposure gives more format risk). It is not large enough by itself to lift returns back to the 22% to 25% of FY2020 to FY2021.

This is a competent, prudent, engineering-led team with a new and financially sharper top. It is not yet a team that sets outlier standards or allocates capital dynamically. C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

F4045th of 54

Judgement: moderate and recently reduced. Management cut its growth ambition from at least 10% to 6% to 8% and its margin ambition from the low 30s to "towards 30%". The ambition is to be the quality leader in containment and to ride the ready-to-use shift, not to dominate the injectable value chain. Stevanato's ambition (devices, engineering, the largest syringe capacity) is larger; West's (premium elastomer franchise, device assembly) is comparable.

  • Limits: no move into devices (a deliberate choice), no M&A, and a parent whose foundation logic favours steadiness over bold moves.
C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

F5047th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the strategy is right but not bold, and it is not dynamic for the bearish environment (overcapacity plus orals). Its three priorities are well chosen: high-value and ready-to-use conversion, local-for-local capacity, and partnerships rather than device ownership. They widen the regulatory and quality moat. What is missing is a plan for the case where capacity outruns demand: no stated capacity discipline, no plan for polymer beyond "applications are developing", no device or platform lever to lock in new drugs as Stevanato's integrated systems do.

  • Fits a range of environments: being hedged across formats (vials, cartridges, syringes) is valuable if GLP-1 formats shift; Annex 1 and FDA rules reward the quality leader; local plants are valuable under tariffs.
  • Does not fit: if syringe pricing falls from 2027, a capacity-heavy strategy with 15% of sales in capital spending is the wrong posture. The company would need to slow spending, which the finance director's free-cash-flow incentives may encourage.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F3843rd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: prudent governance, but not shareholders-first. Management is not enriched at shareholders' expense: pay is moderate, capped and tied to economic value added, free cash flow and margins, and the books are clean. But the structure puts the parent first and minority shareholders a distant second. The parent controls appointments through the general partner, takes a 1.5% royalty, runs the treasury, supplies the main input and sets a low payout. Minorities have no pay or nomination committee and see no insider buying.

  • Prudence (scaffolding): four of six supervisory board members independent; audit committee chaired by an independent (Eva Kienle); conservative accounting (no EBITDA adjustments, research and development expensed); no material weaknesses or restatements since listing; arm's-length related-party dealings confirmed by auditors; hard caps on pay (short-term incentive 150%, long-term incentive 180% of base salary).
  • Not shareholders-first: the KGaA form (management appointed by SCHOTT AG's general partner); 77% parent votes; an 18% to 23% free float that makes the stock "nearly un-investable for many large institutions" (Deutsche Bank); a 1.5% brand royalty; liquidity swept into the parent's cash pool; zero open-market insider purchases in twelve months; cash-settled virtual share awards; one-off listing bonuses (EUR 410k chief executive, EUR 205k finance director); fiscal-2025 individual pay disclosure lacking.
  • Why: the Carl Zeiss Foundation's statute favours long-term stewardship of the group. The listing was a partial capital-raising carve-out, not a handover to public owners.
C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F3445th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: an operator, not an allocator. Capital goes almost entirely into organic capacity (over EUR 800m since 2020) with a 10% to 20% payout. There have been no buybacks and no M&A. Returns on recent capital are low so far (about 5% pre-tax on FY2022 to FY2025 net new capital). The tools of enlightened allocation (buybacks at low multiples, pruning low-return lines, pacing capacity to demand) are absent or blocked by the structure. The one positive sign is that pay now rewards operating free cash flow and economic value added.

Group judgement

Company: Economic strength

C5618th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Economic strength is high in the installed base and unproven at the margin. Revenue is predictable, the balance sheet is light, earnings are clean, and underlying cash earnings are close to net income. But for four years capital has flowed in faster than profit: EPS is flat since FY2023, new-capital returns since FY2022 are near 5% pre-tax, and free cash flow has been a small fraction of net income. Consensus assumes a reversal: EPS growth of 11% to 12% a year in FY2027 and FY2028, margins rising to about 29% by FY2029, and free cash flow tripling. The mechanism is credible: operating leverage on built capacity, mix toward ready-to-use formats, the end of capital-spending growth. It is also untested, and it runs into the industry's capacity and price problem (C-29, C-31). For the model: put the FY2027 EBITDA margin about 30 to 60 basis points below consensus, move consensus capital spending into FY2027, and keep FY2028 and FY2029 margin and cash near consensus, but with a symmetrical ±10% EPS band that is wider than for a typical consumables company.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F2844th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: none at present. EPS has been flat at about EUR 1.00 for four years (FY2023 EUR 1.01, FY2024 0.99, FY2025 0.97, FY2026 consensus 0.98, about 0.90 without the compensation payment), while revenue grew about 16%. Consensus expects EPS growth to resume at 12% (FY2027), 11% (FY2028) and 8% (FY2029) on revenue growth of 6% to 7%. That growth is plausible but not yet durable. It depends on margin recovery that the company has not shown since FY2022.

  • Why revenue growth did not reach EPS (FY2023 to FY2025): depreciation rose from EUR 47m to EUR 79m (5.2% to 8.1% of sales); net interest went from EUR -6.9m to EUR -19.7m; the tax rate went from 19.5% to 23.5%. Together these absorbed all the EBITDA growth (FactSet basis EUR 211m to EUR 255m). The share count is constant at 150.6m (no buybacks, no issuance).
  • FY2021 to FY2025: EPS compound growth was 9.8% a year, almost all from FY2021 to FY2023, the pandemic and early high-value years.
  • Consensus bridge FY2025 to FY2028: EBITDA EUR 278m to EUR 342m (+EUR 64m), less depreciation up EUR 24m, plus lower financing costs, at a tax rate of about 22%, gives EPS EUR 0.97 to EUR 1.22. About two-thirds of the EPS growth comes from EBITDA margin expansion (28.2% to 29.0%) and one-third from revenue.
  • Durability: medium. Revenue growth of 6% to 7% is durable (C-43). The margin part requires utilisation to rise while depreciation keeps rising about 8% a year. The FY2026 base is inflated by the payment. The cleaner way to see FY2027 is about +14% on an underlying base (preface table), which is demanding for a year of peak capital spending.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F4240th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the scope to deploy capital is large (ready-to-use conversion, GLP-1, US localisation, cartridges). But recent returns on new capital have been poor: EBIT rose by only about EUR 14m between FY2022 and FY2025 on about EUR 310m of net new capital, a pre-tax incremental return near 5%. That is because capacity was built ahead of demand during a volume reversal. If consensus FY2029 EBIT is delivered, the incremental return over FY2022 to FY2029 recovers to about 16% pre-tax (about 12% to 13% post-tax). That is above the cost of capital (Jefferies uses 9%) but not exceptional. It supports a fair multiple of about 17x to 20x forward earnings, not the 25x to 30x of a proven compounder.

  • Arithmetic (FactSet basis, my derivation): FY2023 to FY2025 capital spending EUR 464m, less depreciation EUR 191m, plus working capital on EUR 165m of extra revenue (about EUR 36m), gives net new capital of about EUR 310m, against EUR +14m of EBIT. FY2021 to FY2025 overall: EUR +93m of EBIT on about EUR 520m of net new capital, about 18% pre-tax.
  • Pipeline of spending: about EUR 150m a year to FY2027, then a taper; the Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant (over EUR 100m); North Carolina (US$371m, status unconfirmed); Lebanon (done). The FY2024 to FY2027 investments are the ones whose returns are still unproven.
  • Signals of returns to come: the acquired capacity is contracted in part (over EUR 1bn of GLP-1 contracts; the EUR 700m lifetime syringe contract; "many capacity expansion projects supported by specific orders"); the Haemonetics-style "customer-funded" model is not used here (prepayments only about EUR 35m).
  • Fair multiple: a business growing EPS at 8% to 11% with about 13% post-tax incremental returns and very high revenue visibility merits about the five-year median of 18.9x on 24-month-forward EPS. A premium needs evidence that new lines earn the historic 20%-plus.
C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

B7013th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: average returns are good but falling, and new-capital returns are low for now. Company-reported return on capital employed was about 18% (post-tax, Deutsche Bank) to 18.8% (remuneration report) in FY2025, against 22% to 25% in FY2020 and FY2021. My cruder measure, post-tax operating profit over book equity plus net debt, is about 13%. Tangible returns equal reported returns, because there is no acquisition goodwill.

  • The fall comes from capital growing faster than profit (C-50). Over FY2022 to FY2025 the post-tax return on new invested capital was low single digits.
  • Peers: Gerresheimer 10.8% pre-tax return on capital employed (FY2023); Stevanato return on invested capital 7.4% (2024, capital-spending peak). SCHOTT Pharma remains the best of the European containment makers, but the gap is shrinking.
  • The remuneration framework uses economic value added (operating income less a capital charge; long-term incentive target of EUR 404m over four years, threshold EUR 304m). That aligns pay with returns, not just growth. The short-term incentive dropped return on capital in 2026 in favour of operating free cash flow.
  • For the model: returns on capital employed should trough in FY2026 and FY2027 (high capital spending, start-up costs) and recover from FY2028 if utilisation rises. The return path is the best single test of whether the capacity bet is working.
C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F2746th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: low as reported, high underneath. Free cash flow has been 8% to 38% of net income in the last three years on FactSet's definition (FY2025 EUR 11.5m; company definition EUR 36.8m, about 25%), because growth capital spending runs at 15% of sales. Operating cash flow is 1.2 times net income, and maintenance capital spending is only about 4% of sales. Conversion therefore depends almost entirely on when growth spending tapers. Consensus has conversion rising to 0.5 to 0.7 times; the timing of that rise is at risk in FY2027.

  • Underlying cash earnings: FY2025 operating cash flow EUR 180m against net income EUR 146m. Less maintenance spending of about EUR 40m (4% of sales, Jefferies), "steady-state" free cash flow would be about EUR 140m, about 0.95 times net income. That is the business's real cash earnings power once growth spending stops.
  • Recent trend: nine-month FY2026 free cash flow rose 49% to EUR 58.9m (collections, lower tax payments, financing); first-half free cash flow more than doubled to EUR 45m. Management denies factoring.
  • Working capital: stable at about 20% to 22% of revenue (20.2% FY2025; 21.8% at Q3 FY2026), rising with ready-to-use mix because longer production cycles hold more work in progress. Contract assets and receivables rose EUR 17.8m in the third quarter, and inventory EUR 6.1m in the first half. Jefferies' forecast of about 32% of sales by FY2027 looks like a definitional outlier against the company's own figures; model 21% to 23%. Each point of revenue in working capital is about EUR 11m of cash.
  • Consensus free cash flow: EUR 69m (FY2026), EUR 95m (FY2027), EUR 125m (FY2028), EUR 129m (FY2029), on capital spending of EUR 156m, 149m, 153m and 153m. The company says spending peaks in FY2027, so consensus FY2027 free cash flow is probably EUR 10m to 25m too high. Consensus also has no taper in FY2028 and FY2029, so free cash flow in those years could be EUR 15m to 30m higher. The cash profile is back-loaded rather than lower.
C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

C+5616th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: margins are high for the sector and trending down. The gross margin is 31% to 34% (36.0% peak in FY2022), the company EBITDA margin about 27% to 28% (FY2025 28.4%; nine months FY2026 26.7%), and the EBIT margin about 18%. The decline since FY2022 is structural in part (depreciation rising from 6.7% to 8.1% of sales, which explains about 60% of the gross-margin fall) and cyclical in part (polymer and vaccine shrinkage, start-up costs, the 2026 write-down).

  • Gross margin: 33.5% (FY2020), 36.0% (FY2022), 35.1% (FY2023), 33.7% (FY2024 and FY2025), 31.3% (nine months FY2026, with cost of sales +9.6% on revenue +4.1%). The FY2026 fall decomposes roughly into: the write-down (about 1.6 points of nine-month revenue), depreciation (about 0.5 points), and polymer under-absorption with start-up costs (the rest).
  • EBITDA margin (company): 26.6% (FY2023), 26.9% (FY2024), 28.4% (FY2025), 26.7% (nine months FY2026), guided 27% to 28% for FY2026 (about 27.5% without both one-offs). Segments: DDS 29.4% (nine months FY2026; over 34% before), DCS about 25%. Consensus expects 27.4%, 28.1%, 29.0% and 29.3% for FY2026 to FY2029. The FY2029 figure is close to the company's "towards 30%".
  • Peers (brokers' EBITDA basis): Sartorius Stedim 31% to 33%; GVS 25% to 27%; Gerresheimer 16% to 19%; Nolato 16% to 17%; Stevanato guides 26.8% for 2026. SCHOTT Pharma is second only to Sartorius Stedim in the peer table.
  • Trend for the model: margins should trough in FY2026 on an underlying basis and recover 50 to 80 basis points a year as lines fill, about 100 to 150 basis points slower than the company target if like-for-like prices drift down.
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

A783rd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: clean by sector standards. There is no adjusted-to-reported gap (the company does not adjust EBITDA or EPS), research and development is expensed, and audits are clean. Three items still need handling in the model: about EUR 25m to 35m a year of joint-venture and other income inside EBITDA with no matching revenue; the FY2026 one-offs; and leakages to the parent (a 1.5% brand royalty, about EUR 15m a year).

  • Joint ventures: India and Italy are equity-accounted inside EBITDA (associates carried at EUR 95.9m). They lift the reported margin by roughly 2 points and grow with the Indian syringe build-out. They are real profits, but not consolidated revenue or cash.
  • One-offs in FY2026: the EUR 12.3m write-down (cost) and the EUR 15m compensation (revenue). FX: no hedge accounting, so forward contracts' fair-value swings run through the income statement (a EUR 3.4m reduction in FX losses in the first quarter of FY2026; a EUR 1.8m improved FX result). A EUR 1.6m US tariff refund was booked in other operating income.
  • Tax: 23.5% in FY2025 against 19.5% to 20.2% before; consensus about 22%. Every point of tax is about 1.3% of EPS.
  • Other leakages: the brand royalty (1.5% of sales); related-party purchases from the parent (EUR 112m FY2025, mostly tubing at arm's length); interest on parent financing (EUR 19.7m net in FY2025, down in FY2026 after restructuring); lease liabilities of EUR 24.9m outside net debt.
  • Contrast: Gerresheimer capitalised EUR 9m to 17m of development costs and faces a bill-and-hold probe; Stevanato has internal-control weaknesses; GVS reports heavily adjusted figures. SCHOTT Pharma is the cleanest of the group.
C-76

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)
