5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1736C-4532P-0438
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?F3653rd of 54full answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?D3235th of 54full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

D3836th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: like-for-like price contributes about zero to revenue in the base case, roughly +0.5% in FY2027 and 0% from FY2028. Indexation passes through glass costs every six months and labour and overhead every February. Renewal concessions in bulk glass, ready-to-use vials and syringes take it back. Value growth comes from mix (ready-to-use at 5 to 15 times the bulk price), not from price on the same product. Price is therefore not a margin driver in my base case. It is the largest single risk to the margin, because it drops through at close to 100%: 1% of like-for-like price is about EUR 11m of EBITDA and EUR 0.055 of EPS (5%). Pricing splits three ways. Ready-to-use cartridges stay tight through 2027 and hold price. Ready-to-use vials and glass syringes are loosening and will give price on new and renewed contracts from 2027 to 2028. Bulk vials and ampoules keep conceding at renewal.

  • The evidence by product.
  • Ready-to-use cartridges (DCS): demand exceeds capacity at St. Gallen. Stevanato's EZ-fill cartridge lines are fully booked through most of 2027, and it is converting an under-used ready-to-use vial line to cartridges (Deutsche Bank, 3/4-Nov-26). Price holds through FY2027. From FY2028 the new Hungarian plant and Stevanato's conversions add capacity, and Jefferies notes volume discounts for large customers.
  • Ready-to-use vials: the standard 6ml price fell from EUR 0.75 to EUR 0.35 to 0.40 a unit in 2026 on excess sterilisation capacity (Gerresheimer executive, 27-Oct-26). SCHOTT Pharma has just tripled its US ready-to-use vial capacity at Lebanon. Its volume gains in this product come at falling prices.
  • Glass syringes (DDS): SCHOTT Pharma and Stevanato are "functionally equivalent" (Eli Lilly engineering adviser, 29-May-26), and awards follow proximity and capacity. Utilisation fell to about 90% in late 2025, against an equilibrium of 92% to 95%. Stevanato has freed capacity after its November 2026 customer cut. BD prices standard syringes at about US$0.30 to 0.35. New tenders in 2027 will be priced down. Installed registered volume keeps its contract price until renewal.
  • Bulk vials and ampoules: 5% to 10% concessions in recent glass negotiations (former SGD Pharma director, 19-Dec-25). Customers demand 5% to 7.5% on multi-year renewals. Nipro's packaging profit fell 88% and its FY2027 margin target is 1.1%, which shows the floor for undifferentiated glass.
  • Arithmetic for SCHOTT Pharma's mix. Assume about a third of contracts renew each year at three-year locks. FY2027: indexation +1.0% to +1.5% on most contracts; renewal concessions -0.5% to -1.0% on the renewing third; ready-to-use cartridges flat to up; net about +0.5%. FY2028 to FY2030: indexation about +1%, concessions about -1% (more syringe and ready-to-use vial renewals in an overcapacity phase), net about 0%. The bear case (25% to 30% probability, unchanged from the first set) is -2% a year from FY2028 on syringes and ready-to-use vials. That is about -1% for the group, costing about EUR 11m to 12m of EBITDA a year, cumulatively about -9% to -10% of FY2029 EPS.
  • Peers set the benchmark, and SCHOTT Pharma is below it. West: long-term model 2% to 3% a year, actual price above 4 points in the third quarter of 2026 (surcharges plus mix), on a 70% to 75% share of high-value elastomers. GVS: +1.5% a year in its 2026 to 2028 plan against 2.5% cost inflation; +2.0% in the first half of 2026 and +3.7% in the third quarter. SCHOTT Pharma discloses no price/volume split. Its gross margin fell from 36.0% to about 32.9% underlying while revenue rose 25%. The non-disclosure is consistent with price being flat at best.
  • Inflation pass-through works, but with a lag. Glass indexation every six months and labour every February mean that a cost spike (energy, a Swiss franc rise) costs margin for one to two quarters before it is recovered. This is a quarterly-volatility effect, not an annual one. It matters for the third set's view of quarterly predictability.
  • The July 2026 settlement is volume pricing, not price. It turned a contractual volume floor into about EUR 15m of compensation plus revised (lower) volumes. Volume-based pricing lets a supplier charge more per unit when a customer takes fewer. That supports reported price per unit while total revenue from the account falls. Read it as proof the contracts have teeth, and also that the floor is negotiable (Jefferies: customers "possess greater negotiating power than previously assumed"). It is not a sign of pricing power on new business.
  • Regulation helps the installed base, not the price of new volume. The FDA's August 2026 draft container-closure guidance (full extractables-and-leachables and integrity testing) and Annex 1 raise the cost of switching away from a registered supplier. They protect existing prices. They do not stop a buyer running a competitive tender for a new drug or a new format, which is where the price pressure is.
  • For the margin. Price adds nothing to the base-case margin path. Mix adds about 15 to 25 basis points a year (high-value share from 57% to about 60% by FY2029). Any price assumption above zero from FY2028 would need evidence that is not in the files.
5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7546P-0152
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

D4633rd of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: material, and it currently runs in reverse because costs are rising ahead of volume. My estimate: about 45% to 50% of revenue is truly variable (tubing 15%, other materials about 10%, part of energy, freight, some direct labour). Incremental volume through existing plants therefore carries an EBITDA contribution of about 45% to 55%. The FY2025 evidence is +11.5% constant-currency EBITDA on +5.8% revenue, an incremental margin of about 55%; GVS's rule (6% of revenue moves margin 150 to 200 basis points) implies the same. Plant-level fixed costs, most labour and depreciation (8% to 9% of sales and rising) make up the rest. A 1% change in volume moves EBITDA about 1.8% to 2% and EPS about 2.5% to 3%. A 5% volume miss or gain moves FY2028 EPS by about ±13%. Combined with a ±1% like-for-like price uncertainty, the FY2028 EPS range is about EUR 1.02 to 1.42 around consensus of EUR 1.22.

  • Evidence of leverage: FY2025 constant-currency EBITDA +11.5% on +5.8% organic revenue (positive). Third quarter FY2026 EBITDA down about EUR 7m on revenue up about EUR 26m (negative: start-up costs, polymer, optimisation costs, mix toward lower-margin DCS). DDS nine-month EBITDA down EUR 18.6m on revenue down 1.7%, or about EUR 6m without the write-down. DDS Q3 FY2025 margin over 36%, against 29.4% a year later.
  • Unit sensitivity, rule of thumb: EUR 10m of volume revenue is about EUR 5m of EBITDA and EUR 0.025 of EPS. A Stevanato-sized single-customer shock (10 points off DDS growth, about 4 points of group revenue, about EUR 45m) costs about EUR 22m of EBITDA and EUR 0.11 of EPS, about 10%, before any compensation. A like-for-like price change carries close to 100% drop-through: 1% of price is about EUR 10m to 11m of EBITDA and EUR 0.055 of EPS (5%). Price is therefore the more dangerous variable per point.
  • Peer check: GVS says 6% of revenue moves its margin 150 to 200 basis points; Jefferies' bear case (revenue growth 2.5 points lower each year, margin 200 basis points lower) cuts value 35%.
  • What widens the range: new plants add fixed cost in steps (start-up costs in the high single-digit millions of euros a year); depreciation rises regardless of use; locked prices limit the upside offset; the absence of hedging lets FX add ±3% to EPS.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C-5226th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: operating leverage will lift the EBITDA margin modestly, about 40 to 50 basis points a year, not the 80 to 90 basis points that consensus puts into FY2028. At EBIT it will barely show until FY2029. The cost base has two layers. Marginal contribution through existing plants is high, about 45% to 55%. The step-fixed cost of each new site eats much of it, so the realised incremental margin has averaged about 32%. Over FY2027 to FY2030 a larger share of growth runs through capacity already built (Hungarian syringes, St. Gallen and Hungarian cartridges, Lebanon vials, Müllheim polymer), so I raise the realised incremental margin to 36% to 37% from FY2028. FY2027 is held back by the third quarter's exit rate and by start-up costs at the new Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant. The leverage is asymmetric: a volume shortfall on built capacity loses close to the full 45% to 55% contribution, while volume that needs new capacity earns only about a third.

  • The last six years in one line each (company EBITDA, incremental margin on extra revenue). FY2020 to FY2022: about 37%, when the pandemic filled plants. FY2022 to FY2023: about 26% (destocking). FY2023 to FY2024: about 33%. FY2024 to FY2025: about 77%, lifted by currency and the exceptional third quarter. FY2025 to FY2026 underlying: about 10%. FY2022 to FY2026 cumulative: about 32%. The year-to-year range is so wide (10% to 77%) that single years mislead. The multi-year figure is the one to model.
  • Why the realised figure sits below the marginal one. Tubing (about 15% of revenue), other materials (about 10%) and part of energy, freight and direct labour are variable, about 45% to 50% of revenue in total. Each new site, though, adds fixed staff, quality and validation teams and start-up costs before it adds revenue. Management's own figure for the combined Serbian and Hungarian ramp-up costs is a "double-digit million euro annual drag" (third-quarter FY2025 call). That is larger than the "high single-digit millions" the first set used. Capital spending at 14% to 20% of sales against depreciation of 8% to 9% means the fixed base has kept growing faster than revenue.
  • The D&A wedge. Depreciation rises from EUR 79m (FY2025) to about EUR 88.5m (FY2026; EUR 65.5m in the nine months, up 14%), EUR 96m, 103m, 109m and 114m. As a share of sales it peaks at about 8.8% in FY2028 and then eases as capital spending tapers. EBIT therefore grows more slowly than EBITDA until FY2029: EBIT margin 20.4% (FY2025), 19.0%, 19.1%, 19.6%, 20.2%, 20.7%. This is the mechanism behind four years of flat EPS, and it does not fully reverse until FY2029.
  • Where the leverage lands, line by line. Gross margin: down from 36.0% (FY2022) to about 32.9% underlying in the nine months of FY2026. I expect it to stabilise at 32% to 33% in FY2027 and edge up about 50 basis points a year from FY2028 as absorption improves. Operating expenses grow more slowly than revenue (administration digitalisation, the efficiency programme), which is why EBITDA margin holds up better than gross margin. Joint-venture income (about EUR 14m in FY2025, about EUR 20m by FY2030) adds margin with no revenue, about 1.4 to 1.5 points.
  • Peer precedents for the time from commissioning to full use. Stevanato's brownfield Latina site took seven quarters to reach gross-profit breakeven (acquired in the first quarter of 2022, breakeven in the third quarter of 2024). Its greenfield Fishers site started commercial production in the third quarter of 2024, reached gross-profit breakeven in the third quarter of 2026, and runs at 25% to 30% of target capacity. Deutsche Bank forecasts 48% use in 2027 and 65% in 2028, against management's 85% by end-2028. Gerresheimer plans about four years from construction to full capacity, in three equipment waves. Applied to SCHOTT Pharma: the Hungarian syringe lines (opened June 2024) should be in their margin-accretive phase in FY2027. The Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant (ground broken June 2025, over EUR 100m) will be a margin drag in FY2027 and FY2028 and accretive only from FY2029. North Carolina, still "in the planning phase" with "no immediate pressure to commit final capital" (finance director), adds no revenue inside the window. If it is committed, it adds start-up cost and depreciation from about FY2029.
  • The asymmetry that matters for the cone. A 1% volume shortfall on built capacity costs about EUR 5m to 6m of EBITDA and EUR 0.03 of EPS (about 2.5%). A 1% volume gain that needs new capacity earns about EUR 3.5m. The downside per point is roughly 1.5 times the upside. The cone is therefore skewed down for any given volume error, which the third and fourth sets should carry into their assessment of predictability.
  • What would change my view. Upward: an underlying DDS margin back above 32% in the first half of FY2027, and a group margin above 28% in the first quarter of FY2027 against the third quarter's 26.8%. Either would show that the third quarter was a trough, and would point to consensus-like 29% margins in FY2028. Downward: a first-half FY2027 margin below 27% with revenue growing 7% or more. That would mean the fixed-cost layer is growing faster than I assume, and FY2028 would land nearer 27.5%.
5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7666P-0255
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?C6617th of 54full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C5520th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is negligible as an amplifier and turns into a small EPS tailwind. The financial charge falls from about EUR 13m (FY2025) to about EUR 1m (FY2030), worth about EUR 0.06 of EPS, or 4% to 5% of cumulative EPS growth. On my base case the balance sheet moves from net debt of about EUR 104m (September 2026) to net cash of about EUR 80m in FY2029 and EUR 195m to 200m in FY2030. Two things matter more than the level of debt. First, the dependence on the parent: the EUR 412m revolving facilities end on 31-Dec-2027 and surplus cash is swept into SCHOTT AG's cash pool. Second, what the company does with a balance sheet that becomes net cash: commit North Carolina, raise the payout, or nothing. That choice, not leverage, is the balance-sheet variable for FY2029 and FY2030.

  • The facts. Company net debt (excluding leases of EUR 24.9m) was EUR 106.0m at 30-Jun-2026, against EUR 122.2m at 30-Sep-2025. Gross liabilities to SCHOTT AG were EUR 231m. The facilities total EUR 412m, of which EUR 224m was drawn at 31-Mar-2026, at a monthly reference rate plus a defined margin. Equity ratio 58.9%. Net debt to EBITDA is about 0.4x.
  • The interest path I model. FY2026 EUR -8.5m (nine months -6.2m, after the first-quarter intra-group restructuring). FY2027 -7.5m: average net debt of about EUR 90m during the capital-spending peak, plus pension and lease interest of about EUR 1.7m, facility fees and currency effects. FY2028 -5.5m. FY2029 -3.0m. FY2030 -1.0m, as net cash earns a low cash-pool rate. Consensus implies about EUR -4m from FY2027 (EBIT EUR 217m against pre-tax EUR 213m). That is EUR 3m to 4m, or about EUR 0.02 of EPS, kinder than the debt level supports in the peak capital-spending year.
  • Sensitivity. 100 basis points on about EUR 230m of gross parent debt is about EUR 2.3m pre-tax, or 1.2% of EPS. No hedge accounting is applied, so the fair-value swings of up to EUR 292m of currency forwards run through the income statement. Expect EUR 2m to 4m of unexplained noise in the financial result in any half-year (FY2026 first-quarter currency losses fell by EUR 3.4m; the nine-month currency result improved by EUR 1.8m).
  • The December 2027 refinancing. The parent facilities end on 31-Dec-2027, in the first quarter of FY2028 and the last quarter of the 18-month window. Base case: rolled on similar arm's-length terms. A 50 basis point wider margin would cost about EUR 1m a year, which is immaterial. The real risk is non-financial. The parent sets the terms of the cash pool, the tubing contract (also December 2027) and the royalty together, so any change in its stance shows up across all three. I give a material re-pricing about a 10% probability.
  • The balance sheet becomes lazy from FY2029. Without a buyback, which the KGaA form and the thin float rule out, and without M&A, net cash builds at EUR 90m to 115m a year from FY2029. Three uses are plausible. (a) North Carolina (US$371m, about EUR 330m, announced March 2024): committing it in FY2028 would keep capital spending near EUR 165m to 190m to FY2030 and hold net debt near zero. (b) A higher payout: the 10% to 20% policy leaves room, and the parent is the main recipient. (c) Nothing: cash sits in the parent's pool. My base case assumes nothing is committed and a payout of about 18%. Given the investment habit since 2020, I judge (a) the likelier departure from the base case, and it would cut FY2029 to FY2030 free cash flow by EUR 40m to 60m a year without touching EPS until the plant runs.
  • Peer contrast. Gerresheimer at 4.7x to 4.95x leverage had to sell Centor and its plastics unit for EUR 1.5bn, diluting its margin. GVS runs at 2.2x, targets 1.0x by 2028, and holds EUR 260m of its EUR 280m gross debt at fixed rates. SCHOTT Pharma's cash-pool debt is floating, so it carries more rate sensitivity per euro, but on far less debt.
  • Book value per share. About EUR 6.7 (FY2026) rising to about EUR 9.9 (FY2030) on retained earnings of about 82%. Return on equity stays about 14% to 15%. It is diluted from FY2029 by low-yield net cash unless that cash is redeployed.
5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7752P-0353
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

C5219th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: only a small part is cuttable without hurting the franchise: about EUR 15m to 25m gross over three years, 1.5 to 2.5 points of margin, mostly from administration, digitalisation and footprint. The largest "reducible" item is not a cut at all: start-up costs and polymer under-absorption fall away as lines fill or are redeployed.

Cost base FY2025, EUR 986m of revenue, EUR 706m of costs before depreciation (proportions are my estimates, labelled as such):

  • Tubing, about 15% of revenue (about EUR 150m): not cuttable before December 2027 (parent contract, volume discounts already in place). Reducible only through better yields and less scrap.
  • Other materials (polymer resin, elastomers, packaging, process media), about 10%: limited; single sources (Topas, West).
  • Personnel (about 4,800 staff), roughly 30% of revenue: partly reducible through moves to best-cost sites (Serbia, Hungary) and automation; quality and validation staff are untouchable in a regulated business.
  • Energy and utilities, about 4% to 5%: reducible only through prices and technology (electric melting at the parent).
  • Brand royalty (1.5%, about EUR 15m) and parent services: contractual; not cuttable.
  • Start-up costs at new sites (high single-digit millions of euros a year) and polymer under-absorption (my estimate EUR 15m to 25m of lost contribution since FY2024): transitory, if utilisation rises.
  • Administration and selling: the "double-digit million euro" efficiency programme (digitalisation of administrative and manufacturing processes) sits here.
  • Depreciation (8% of sales, rising): not cuttable.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C-5325th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: about EUR 10m to 13m net of all cost actions reaches EBITDA by FY2029, about 90 to 110 basis points, phased roughly 40% in FY2027, 40% in FY2028 and 20% in FY2029. The finance director's "double-digit million euro" efficiency programme is worth EUR 10m to 20m gross. About half reaches the margin; the rest offsets wage inflation net of the February labour indexation. The fading of ramp-up costs at the Serbian and Hungarian sites adds about EUR 3m to 5m by FY2029, but the new Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant adds start-up costs of its own in FY2027 and FY2028. The large fixed items are not cuttable inside the window: the brand royalty (1.5% of sales, about EUR 16m), the tubing framework agreement (fixed to December 2027), depreciation, and quality and validation staff. A peer-style "large cut" is not on the table and would be the wrong signal for a company whose moat is quality.

  • The programme. Confirmed by Reinhard Mayer (finance director); "double-digit million euro" savings, mainly from digitalising administrative and manufacturing processes, automating visual inspection and packaging, and reducing manual labour (Jefferies, August 2026). No phasing, baseline or cost-to-achieve has been disclosed (C-72: guidance is vague). Model it as gross EUR 6m in FY2027, EUR 6m in FY2028 and EUR 3m in FY2029, about EUR 15m in total. That runs against wage inflation of about EUR 9m a year (personnel about 30% of sales at 3%), of which the annual February labour and overhead indexation in customer contracts recovers perhaps half. I keep one-off costs to achieve the savings inside operating costs, because the company does not adjust.
  • Ramp-up costs fading (gross EUR 10m to 15m a year now). The Serbian ampoule hub (opened April 2025) and the Hungarian syringe site (opened June 2024) should be largely absorbed by FY2028, worth about EUR 5m to 8m. The Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant then adds new pre-operating and validation cost of about EUR 3m to 5m a year in FY2027 and FY2028. Net, about EUR 3m to 5m by FY2029. Stevanato's own start-up costs for Fishers and Latina were EUR 6.7m in the nine months of 2026, which shows the scale per greenfield site.
  • Polymer under-absorption (the largest "reducible" item, not a cut). Müllheim's polymer lines were sized to triple capacity by 2027 for mRNA. mRNA work fell from about 8% (FY2025) to about 5% of sales (FY2026). Polymer syringes cost 2.5 to 3 times glass to make, so the fixed-cost penalty per idle line is higher than for glass. The company is redeploying the lines (mental health, aesthetics, surgery, intravenous, long-acting injectables, large-volume syringes) and has not announced a closure or impairment. Base case: about EUR 2m to 3m a year of recovery from FY2027 as non-mRNA work qualifies. Qualification takes 9 to 24 months, and management has promised "recovery ahead" for two years. A closure would release more (my estimate EUR 10m to 15m of fixed cost a year) but would bring an impairment. I give it about a 10% probability inside the window.
  • What is not cuttable, and why. The royalty is a percentage of revenue under the parent's contract, and Jefferies notes the KGaA form prevents independent renegotiation. Tubing is about 15% of revenue under arm's-length terms with volume discounts, fixed to December 2027. Depreciation rises mechanically. Quality and validation staff cannot be touched in a regulated business. So the only "material large cut" would be footprint, and the footprint is being expanded, not shrunk.
  • Peer precedents and what they say. Gerresheimer's "gto" programme targets EUR 50m to 70m of annual EBITDA improvement by 2028, about 2 to 3 points on its sales. It includes the Chicago Heights closure and a headquarters-cost cut of more than 20%, but needs up-front cash, and Deutsche Bank expects the full run-rate only in 2028. GVS's Puerto Rico closure saved about EUR 200k a month, about 50 basis points, within one quarter. Nolato's US start-ups cut its Medical EBITA margin to 11.6% from 12.3%. The precedent is clear: large margin gains from cost come from closures under duress. SCHOTT Pharma is neither under duress nor shrinking, so its cost gains will be incremental, and they will be offset partly by its own expansion.
  • Timing. About EUR 4m to 5m net in FY2027, a further EUR 4m to 5m in FY2028 and about EUR 2m to 3m in FY2029. Most of it shows in the second half of each year, because indexation resets in February and the programme ramps.
5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4760P-0550
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?C+6015th of 54full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D5028th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: two loops will decide margins from 2027 to 2030. One is reinforcing: capacity filling, then absorption, then margin, then cash, then more ready-to-use conversion. The other is balancing: synchronised capacity, then lower utilisation, then tender discounting, then slower capital spending. They act on different products. The reinforcing loop dominates in DCS (cartridges, specialty and US ready-to-use vials) through FY2028. The balancing loop dominates in DDS glass syringes from 2027 and spreads to ready-to-use vials. Net, the reinforcing loop wins modestly in FY2027 and FY2028, giving margin gains of 40 to 50 basis points a year. The balancing loop strengthens in FY2029 and FY2030 and caps the margin near 29% to 29.5%. A slower, steadier loop underlies both and protects revenue: regulatory lock-in, where each new registration is a 10- to 30-year annuity. The single fast loop that could tip the balance is the customer-cut-and-backfill loop, which Stevanato's November 2026 shock has just started.

  1. Capacity filling (reinforcing; strongest in DCS; FY2027 to FY2028). New lines fill, fixed costs are absorbed, margin and cash rise, and the cash funds the next ready-to-use conversion. Evidence it runs: DCS EBITDA +10.1% in the nine months of FY2026 (margin 24.6%, up from 24.1%), DCS organic +11% in the third quarter, St. Gallen demand above capacity, Lebanon tripled. What stalls it: validation timelines of 6 to 24 months (Stevanato suffered a two-year delay), and new capacity added before old capacity is full. On the line items it delivers the DCS margin of about 25.0% (FY2027) rising to about 25.9% (FY2030), and DCS revenue growth of 7% to 8%. A caution: the DCS margin includes all the joint-venture income (Jefferies), worth about 2.5 points. DCS without the joint ventures earns about 22%, so its operating leverage is less spectacular than the reported figure suggests.
  2. Synchronised overcapacity (balancing; strongest in DDS glass syringes; from 2027). Every leader adds capacity, utilisation falls, tenders get discounted, margins fall and capital spending eventually slows. Evidence it runs: syringe utilisation down from 100% to about 90% in late 2025 (Aptar). Experts project 10% to 20% excess capacity in three to five years and 20% by 2030. Stevanato's Fishers site is at 25% to 30% of target. BD's US$110m Columbus Neopak line supplies from mid-2026. SCHOTT Pharma is itself adding 60% Hungarian syringe capacity. How long similar cycles lasted: the post-pandemic sterile fill-finish overhang left utilisation near 50%, and new GLP-1 demand took about two to three years to absorb it. Bioprocessing destocking took about two years to normalise (Sartorius Stedim, 2023 to 2024). What stops this loop: exit is almost impossible for 20-year sterile lines, so the loop closes through slower capital spending, not closures. That is why the most important counter-move is SCHOTT Pharma's own decision on North Carolina. On the line items it holds the DDS margin at 31.5% to 32% (FY2026 underlying) rising only to about 34% by FY2030, against over 35% in FY2025, and DDS growth at 5% to 6%.
  3. Customer cut and backfill (balancing; fast; 2027). A customer cuts volume, the supplier's freed capacity bids for other volume, and prices fall across suppliers. Stevanato lost at least 10 points of 2027 growth on Nexa syringes from one GLP-1 originator (3-Nov-26), says it has backfilled "a majority" through non-GLP-1 biologics (+30% in the third quarter), and received variable compensation. Validation takes 30 months to three years (Deutsche Bank), so backfill comes slowly and through new programmes, not existing registered ones. The pressure therefore falls on 2027 to 2028 tenders for new biologics and biosimilar syringes, exactly where SCHOTT Pharma wants to fill Hungary. SCHOTT Pharma ran this same loop in reverse in FY2026: its own glass syringe customer's cut produced the EUR 12.25m write-down and the EUR 15m compensation. The cut volumes remain lower under the revised contract.
  4. Regulatory lock-in annuity (reinforcing; slow; all years). Each drug registered in SCHOTT Pharma containment locks in a supplier for 10 to 30 years (97% repeat revenue). Annex 1 and the FDA guidance raise the bar each year. This loop is why revenue visibility is high. It protects volume on existing drugs, not price on new ones, and not volume when a customer orders less. On the line items it puts a floor of about 4% to 5% under organic growth.
  5. Ready-to-use capacity versus premium (mixed). More ready-to-use capacity lowers prices, which speeds adoption and raises value per unit against bulk, but erodes the premium over time. In vials the erosion is already visible (price roughly halved in 2026). In cartridges it lies ahead (fully booked through 2027). The net is positive for revenue (the value pool grows several-fold as cartridges go from under 5% to an expected 66% to 70% ready-to-use over about ten years). It is mildly positive for margin until the premium narrows.
  6. GLP-1 per-dose deflation (mixed, and better for SCHOTT Pharma than for syringe specialists). Lower prices bring more patients and more doses, but push demand toward cheaper formats: vials (direct-to-consumer at about US$300; ex-US pens with multi-dose cartridges at 75% of prescriptions). A monthly multi-dose cartridge at US$0.05 to 0.06 replaces four syringes at US$0.30 to 0.35, a potential US$100m revenue headwind for syringe suppliers (Deutsche Bank). SCHOTT Pharma leads in vials, is second in cartridges and third in syringes, so the format shift moves volume between its own segments, from DDS to DCS. That costs some revenue per patient but keeps the business. More than 45 generic semaglutide programmes add a long tail of lower-price volume.
  7. Gerresheimer as share donor (reinforcing; slow). BaFin probe, the Chicago Heights closure, customers "hesitant to award new business". This helps SCHOTT Pharma in vials and moulded glass. It helps more in new awards than in existing registrations, because switching takes years, so it is worth perhaps 0.5 points of growth a year from FY2028. It is not in my base case and is upside.
  • The earliest dated signs of which loop is winning. (a) The FY2027 margin guide on 10-Dec-2026 and the fourth-quarter FY2026 underlying margin, which reads only as the reported figure less about EUR 15m. (b) The first-quarter FY2027 DDS margin in February 2027, against 29.4% in the third quarter of FY2026: above 31% says capacity filling, below 29% says balancing. (c) Stevanato's fourth-quarter 2026 results and 2027 guidance (about March 2027), for backfill progress and Fishers utilisation. (d) The high-value share above 59% to 60% in the first half of FY2027. (e) Any disclosed syringe-tender pricing in the first half of 2027 from BD, Stevanato or Gerresheimer.

6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement60C-7976D-0268D-0368D-0460D-0556D-0656
Group judgement

Risks

C6016th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecasts for the next 18 months are moderately predictable, and more predictable than the last three years of estimate cuts suggest. The base case is built almost wholly on strengths the company has shown. The improvement it forecasts comes mainly from lines that are mechanical. The parts of the earlier work agree with one another. What limits predictability is concentrated and specific: one margin line, one large customer relationship, and two guidance days.

The reasoning, joined up.

  • The case is internally consistent (D-01). Both earlier sets and the later research tell one story: annuity-like revenue, with earnings that depend on filling built capacity at today's prices. The seven frictions I found are about size and timing. They leave the base case where it was, with less price risk inside FY2027, a lower starting margin and a larger single-customer exposure than the earlier sets stated.
  • The base case needs no new capability (D-02). EBITDA growth of about EUR 20m has been delivered in two of the last three years. Everything above the base case (consensus, the company's target, our bull case) needs operating leverage through new capacity, which the company has not shown since the pandemic, and which peers achieved on time only with price or plant closures.
  • The return to EPS growth is mostly mechanical (D-03). About three-quarters of the swing from flat EPS to about +8% in FY2027 comes from depreciation growing more slowly, a tax rate that has stopped rising and a lower financial charge. This is the main reason I rate the 18-month EPS forecast as more reliable than the company's record on medium-term promises would suggest. Beyond FY2027 the forecasts lean on one driver, the incremental margin, and predictability falls.
  • The cone is moderate and its tail is to the left (D-04). FY2027 EPS of EUR 0.96 to 1.12 at 80% confidence. Balanced around our base, clearly negative against consensus, mildly positive against the guidance I expect. The risk gathers on guidance days, 10-Dec-2026 and December 2027, not inside the year.
  • The case can be followed but not diagnosed (D-05, D-06). Three clean quarterly reports inside six months carry the DDS margin, which tests the thesis soonest. Nothing the company discloses separates price from absorption. The trace from EBITDA to EPS is exact; the trace from the company's statements to EBITDA is not.
  • The share price is the company's own, except in industry shocks (D-07). Delivery will be rewarded without help from the sector, and GLP-1 news will be punished without regard to the company's exposure.

The steer for the memo's riskiness topic. Describe the case as simple and trackable in its central path, and fragile in a narrow place. The fragilities, in order: (1) the starting margin for FY2027, tested in February 2027; (2) a format or volume decision by Eli Lilly, which cannot be forecast and can only be watched through prescription data and Lilly's own statements; (3) price on renewals from 2028, which lies mostly outside the 18 months; (4) the parent contracts in December 2027, where the tubing agreement renews automatically for three years unless a party asks for changes, so the default is continuity.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

A765th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: narrow, and not changing. There is one industry (injectable primary packaging), two segments, 17 sites in Europe, the Americas and Asia, over 1,800 customers, and no customer above 10% of sales. That makes the industry drivers easy to read and revenue highly predictable. The product-level exposures, though, are concentrated: GLP-1 at a low-teens share of sales (two-thirds Lilly); polymer at about 5% (mostly mRNA, under-used); vaccines at 15% to 20%. So predictability of total revenue is high, and predictability of margins is moderate.

  • Regions: EMEA 54%, Americas 29%, Asia and the South Pacific 17% (FY2025); joint ventures in India and Italy add equity income.
  • Customer mix: the top five are about 30% of revenue and the next five about 15%, against Stevanato's top ten at 53.6%.
  • What it means: the revenue line can be modelled from three drivers (market growth, high-value mix, the GLP-1 and vaccine sub-trends); the margin line needs a site-by-site utilisation view, which the company does not disclose.
D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D6829th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: it depends on which "expected change" is meant, and the distinction is the most useful thing I can give the Consumer here. Our base case for the next 18 months (EPS from EUR 0.97 to about EUR 1.05, a margin of 27.9%) needs nothing the business has not already shown. It needs EBITDA to grow by about EUR 20m, which the company did in FY2024 (+EUR 18.5m) and FY2025 (+EUR 22.8m), and it needs no new shock. Consensus (EUR 1.10 in FY2027, a 29.0% margin in FY2028) needs one strength the company has not shown since FY2022: turning volume into margin while new capacity is still arriving. The company's own target ("towards 30%" by FY2029) and our bull case need two more: refilling the polymer lines, and holding price on renewals in an over-supplied market. The base case is plausible on demonstrated strengths. The cases above it are not yet.

What each layer of the expected change rests on.

Layer What it needs Has the business shown it?
Revenue growth of 5.5% to 6.5% (about 7% underlying) Volume in cartridges, specialty and ready-to-use vials, GLP-1 glass syringes; no further fall in mRNA or vaccines Yes. DCS grew 9.2% in the nine months and 11% in the third quarter. Quarterly revenue has met or beaten estimates more often than not. Order visibility reaches into FY2027 in both segments. St. Gallen cartridge demand exceeds capacity.
EBITDA of about EUR 306m in FY2027 (+EUR 20m; +EUR 22m underlying) An incremental margin of about 30% on about EUR 72m of extra underlying revenue Yes, as an average: the realised incremental margin since FY2022 is about 32%. Not in FY2026 itself (about 10%). The condition is that the second half of FY2026 was a trough (D-01, point 1).
Lines below EBITDA in FY2027 (depreciation +EUR 7.5m; financial result EUR 1m better; tax 22.5%) Nothing operational Yes. Quarterly depreciation has already flattened at EUR 22m to 23m. The intra-group refinancing is done (nine-month financial result EUR -6.2m).
Margin up 50 basis points a year from FY2028 (our base) An incremental margin of 36% to 37%: more growth through plants already built Partly. The Hungarian syringe site took 14 months from opening (June 2024) to commercial supply (August 2025), and its ramp-up costs still run at EUR 2m to 3.5m a quarter (finance director, August 2025).
Margin of 29.0% in FY2028 (consensus) and "towards 30%" by FY2029 (company) Operating leverage of 80 to 90 basis points a year during the capital-spending peak; polymer refilled; price held No. Not since FY2020 to FY2022, and that expansion rested on conditions now absent (below).
Free cash flow tripling by FY2029 Capital spending falling to about 11% of sales; no commitment to North Carolina The decision is management's and it is now paid on operating free cash flow. Deferring North Carolina since 2024 is restraint demonstrated. The taper itself is still a promise.

Why the FY2020 to FY2022 expansion is not evidence for the next one. The margin rose from 22.5% to 26.7% on three things: a pandemic that filled every plant (customers stockpiled), a high-value share of sales that rose by many points in a short time, and price rises of 25% to 40% in glass. Today the high-value share has stalled (59% in the third quarter of FY2026 against 60% a year earlier), utilisation is below the equilibrium in syringes and polymer, and price is flat at best. Jefferies and Deutsche Bank both say the earlier conditions are absent. The strength that expansion demonstrated was filling plants in a shortage. The strength now needed is filling plants in a surplus, which is a commercial skill, and the company has just lost its most senior commercial executive to Becton Dickinson (BD).

What the peer record says about the strength that is missing. I count seven attempts by comparable companies to lift margins out of a capital-spending peak. Two succeeded on time, and both had a lever SCHOTT Pharma lacks. West (2016 to 2021) had price of 2% to 4% a year and added capacity inside existing plants, keeping capital spending at 6% to 8% of sales. GVS (FY2022 to FY2025, from 20.4% to 25.2%) closed sites. Three were late or failed, and all three had greenfield plants and concentrated customers: Stevanato missed its 30% target on schedule, and its Fishers plant needed eight quarters to reach gross-profit breakeven and runs at 25% to 30% of target capacity more than two years after its start; Gerresheimer needed a restructuring; Nolato's medical margin fell to about 11% on start-up costs. The other two are SCHOTT Pharma's own: FY2020 to FY2022 (success, in a shortage) and FY2023 to FY2026 (no progress at EBIT). The pattern: success came with price or with closures, and from adding capacity at existing sites; lateness came with greenfield validation and customer concentration. SCHOTT Pharma has neither price nor closures. But its expansions are at existing sites (Lukácsháza, St. Gallen, Lebanon, Müllheim) and it has deferred its one greenfield plant. That places it between the two groups, which is where our base case sits: margin gains that are real, about half the size consensus assumes, and about a year late against the company's target.

The strengths the thesis does rely on, all demonstrated. Registration lock-in and repeat revenue. Quality and a clean audit record. Capital projects delivered on time. Contracts with teeth (the July 2026 settlement turned a volume floor into about EUR 15m). Cost pass-through by indexation. A light balance sheet. And, new since August 2025, a finance director who has delivered the financing saving and the cash improvement he promised (nine-month free cash flow +49%).

The strengths it would need to acquire for anything above the base case. A record of winning new syringe volume against Stevanato and BD without giving price. A proven redeployment of polymer capacity (promised for two years). Pricing power on new volume (no evidence; the company discloses no price and volume split). A record of keeping medium-term promises (the listing-era targets were cut within 27 months). And a chief executive with more than six months of results.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?B+685th of 54full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

B6010th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the cone is narrow at revenue, moderate at EBITDA and EPS, and wide at free cash flow. For FY2027 my 80% ranges are: revenue EUR 1,065m to 1,130m (about ±3%); EBITDA EUR 290m to 320m (about ±5%); EPS EUR 0.96 to 1.12 around a base of about EUR 1.04 to 1.05 (about -8% to +7%); free cash flow EUR 30m to 80m around EUR 54m. Around our own base the EPS cone is close to symmetrical in its body, with a fatter left tail. The skew depends on the reference point, and that is the practical finding: against the guidance the company is likely to give, outcomes lean to the upper half on margin; against consensus (EUR 1.10), about four outcomes in five fall below. The case is moderately predictable: more so than Stevanato or Gerresheimer, less so than West. Most of what is uncertain becomes visible by May 2027.

What makes the cone, source by source (effect on FY2027 EPS; my estimates).

Source Downside Upside Why the two sides differ
The starting margin: was the second half of FY2026 (about 26.3% underlying) a trough or a run-rate? -5% (a 27.0% margin) +3% (28.5%) Costs management calls temporary are unquantified; the upside needs polymer to help
Volume, ±1.5 points of growth -4% +2.5% A shortfall on built capacity loses 45% to 55% of the revenue; a gain that needs new shifts or lines earns about 30% (second set, P-01)
Like-for-like price, ±0.5% in FY2027 -2.5% +2.5% Three-year price locks; only about a third of contracts renew in a year; no reallocation tenders after Stevanato's cut (D-01, point 3)
Currency (a 5% move in the US dollar; the Swiss franc) -2.5% +2.5% Translation is unhedged; the cost base is net short the franc
Lines below EBITDA -2% +2% No hedge accounting (EUR 2m to 4m of noise in a half-year); timing of depreciation on the Hungarian cartridge plant; one point of tax is 1.3%
A single-customer event (a format, volume or supplier change at a GLP-1 originator) -6% to -10% before compensation; about a one-in-ten chance of reaching FY2027 results none Stevanato's cut was announced in November for the following year; take-or-pay compensation arrives later and in part

Combined, the symmetric sources give about ±6%. The single-customer tail and the asymmetry in volume stretch the lower end to about -8%.

The skew, stated against three reference points.

  • Against our base (about EUR 1.04 to 1.05): I put about a 12% chance on EPS below EUR 0.96 and about 8% on EPS above EUR 1.12. The body is balanced. The tail is to the left.
  • Against the guidance I expect (growth of 5% to 7% on the reported base; a margin of 27% to 28% or "around 28%"): the lean is positive on margin, because our 27.9% sits at the top of that range, and neutral to slightly negative on growth, because our 5.5% sits in its lower half.
  • Against consensus (EUR 1.10; a 28.1% margin): the skew is clearly negative. Consensus sits at about the 80th percentile of my range.

The cone is narrower inside a fiscal year than the history of estimate revisions suggests. Consensus FY2027 EPS fell 17% in twelve months, and FY2026 EPS has ranged from EUR 1.38 to EUR 0.93. Those falls came at guidance events (May 2024 for mRNA; December 2025 for FY2026 and the medium-term targets), when the next year's range was reset. Within each fiscal year since the listing, the outcome has landed inside or above the margin range first guided, and inside the growth range (at or just below its lower end in FY2025, after the mRNA warning of May 2024). So predictability is high once a year's guide is set, and the risk gathers on the two guidance days in the window: 10-Dec-2026 (month 1) and December 2027 (month 13), when the FY2028 guide meets the syringe-capacity and oral-drug debates.

The cone narrows early. Two of the three largest sources (the starting margin and the DDS recovery) are observable in the first-quarter results in February 2027 and confirmed or not by the first-half results in May 2027. Order visibility already covers part of FY2027. Price on renewals, the capital-spending taper and the parent contracts (December 2027) resolve late.

The tail events in this industry are large, which is why the tail is fat. Stevanato lost about 10 points of 2027 growth in one announcement. Gerresheimer's injectables unit earned EUR 6m of EBITDA in a quarter against EUR 32m a year earlier when it stopped building stock. SCHOTT Pharma's own FY2026 shows the protected version of the same event: a customer cut produced a EUR 12.25m write-down in the first half and EUR 15m of compensation in the fourth quarter, which nearly cancelled at EBITDA within the year but moved single quarters by 3 to 5 points of margin.

Beyond FY2027 the cone widens fast. FY2028 EPS: EUR 1.00 to 1.30 around EUR 1.15 (about ±13%), because price on renewed and new contracts, the pace of filling, and oral GLP-1 share all bear on that year.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C+5617th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: helpful for seeing whether the case is on track, unhelpful for seeing why it is or is not. The company reports every quarter, without adjustments, with revenue and EBITDA for both segments, the high-value share of sales, capital spending and free cash flow, and it pre-releases when results differ materially (5-Aug-2025; 8-Jul-2026). Three reports fall inside six months (10-Dec-2026, February 2027, May 2027), and each carries the one number that tests the thesis soonest: the DDS margin. A wrong base case would therefore show within three to six months. But the company gives no price and volume split, no utilisation, no GLP-1 or polymer revenue, and no amount for start-up or optimisation costs. If a margin disappoints, the disclosure cannot tell the Consumer whether the cause is price (which would break the thesis) or absorption (which delays it). Outside data partly fill the gap for the largest external risk.

What to read at each event, and the readings that would move my view.

Date What to read Supports the base or better Points to the bear case
10-Dec-2026, FY2026 results and annual report Fourth-quarter EBITDA less about EUR 15m of compensation, as a margin on revenue less EUR 15m 27% or more: the dip was costs that are ending 26% or less with DCS also weaker: a run-rate
FY2027 guidance: the growth base and the margin wording "Around 28%", or a range reaching 29% A range centred below 27.5%
Capital spending: the FY2026 actual and the FY2027 range FY2026 at EUR 140m to 145m; FY2027 to EUR 165m; a taper restated A range reaching EUR 180m, or North Carolina committed
Annual report notes: any customer above 10% of sales; joint-venture income and dividends received; purchases from and interest paid to SCHOTT AG; contract liabilities (customer prepayments); impairment testing of polymer assets An impairment indicator on polymer lines; a customer well above 10%
February 2027, first quarter FY2027 Group margin against 27.2% a year earlier (derived, company basis; the first set's "about 25%" was FactSet's basis) and 26.3% in the second half of FY2026; DDS margin against 29.4% in the third quarter Group above 28%; DDS above 31% Group below 27%; DDS below 29%
May 2027, first half FY2027 DDS margin with the write-down's lapping removed; high-value share; first-half free cash flow DDS at 31% to 32% or more; high-value share at 59% to 60% Revenue growth of 7% or more with a group margin below 27%

How to read the reported numbers in this window. Compare margins with the previous quarters on an underlying basis, not with a year earlier. The second quarter of FY2027 laps about EUR 7m of write-down, and the fourth quarter of FY2026 contains the compensation. Year-on-year margin changes will flatter the first half of FY2027 whatever the trend.

What the company does not give, in order of how much it matters. (1) Price against volume. (2) Utilisation by site or product, including polymer. (3) GLP-1 revenue and its split by format; management has declined repeated requests. (4) The size of start-up and production-optimisation costs. (5) Terms of the take-or-pay contracts and the renewal calendar. (6) Order intake or book-to-bill as a regular figure.

Where outside data help.

  • GLP-1 format and oral share. United States prescription data are published weekly and brokers summarise them monthly (Jefferies: oral share about 10% of prescriptions in the September 2026 data; injections 89.9% of prescriptions for the new obesity drugs). Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk report in early February 2027 and say which formats they are expanding. This is the largest external risk, and it can be followed at high frequency, though the link from prescriptions to SCHOTT Pharma's orders is loose and lagged.
  • Syringe capacity and price. Stevanato's fourth-quarter results (about March 2027) give its backfill, its Fishers utilisation (25% to 30% of target today) and any comment on pricing. West's results give the price contribution for high-value components (over 4 points in the third quarter of 2026). Expert calls are the only source for container prices (for example the ready-to-use 6ml vial at EUR 0.35 to 0.40 against EUR 0.75).
  • Policy and currency. The BIOSECURE list (18-Dec-2026). The euro against the dollar (about 1.12) and the Swiss franc.

Net. The cadence, the cleanliness of the numbers and the pre-release habit make the outcome easy to follow. The absence of driver disclosure makes any surprise hard to diagnose, and so hard to size for later years.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

C+5616th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the trace is clear at its two ends and unclear in the middle. From EBITDA to EPS it is as clean as any company in the sector: no adjustments, a constant 150.6m shares, negligible minorities, simple parent debt, so that EUR 10m of EBITDA is EUR 0.051 of EPS (about 5%). From what the company says to EBITDA it is weak: the company names its building blocks but puts numbers on almost none, and the one block it does quantify explains little of its margin target. The external transmission is clear in direction and rough in size: each link from prescriptions to containers to revenue to profit has a usable rule of thumb, but two links (the originators' choice of format and supplier, and SCHOTT Pharma's share of each programme) are not observable, and the effects arrive with lags of two to four quarters.

The company's statements, traced.

What the company says The driver it names Where it lands How traceable
Organic growth of 6% to 8% a year, FY2027 to FY2029 Market growth of about 9%; GLP-1 contracts of over EUR 1bn to 2030 and a EUR 700m lifetime syringe contract; the high-value share above 60% Segment revenue (DCS, DDS) Moderate. Segment revenue is reported, but there is no price, volume, GLP-1 or polymer figure, so the range cannot be rebuilt from its parts
EBITDA margin "increasing towards 30%" by FY2029 High-value mix; utilisation of new capacity; the end of ramp-up costs; an efficiency programme Segment EBITDA only Weak (see below)
Capital spending peaks in FY2027 and then tapers Completion of projects started in 2024 Capital spending; free cash flow Good on direction; no numbers beyond one year; North Carolina undecided
Efficiency savings of a "double-digit million euro" amount Digitalisation of administration and manufacturing; automated inspection Costs, not attributed to a line Weak: no baseline, phasing or cost to achieve
Dividend of 10% to 20% of earnings Cash Clear

The margin target, rebuilt from the company's own blocks. The target needs about 250 basis points from the FY2026 level of about 27.5%. Three blocks can be sized from what management has said.

  • Mix. High-value products carry about 10 points more margin than the rest (company presentations, 2026). Three more points of high-value share (57% to above 60%) is therefore worth only about 30 basis points.
  • Ramp-up costs. They run at EUR 2m to 3.5m a quarter for Hungary and Serbia (finance director, August 2025): EUR 8m to 14m a year, or 80 to 130 basis points if they disappear.
  • Efficiency. At least EUR 10m: 100 basis points or more.

The three sum to about 210 to 260 basis points, which is the target. So the target is not built on volume leverage; it is the gross sum of three stated blocks, with nothing deducted for wage inflation that is not recovered, for the start-up of the new Hungarian cartridge plant (over EUR 100m), or for any price concession. That is why our base case arrives about a year late and about one point short. It also tells the Consumer what to listen for: any quantified phasing of the efficiency programme or the ramp-up costs would be the first hard building block the company has given.

From EBITDA to EPS: the rules of thumb (FY2027 base).

  • EUR 10m of EBITDA: EUR 0.051 of EPS, about 5%.
  • One point of group organic growth (about EUR 10m of revenue): about 2.5% of EPS if it runs through built capacity, about 1.5% at the realised incremental margin of about 30%.
  • 1% of like-for-like price: about EUR 11m of EBITDA and about 5% of EPS.
  • One point of DDS margin: about EUR 4.6m and about 2.3% of EPS.
  • A 10% move in the US dollar: about 5% of EPS. A 10% rise in the Swiss franc: about -4%.
  • One point of tax rate: 1.3% of EPS. EUR 2m of depreciation or financial result: 1%.

The external chain, link by link.

  1. Patients and prescriptions (obesity and diabetes drugs; biologic approvals): observable weekly.
  2. The originator's choice of format and supplier (single-dose syringe, multi-dose cartridge, vial, pill; SCHOTT Pharma, Stevanato, Gerresheimer or BD): announced only in part. One expert puts SCHOTT Pharma at about 40% of Eli Lilly's injectable packaging, with Stevanato and Gerresheimer at about 30% each; this is one former executive's estimate and I give it low weight.
  3. Containers and their prices: a standard glass syringe at about US$0.30 to 0.45; a bulk cartridge at US$0.05 to 0.07 against US$0.50 to 0.75 ready-to-use; high-value formats at 10 to 15 times bulk.
  4. SCHOTT Pharma's revenue: EUR 10m of GLP-1 revenue is about 1% of group sales.
  5. Profit: about EUR 5m of EBITDA and about 2.4% of EPS for each EUR 10m of volume on built capacity.

The lags: orders follow use by three to four months now that customers hold little stock; prices are locked for three years; a cut for the following year is set in the customer's summer budget round (as at Stevanato); compensation under take-or-pay follows two to three quarters after the shortfall (as at SCHOTT Pharma in FY2026). The chain is therefore usable for sizing a scenario, and of little use for timing one.

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: a cautious, evidence-led raiser, but not a serial "beat-and-raise" company. It raised in FY2024 (to the upper half) and in July 2026 (mostly the compensation). The new chief executive's only raise was the July 2026 one.

  • This is closer to the Wisdom Library's "honest tracker" (raising only on hard evidence) than to "sandbagging precision" (deliberately low guides beaten exactly). The December 2025 FY2026 guide of 2% to 5% looks conservative in hindsight, which supports some conservatism in the FY2027 guide.
  • Peer contrast: GVS raises margin targets mid-year when costs come in better; Stevanato has missed and cut.

The record is mixed-to-poor, with the misses concentrated in the post-pandemic reversal, a comparable environment to a possible 2028 to 2030 overcapacity phase.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

D4439th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the record is good on revenue, capital projects and cash, mixed on near-term margins, and poor on medium-term margin promises. So the forecasts deserve different weights by line. FY2027 revenue deserves high weight (an 80% confidence range of about ±3%), leaned neutral. FY2027 EBITDA deserves moderate weight (±5%), leaned slightly down against consensus. FY2027 EPS deserves lower weight (±7% to 8%), leaned down about 5%, mainly through below-EBITDA items. FY2028 and FY2029 margins deserve low weight, leaned down 30 to 60 basis points against consensus. FY2027 free cash flow should be leaned down hard against consensus (my EUR 62m on the company definition against EUR 95m), because the company has said capital spending peaks in FY2027 and consensus has it falling. Company guidance and consensus should be leaned in opposite directions. Management behaves as an "honest tracker" and is likely to guide FY2027 conservatively and then meet or beat that guide. Consensus is likely to be trimmed modestly on EPS at the same time.

  • The record by line (three listed years, since September 2023).
  • Revenue: FY2024 at the top of raised guidance (12.1% organic). FY2025 EUR 986m against EUR 987m consensus, although sales guidance was cut in August 2025. FY2026 guided at 2% to 5% (December 2025), raised to 5% to 6% (July 2026), of which about three-quarters was the compensation payment. Third-quarter FY2026 organic growth was 8.3% against about 3.5% expected. Quarterly revenue has been met or beaten more often than missed. Weight: high.
  • Margins: FY2025 28.4% against 28.1% expected (a beat, flattered by the exceptional third quarter). FY2026 guided at about 27%, raised to 27% to 28%, and likely about 27.5% reported (27.7% underlying). The second quarter of FY2026 missed (DDS EBITDA EUR 26.9m against EUR 32.0m). The third quarter fell to 26.8%. The listing-era "low 30s" target was cut to "towards 30%" within 27 months. Weight: moderate near term, low medium term.
  • Capital projects: projects above US$100m delivered without delays (former head of process engineering). Serbia opened on time (April 2025), Lebanon inaugurated (June 2026), Hungarian syringes opened (June 2024). Capital spending has stayed inside its guided range. Weight on the FY2027 peak: high.
  • Cash: nine-month FY2026 free cash flow +49% to EUR 58.9m. The finance director's incentive now pays 30% on operating free cash flow. Weight: moderate to high on working capital and collections.
  • Below-EBITDA: the guided tax rate of about 22% for FY2026 looks to be landing nearer 23% on my derivation. The financial result improved as promised. Weight: moderate. Lean the tax rate slightly up.
  • Consensus revisions show the same thing. FY2027 consensus EPS is down 17% over twelve months, EBITDA down 12% and sales down 7%. Revenue estimates have been the most reliable and EPS the least. Since the February 2026 trough, EPS estimates have risen only 2% to 3%, while target prices rose 21% (three months) and 32% (six months). The upgrade has been in the multiple, not in the numbers.
  • Comparable conditions. In the FY2023 to FY2025 destocking and vaccine reversal, the company held its margin while missing on growth, until a single customer's cut broke both. In the 2027 to 2030 phase the pattern is likely to flip: revenue near plan, because demand and lock-in are robust, and margin below plan, because of price on new volume and the absorption of new capacity. That is why I lean margins, not revenue.
  • Reported optics that will distort the record in the window. My quarterly path gives reported revenue growth of +6.5%, +10.0%, +4.5% and +1.5% in the four quarters of FY2027. The EBITDA margin compares with a year earlier at about flat, +180, +200 and -260 basis points: the second and third quarters lap the write-down and the third quarter's dip, and the fourth laps the compensation. The results in May and August 2027 will look strong, and the FY2027 results in December 2027 will look weak, with no change in the underlying trend. The fourth set should expect a narrative swing around the December 2027 results that this calendar causes on its own.
  • How much weight overall. Weight the 18-month forecasts at about 60% to 65% (moderately predictable), carried by revenue visibility and a clean, unadjusted earnings base. Weight the FY2029 to FY2030 margin and EPS forecasts at about 40%. They depend on price on new contracts in an overcapacity phase, which no record in the files can test.
  • My 80% ranges. FY2027 revenue EUR 1,065m to 1,130m. EBITDA EUR 290m to 320m. EPS EUR 0.96 to 1.12 (base 1.04). FY2028 EPS EUR 1.00 to 1.30 (base 1.15). The probability-weighted outcome equals my base. Consensus FY2027 EPS (EUR 1.10) sits near the 80th percentile of my FY2027 range. Consensus FY2028 EPS (EUR 1.22) sits at about the 70th percentile of my FY2028 range.

Forecasts with good growth rates and higher growth/margins than past

7a

Growth rate

4 answers
Judgement47C-4928P-0862P-1048
Group judgement

Financial forecasts

D4735th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts describe a business whose revenue is highly predictable and whose earnings and cash hinge on a few internal mechanics. The most important is how much of the next four years' growth flows through capacity already built. Demand, lock-in and the end of destocking support 6% to 7% underlying growth. Price is not a lever: it is flat in the base case, and it is the main downside risk because it drops through at close to 100%. The realised incremental margin has averaged about a third since FY2022, so the EBITDA margin rises slowly (about 40 to 50 basis points a year, to about 29% by FY2029 and 29.3% by FY2030). At EBIT, rising depreciation absorbs most of the gain until FY2029. EPS growth therefore resumes at a moderate 7% (FY2027), accelerating to about 10% (FY2028 to FY2030) as depreciation growth slows and interest falls. The real step change is cash. Free cash flow roughly triples by FY2029 as capital spending passes its FY2027 peak, and the balance sheet turns to net cash, which raises the question of what the company and its parent will do with it. Against consensus, my revenue is in line, my EBITDA 1% to 3% lower, my EPS about 5% lower in FY2027 to FY2028, and my free cash flow lower in FY2027 to FY2028 and higher in FY2029. Consensus has the right shape but runs about a year early on margin and misses the capital-spending peak. For the third set: the drivers are concentrated (the incremental margin, like-for-like price, the DDS recovery), and the leverage is asymmetric (volume misses cost more than volume gains earn). The 18-month cone is therefore moderately wide and tilted down at EPS, though not at revenue. For the fourth set: company guidance on 10-Dec-2026 is likely to be beatable, while consensus EPS is likely to be trimmed by a few percent. The reported quarterly profile will flatter May and August 2027 and disappoint in December 2027, independent of the underlying trend.

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?F2844th of 54full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B6213th of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the items below operating profit turn from a headwind into a modest tailwind. From FY2023 to FY2025 they absorbed all of the EBITDA growth. Depreciation rose EUR 33m, the financial charge rose to about EUR 13m and the tax rate rose with Pillar Two, which is why EPS stayed flat at about EUR 1.00. From FY2026 to FY2030 the financial result improves by about EUR 12m (EUR 0.06 of EPS), the tax rate is stable at about 22.5% on the full pre-tax basis, depreciation growth slows from 14% (FY2026) to about 4.5% (FY2030), and the share count stays at 150.6m. Net income grows from EUR 146m to about EUR 210m (FY2030), a 7.5% compound rate, against close to zero from FY2023 to FY2025. The larger change is in cash. Free cash flow conversion rises from about 25% of net income (FY2025, company definition) to about 70% (FY2030), because capital spending falls below EUR 140m while depreciation keeps rising. The FY2026 one-offs net to about EUR +2m at EBITDA and do not recur. There will be no buybacks and no issuance.

  • Joint-venture income (inside EBITDA; a minority investment). SCHOTT Pharma India (50% owned; TPG bought 35% from the Serum Institute in 2025) had FY2025 revenue of EUR 113.5m and net income of EUR 23.96m, a 21% net margin. SCHOTT Pharma's share was EUR 11.98m, plus EUR 2.06m from the Italian joint venture (Empha). India is adding prefillable syringe and cartridge capacity, with local tubing supply from SCHOTT Glass India in Gujarat. I model the joint-venture share at about EUR 15m (FY2026) rising about 8% a year to about EUR 21m (FY2030). That is about 1.4 to 1.6 points of margin with no revenue and no consolidated capital spending. It is non-cash except for dividends, so I deduct about EUR 9m to 13m a year in the cash flow. Joint-venture income sits wholly in DCS, so DCS margins look about 2.5 points stronger than its own operations.
  • Tax. 23.0% (FY2026) and then 22.5%, on pre-tax profit including joint-venture income. The Pillar Two floor (15% minimum) and the country mix (Switzerland, Hungary, Serbia, Germany, United States) set the level. Growing joint-venture income, which is post-tax, pulls the blended rate down slightly. Each point is about EUR 2m of net income and 1.3% of EPS. Consensus is at about 22%, close enough that tax is not a source of disagreement.
  • Interest. EUR -8.5m, -7.5m, -5.5m, -3.0m, -1.0m (P-02). Against the history: FactSet net interest went from EUR -1.4m (FY2021) to EUR -19.7m (FY2025) on FactSet's line, or about EUR -13m on the company basis. The FY2026 to FY2030 improvement adds about EUR 0.06 to EPS, a third of the EPS growth that EBIT does not deliver in FY2026 to FY2027.
  • One-offs. FY2026: inventory write-down of EUR 12.25m in cost of sales (first half) and compensation of about EUR 15m in revenue (fourth quarter). Net about EUR +2m at EBITDA, about +1.5 points of revenue growth, +EUR 0.01 of EPS. None is assumed later. Unmodelled risks: an impairment of polymer lines (book value not disclosed; probability about 10%), a further take-or-pay settlement (either way), and currency derivatives swinging the financial result by EUR 2m to 4m (no hedge accounting). The company adjusts nothing, so every such item shows in reported EPS, and consensus EPS is on the same unadjusted basis. That makes the reported EPS line noisier than most peers', but it does not leave a hidden gap between adjusted and reported earnings.
  • Minorities. About EUR 0.3m to 0.4m a year. Negligible.
  • Share count, buybacks, issuance. Constant at 150.6m since the listing. Long-term incentives are virtual shares settled in cash, so there is no dilution. The KGaA form, the 77% parent holding and an 18% to 23% free float make a buyback very unlikely: it would shrink the float that already makes the stock "nearly un-investable for many large institutions" (Deutsche Bank). The GVS model is the only route in the files to a buyback that adds to EPS without cutting the float: a tender in which the controlling shareholder does not take part (12.29% of capital, EUR 100m). Nothing similar is signalled. Base case: zero.
  • Dividends. A 10% to 20% payout; FY2025 EUR 0.18, 18.5%. I model EUR 0.18 (FY2026; EPS flat, so a hold is likelier than a rise), 0.19, 0.21, 0.23 and 0.25, about 18%. Cash dividends follow one year later (paid in February), EUR 27m to 35m a year. The FactSet dividend line here dates the dividend by the year it is declared for (FY2025 EUR 0.18). Cash paid in FY2025 (EUR 24.1m) was the FY2024 dividend.
  • Free cash flow, against history. Company definition (operating cash flow less capital spending): about EUR 21m, 37m, 30m, 7m, 80m and 37m in FY2020 to FY2025, an average of about EUR 35m, or 26% of net income. My FY2026 to FY2030: EUR 65m, 62m, 102m, 136m and 158m before leases; EUR 58m, 54m, 94m, 127m and 149m after leases. Against consensus (EUR 69m, 95m, 125m, 129m): close in FY2026, about EUR 33m below in FY2027 (capital spending EUR 165m against EUR 149m; operating cash flow about EUR 227m against EUR 241m), about EUR 23m below in FY2028, and above from FY2029 once the taper passes the consensus assumption of a flat EUR 153m. The cash profile is back-loaded, not lower: cumulative FY2026 to FY2029 free cash flow is close to consensus, but the years are in a different order.
  • What would move these items materially. Committing North Carolina (FY2029 to FY2030 free cash flow down EUR 40m to 60m a year). A parent re-pricing of tubing at the December 2027 renewal (10% is about EUR 15m of EBITDA, about -7% EPS). A 10% Swiss franc rise (about -3% EBITDA, about -4% EPS; net short CHF 135m). A 10% dollar fall (about -5% EPS, translation unhedged).

Scenarios (FY2028 EPS; probability).

  • Bear, EUR 0.98 to 1.02 (25%). Eli Lilly or another GLP-1 originator cuts syringe volume, or shifts format or supplier, by 30% to 50%: about -2.5% to -4% of revenue, partly compensated. Syringe and ready-to-use vial renewals fall 2% a year from FY2028. The DDS margin stays near 29% to 30%. The group margin is about 27.3%.
  • Base, EUR 1.15 (50%). As above.
  • Bull, EUR 1.27 to 1.31 (25%). The first-half FY2027 DDS margin is back above 32%. Polymer redeployment works. Gerresheimer share gains add about 0.5 points of growth. The dollar stays at about 1.12. The FY2028 margin is about 29.4%, close to consensus.
  • The probability-weighted FY2028 EPS is about EUR 1.15. Consensus (EUR 1.22) needs the bull drivers to be about half-delivered. For the fourth set: consensus is not out of reach, but it prices more than the base case, in the year (FY2028) when the overcapacity and orals debates also peak.

What the base case most depends on, in order. (1) The underlying margin exit rate from the third quarter of FY2026 (26.8%): trough or run-rate. (2) No GLP-1 volume shock at Eli Lilly, where SCHOTT Pharma has about 8% of group sales. (3) The FY2027 capital-spending peak at about EUR 165m, then a real taper, with North Carolina not committed. (4) Flat, not falling, like-for-like price on syringe and ready-to-use vial renewals in 2027 to 2028. (5) Parent contracts (tubing, facilities) rolled in December 2027 on similar terms.

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

D6428th of 54

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: plausible scope is about 2 to 3 points of EBITDA margin by FY2029 (from about 27.5% underlying in FY2026 to 29.5% to 30%) and about EUR 100m of extra annual free cash flow by FY2029. It comes from a few big areas: filling Hungarian syringes and cartridges, St. Gallen cartridges, Lebanon ready-to-use vials and Müllheim specialty lines; redeploying polymer lines; tapering capital spending; and the mix toward ready-to-use products. It is congruent with the strategy. Peer precedent shows it is achievable but often late.

  • Bridge, my estimate (underlying FY2026 EBITDA about EUR 282m to consensus FY2029 EUR 367m, +EUR 85m on about +EUR 228m of revenue, a 37% incremental margin):
  • volume through existing capacity, about EUR 60m to 70m (about EUR 130m to 140m of volume revenue at a 45% to 55% contribution);
  • mix to ready-to-use (high-value share from 57% to above 60%), about EUR 15m to 20m;
  • efficiency programme net, about EUR 10m;
  • start-up costs fading, about EUR 5m to 8m;
  • less like-for-like price and wage drift, about EUR -10m to -20m.
  • Precedents: West expanded margins through high-value mix with full plants from 2016 to 2021. GVS lifted its EBITDA margin from 20.4% (FY2022) to 25.2% (FY2025) through footprint consolidation and insourcing. Stevanato missed its 30% target on schedule because its Engineering business and validation timelines slipped. SCHOTT Pharma itself reached 28.4% in FY2025.
  • Timing risk: customer validations can take 6 to 24 months; Stevanato suffered a two-year validation delay on new capacity. Treat the FY2029 target as reached in FY2030 in a cautious case.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

D4240th of 54

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: yes, growth has decelerated below the company's normal rate. Trailing twelve-month organic growth is about 4.5% to 5%, against an old "normal" of about 10% (listing promise; Reisse's five-year average) and a new target of 6% to 8%. But the input force has not slowed: injectables packaging grows about 9%, GLP-1 double digits, ready-to-use cartridges about 20%. The deceleration comes from the company's mix (vaccines, mRNA polymer, the late-2025 syringe customer cut). It is now reversing, with third-quarter growth of 8.3%.

  • Path: organic 12.1% (FY2024), 5.8% (FY2025), 2.3% (first half FY2026), 8.3% (third quarter FY2026), 4.4% (nine months FY2026).
  • Segment split: DCS accelerated (+9.2% nine months; +11% third quarter); DDS decelerated (-1.7% nine months; +5% third quarter after -9.8% in the second).
  • Input force: biologics and injectable drug volumes keep growing; destocking is over. The one input force that is slowing is the GLP-1 syringe sub-segment's price and supplier concentration (Stevanato's cut). That does not show in SCHOTT Pharma's numbers yet.
  • Model implication: FY2027 underlying organic growth of 6.5% to 7.5% is consistent with the input force once the vaccine and polymer drag fades. Reported FY2027 growth is about 1.5 points lower because of the compensation base.
