D5033rd of 54

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the next four years look like a slower, cleaner version of FY2020 to FY2025 on revenue, a modest improvement on EBITDA margin, a return to EPS growth after four flat years, and a step change in free cash flow. Revenue compounds at about 6% a year (against 11% in FY2020 to FY2025 with the pandemic, and 6.3% in FY2022 to FY2025). The EBITDA margin rises from about 27.7% underlying to about 29.3% by FY2030 (against 22.5% to 28.4% in FY2020 to FY2025). EPS compounds at about 7.6% (against flat in FY2023 to FY2025), with growth accelerating to about 10% a year in FY2028 to FY2030 as depreciation growth slows and interest falls. Free cash flow roughly triples, from an average of about EUR 35m to an average of about EUR 105m (company definition). The drivers of the difference against history are concentrated in three: absorption of capacity already built, the capital-spending taper, and the absence of further negative mix shocks (mRNA, vaccines, single-customer cuts). Price adds nothing. The case is therefore an execution-and-cash case, not a growth or pricing case.

  • How concentrated the drivers are (for D-03). Of the EUR +0.43 EPS gain from FY2025 to FY2030, about 60% comes from EBITDA growth net of depreciation (volume through built capacity and mix about EUR 0.30; efficiency about EUR 0.05; joint-venture growth about EUR 0.03; less wage drift and start-up costs). About 15% comes from the falling financial charge. The rest comes from revenue growth at a constant margin. The single largest swing factor is the realised incremental margin: each 5 points on about EUR 335m of extra revenue is about EUR 17m of FY2030 EBITDA, or EUR 0.09 of EPS. After that come like-for-like price (each 1% is EUR 0.055) and the DDS margin recovery (each point of DDS margin is EUR 4.5m to 5m, or EUR 0.025). The forecasts therefore rest on two or three drivers, not many: concentrated, not diffuse.
  • Where I differ from the first set. (1) Its FY2027 EBITDA of EUR 295m to 305m (27.0% to 27.6%) looks too low. The two one-offs net to about zero at EBITDA, the FY2026 underlying base is about 27.7%, and the realised incremental margin supports +20 to 30 basis points even after start-up costs. I am at EUR 306m (27.9%). The EPS gap to consensus comes mostly from the financial result and depreciation, not EBITDA. (2) Its FY2028 EPS of EUR 1.15 to 1.20 is consistent with mine (EUR 1.15). (3) Its 45% to 55% operating-leverage sensitivity is right for downside volume errors but overstates the upside from volume that needs new capacity (P-01). (4) The first set read UBS's low EBITDA as a sign that consensus will drift down. Most of that gap is a basis difference (note 2.4). The drift is real but smaller, and it is in EPS.
  • Where I differ from consensus. Revenue: about 0.5% below in FY2027 and in line after. EBITDA: about -1% (FY2027), -3% (FY2028), -2% (FY2029). EPS: -5% (FY2027), -6% (FY2028), -4% (FY2029). Free cash flow: well below in FY2027 and FY2028, above in FY2029. Consensus has the right shape but runs about a year early on margin, and it misses the FY2027 capital-spending peak.

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

D4426th of 54

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Our assessment is, for the next 18 months, largely reflected already. Guidance will probably be set where our base case sits. The informed sell-side is at our numbers, and the stale median above them should drift down by 2% to 4%. The narrative knows the near-term mechanics and the GLP-1 risk. The investment case therefore does not rest on a gap between what we see and what the market sees over the next six months. What is not reflected is dated for months 12 to 18 and beyond: the fall in capital spending, the rise in free cash flow and the return of EPS growth to about 10% a year from FY2028. That is a favourable gap, but a distant one, and it is exposed to the single-customer and price risks that fall in the same period.

By period.

  • Next 6 months (to May 2027). Guidance: FY2026 delivered in the upper half; FY2027 guided at about our base, which will read as a little soft against the consensus margin and above consensus on capital spending. Estimates: small cuts to FY2027 and FY2028 EPS; a larger cut to FY2027 free cash flow; target prices at risk of converging on EUR 22 to 24. Narrative: no dated positive of the company's own; exposure to GLP-1 news that the company does not control. The gap is neutral to slightly unfavourable.
  • Months 6 to 18 (May 2027 to May 2028). Guidance met, with about a one-in-three chance of a margin raise in the summer of 2027. Estimates supported by flattering first-half figures, then tested in December 2027. The first clean guide (FY2028), with lower capital spending, is the first occasion on which guidance, estimates and narrative could all move up together. The gap is mildly favourable if FY2027 is delivered.
  • 18 months to three years. Consensus earnings look half a point to a point too high on margin. Consensus free cash flow looks EUR 15m to 20m too low for FY2029. The narrative has not begun to discuss what a net-cash balance sheet under a 77% owner will be used for.

Two steers for the memo's fair-multiple and return topics. These go beyond the questions asked, but the Consumer will need them.

  1. The 18-month return in the model (+45.8%) is about half multiple. It assumes a move from 18.9x to the guideline's 23.32x. (The 18.9x is on the model's own EPS; on consensus EPS the stock is at 17.4x to 17.6x.) The guideline multiple is above the 75th percentile of the stock's five-year range (22.6x) and above Deutsche Bank's 22x target multiple. On my reading of the phase (a recovery that is turning up, with estimates drifting slightly down, guidance met and not raised, and the sell-side split at 46% buys), the evidence that would justify a top-quartile multiple cannot be in hand before the first-half results of May 2027, and more probably not before December 2027. At an unchanged multiple the same forecasts give an 18-month return of about +18% to +19%. At 13.5x, the 10th percentile of the five-year range, the same forward EPS gives a price of about EUR 17.6 at the horizon, a loss of about 15% after dividends.
  2. The earnings part of that return (+17%) is a statement about FY2029 and FY2030. A two-year-forward multiple applied at 31-Dec-2027 capitalises the EPS of calendar 2029 (about EUR 1.31, mostly FY2029's EUR 1.28 with a quarter of FY2030's EUR 1.40). The second set gives its forecasts for those years a weight of about 40%, and they depend on price on new contracts in an over-supplied market. So the 18-month return is only as predictable as the three-year forecast. The 18-month forecast of FY2027 itself, which the third set finds moderately predictable, enters the return only through what it proves about the later years.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

C-5221st of 54

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: guidance is most likely to be met at every one of the seven earnings events in the next 18 months. It is likely to be raised at most once, on margin, at the third-quarter stage in July or August 2027 (about a one-in-three chance). The chance of a cut at some point in FY2027 is about 15% to 20%. The event that matters is the first: on 10-Dec-2026 I expect FY2027 guidance of 5% to 7% organic growth on the reported FY2026 base and a margin of 27% to 28% or "around 28%". That is in line with consensus revenue, slightly below the consensus margin, and it comes with a capital-spending range above consensus. Our base case sits inside that guidance, in the lower half on growth and at the top on margin. So, on our numbers, the guide is conservative on margin only; on growth it is one to be met, not beaten. The medium-term targets will be kept: the growth range because our forecasts sit at its lower edge, the margin target because "increasing towards 30%" is elastic enough to survive our 28.9%. Neither will be raised.

December 2027: FY2027 result, and the first FY2028 guide FY2027 inside the final range; FY2028 guide of 6% to 8% growth, a higher margin and lower capital spending FY2028 guide above the then consensus: about 15% About 55% Below: about 30%
February and May 2028 Confirmed 5% 85% 10%

Why "met" dominates.

  • The company's behaviour. It raises only on hard evidence. It declined to raise after the first-quarter FY2026 beat because extra shifts had flattered it. It raised in July 2026 only once the settlement was signed. Its December 2025 guide proved conservative.
  • The cover. Order visibility already reaches into FY2027 in both segments, and prices are locked for three years, so only about a third of contracts reprice in any one year.
  • The record. In each fiscal year since the listing the outcome has landed inside or above the margin range first guided (D-04).

What would drive a raise. A DDS margin back above 32% in the first half; polymer work outside mRNA qualifying faster; the production-optimisation costs ending as management said; a stronger dollar (growth is guided at constant currency, the margin is not). A raise on growth is unlikely even in a good year, because of the shape of the comparisons. On the second set's quarterly path, reported growth is about 8% in the first half of FY2027 and about 3% in the second, when the third quarter's 8.3% and the fourth quarter's compensation are lapped. Expect a first half that runs above the full-year range with no raise. The Consumer should not read that as sandbagging; the arithmetic forces it.

What would drive a cut. A change of format, volume or supplier at a GLP-1 originator, announced for the following year (Stevanato's was set in the customer's summer budget round, so a cut for FY2028 would most likely surface between July and December 2027). A first-half margin that shows the second half of FY2026 was a run-rate. Start-up costs at the Hungarian cartridge plant above plan. A sharp fall in the dollar or rise in the Swiss franc, for the margin.

December 2027 deserves separate attention. It joins a weak-looking fourth quarter (reported growth of about 1.5% and a margin about 260 basis points below a year earlier, both caused by the compensation in the base) to the first guide on a clean base: growth inside 6% to 8%, a rising margin, and the first year of lower capital spending. If FY2027 has gone to plan, this is the first guidance day since the listing that can show acceleration on every line. It is also the date on which a FY2028 customer cut or price pressure on renewals would have to be disclosed, and the month in which the parent's tubing agreement and credit facilities reach their terms.

Targets for 18 months to three years out.

  • Growth of 6% to 8% a year, FY2027 to FY2029: raised 5%; kept 75%; lowered 20%. Our 7.0%, 6.5% and 6.5% underlying sits in the lower half. A first-year guide of 5% to 7% will be presented as the base effect, not as a change to the target. Only a GLP-1 event would force a cut.
  • Margin "increasing towards 30%" by FY2029: raised under 5%; kept about 80%; softened or deferred about 15% to 20%. We forecast 28.9%. The wording lets the company claim delivery at 29%. That elasticity lowers the risk of a formal cut. It also means the target cannot be treated as a commitment to 30%.
  • A new capital markets day or new targets under the new chief executive: possible in 2027; nothing in the files signals one. If it came, I would expect a free-cash-flow or capital-spending ambition to be added, since management is now paid on operating free cash flow, and not higher growth or margin targets.

How this compares with our assessment. For FY2027 the guidance I expect and our base case nearly coincide, so there is little gap either way. On the medium-term margin, the company's ambition is above our forecast by about a point and a year. What guidance will not reflect until December 2027 is the part of our assessment that is better than the past: the fall in capital spending and the rise in free cash flow from FY2028.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

C-4025th of 54

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: consensus EPS is more likely to be trimmed than raised at the next two events, and the trims will be small. I expect the FY2027 median to move from EUR 1.10 to about EUR 1.06 to 1.08 by February 2027 (-2% to -4%), and FY2028 from EUR 1.22 to about EUR 1.18 to 1.20 over the next twelve months. Revenue estimates should hold. EBITDA should slip 1% to 2%. FY2027 free cash flow (EUR 95m) should be cut by a quarter or more. The net breadth of revisions should be negative from December 2026 to February 2027, flat to positive from May to August 2027 when the reported comparisons flatter, and at risk again in December 2027. The reason the trims are small is the important point: the best-informed brokers are already at our numbers. The gap is between a stale median and the fresh estimates. It is not a gap between the sell-side and us, and nowhere on the profit-and-loss account are we above consensus.

Consensus is two groups of brokers.

FY2027 EPS (EUR) FY2028 EPS (EUR)
Our base (the model) 1.05 1.15
The three houses with the deepest published work: UBS (6-Nov-26), Deutsche Bank (8-Oct-26), Jefferies (12-Aug-26) 1.04, 1.05, 1.06 1.15, 1.18, 1.19
Other estimates dated since 1 October: RBC (4-Nov-26), Stifel (12-Oct-26) 1.13, 1.10 1.23, 1.20
Estimates dated August and September: Barclays, EQUI.TS, Oddo BHF and two unnamed brokers 1.07 to 1.16 1.25 to 1.31
FactSet median (ten brokers) 1.10 1.22

Six of the ten estimates predate October. Deutsche Bank cut its FY2027 EPS by 4% in October while FactSet's consensus did not move in the month. The median will fall as the older estimates are refreshed after 10-Dec-2026, without any new disappointment being needed.

Where the gap lies, line by line (FY2027; consensus against our base).

  • Revenue: EUR 1,104m against EUR 1,099m. No gap. Currency is the swing.
  • EBITDA: EUR 310m against EUR 307m; a margin of 28.1% against 27.9%. A guide of 27% to 28% would pull the median to about EUR 305m to 307m.
  • Depreciation: EUR 95m against EUR 96m. No gap.
  • Financial result: consensus implies about EUR -4m (EBIT of EUR 217m against pre-tax profit of EUR 213m); we have EUR -7.5m, which is what net debt of about EUR 90m and the nine-month run-rate (EUR -6.2m) support. This is worth about EUR 0.02 of EPS, and most brokers will correct it only when they rebuild their models after the annual report.
  • Capital spending and free cash flow: EUR 149m and EUR 95m, against our EUR 165m and EUR 54m to 62m. About half the free-cash-flow gap is capital spending. The rest is operating cash flow (joint-venture income not received in cash, and working capital).

Why I do not expect larger cuts. Revenue visibility is high. The guide should bracket consensus EBITDA. The lines below EBITDA are mechanical. FY2026 itself should land on consensus (EUR 0.98 against our EUR 0.97), and FY2026 free cash flow may beat it (consensus assumes capital spending of EUR 156m; nine-month spending was EUR 82.5m).

Why I do not expect upgrades. They would need a first-half FY2027 DDS margin above 32% and a group margin above 28%, which is our bull case (about 25%). Even then consensus would be confirmed, not raised, since it already assumes 28.1% and 29.0%.

My probabilities.

Ratings and targets. The buy share is 46%, down 23 points in a month. The mean target is EUR 25.0, 18% above the price. Targets rose 21% in three months and 32% in six while FY2027 EPS rose 2% to 3%: the upgrade cycle was in multiples, and it has stopped (Deutsche Bank moved to Hold on valuation on 9-Oct-2026; UBS's target is EUR 22.30 as of 6-Nov-2026). The dashboard's sell-side momentum rating of A blends these parts. Its earnings part is about to turn slightly negative and its target part has no further rise in view, so I expect the rating to fade over the next three months. A cluster of target cuts toward EUR 22 to 24 after a guide that reads soft is the main risk to the share price from the sell-side in the next six months.

Consensus for 18 months to three years out.

  • FY2029 EPS (EUR 1.32, only four brokers; ours is EUR 1.28): held or trimmed by 2% to 3%. Lower about 50%, held 40%, higher 10%.
  • FY2028 and FY2029 margins (29.0% and 29.3%, against our 28.4% and 28.9%): the same half point to a point of excess, which will be tested only by FY2027 delivery.
  • FY2028 and FY2029 free cash flow (EUR 125m and EUR 129m): this is the one line where I see upgrades. Consensus holds capital spending flat at EUR 153m in both years. If the company confirms a taper in December 2027 (we have EUR 145m and EUR 135m), consensus free cash flow for FY2029 rises by EUR 15m to 20m. Our FY2029 figure (EUR 127m, or EUR 136m before lease payments) is already level with consensus despite lower earnings. I put about a 50% chance on this upgrade by December 2027. North Carolina is the reason it is not higher.

11d

Current multiple

–not yet ratedMemo topic 11, : the Rater's score1 answer
D-0755
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C+5514th of 54

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: day to day the share price is overwhelmingly its own. Over the latest six months the peer basket explains 12% of the daily price variance and 88% is specific to the stock; over 24 months the explained share has never exceeded 23%. The multiple is company-driven in its trend and industry-driven in its shocks. About nine-tenths of the six-month rise of 34% was a higher multiple, earned on company events while the basket rose 6%. The one sharp fall, in November 2026, came from an industry event at a peer that is not in the basket. The co-movement is therefore asymmetric: low when the news is the company's own, high when the news is about GLP-1 packaging. The stock gets full credit for its own delivery and no shelter from the industry's shocks.

The measurements (the co-movement table, daily returns, windows of 126 trading days).

  • Latest window (21-May to 12-Nov-2026): correlation with the equal-weighted basket of 12 dashboard peers 0.35; beta 0.84; R-squared 12%. Six-month return +34.3% against +6.3% for the basket.
  • Over 24 month-ends, R-squared has ranged from 8% to 23% and correlation from 0.28 to 0.48. There is no trend toward closer co-movement.
  • Beta has risen, from about 0.5 to 0.6 in early 2026 to 0.84 to 0.95 since August 2026. The stock now moves further for a given sector move than it did, without being better explained by the sector.
  • No single peer explains more than 12%. The highest correlations are with Siegfried (0.34), Sartorius Stedim (0.30), Lonza (0.27) and Bachem (0.26). Gerresheimer, the only containment maker in the basket, is at 0.14, because Gerresheimer trades on its own balance-sheet and regulatory events.

A caveat that changes the reading. The basket is the dashboard's sector (contract manufacturers and pharma suppliers listed in Europe). It leaves out the true industry peers: Stevanato and West, which are listed in the United States, and BD and Ypsomed. The table therefore measures co-movement with the sector, and understates co-movement with the industry. The event evidence fills the gap: when Stevanato fell 28% on 3-Nov-2026, SCHOTT Pharma fell from about EUR 25 to 26 to about EUR 21, some 15% to 18%, with no news of its own.

The multiple. The 24-month-forward P/E went from 11.5x (the February 2026 low) to 20.8x (22-Oct-2026) and back to 17.4x. Over the same six months consensus EPS rose 2% to 3%. The moves in the multiple line up with company events (the guidance reset of December 2025; the pre-release and customer settlement of July 2026; Deutsche Bank's upgrade and later downgrade) until November, when the industry event took about three points of multiple off. Multiples across the dashboard sector run from 10x (Gerresheimer) to 33x (Sartorius Stedim), each near an extreme of its own history. There is no sector multiple to which this stock is tied.

Why it behaves this way. A free float of 18% to 23%, about US$4m to 6m traded a day, and ten brokers. My inference is that little of the trading comes from sector or index funds, so sector flows barely reach the stock, while a single institution acting on company or theme news moves it far.

What it means for the case.

  • The multiple will follow the company's own evidence: the FY2027 guide, the DDS margin, the capital-spending taper. It will not be lifted by a sector re-rating.
  • Peer shocks on the GLP-1 theme pass through fully, whatever the read-across to fundamentals; I argue in D-01 that the fundamental read-across from Stevanato was close to neutral.
  • For the fair-multiple discussion in the memo, anchor on the stock's own phase and its own range. Peer multiples say little.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

D4238th of 54

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: in pharma packaging, outperformance has come from a rising high-value mix with full plants: West from 2015 to 2021, and SCHOTT Pharma's own FY2020 to FY2022 run (revenue +18% a year, margins +7 points, return on capital employed of 22% to 25%). Underperformance has come from three repeating patterns: a capital-spending peak that runs ahead of utilisation, a single-customer volume shock, and leverage or governance failure. SCHOTT Pharma currently fits the first two of the three underperformance patterns and none of the outperformance ones. Its share price has already de-rated for them twice, in December 2025 and November 2026. Its clean balance sheet and governance exclude the third.

  • Capital-spending peak before utilisation: Stevanato spent 42% of sales on capital (2023) and its return on invested capital fell to 7.4% (2024). It missed its 30% margin target on schedule, and its Fishers plant sits at 50% occupancy after more than four years. SCHOTT Pharma has spent 14.7% to 20% of sales on capital since FY2022, and its EBIT has barely moved since FY2022 (EUR 162m to EUR 176m, FactSet basis). It fits, in a milder form.
  • Single-customer shock: SCHOTT Pharma (late 2025 glass syringe customer: FY2026 guidance cut, shares down 35% to 44% over twelve months); Stevanato (November 2026 GLP-1 customer: -28% in a day). The Sector Digest rule: "long supply contracts protect against a customer switching supplier, not against it ordering less."
  • Leverage and governance: Gerresheimer (4.95x leverage, bill-and-hold probe, results delayed, stock halved). SCHOTT Pharma is the cleanest in the group.
  • Re-rating mechanics: "Slow growth is punished twice" (Wisdom Library): medtech consumables de-rate on growth falling below about 8% to 10%. SCHOTT Pharma's P/E multiple fell from listing levels around 25x to 30x to an 11.5x low in the trough. Re-rating needs evidence that margins expand with volume (operating leverage proven) more than headline growth. Deutsche Bank raised its target P/E to 22x in October 2026 on "more reliable delivery".
  • What would make SCHOTT Pharma fit the outperformance pattern: two or three quarters in which revenue growth of 6% to 8% comes with rising DDS and group margins and falling capital intensity. Only then do the share price's past patterns support a sustained re-rating.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

C-5023rd of 54

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: to a fair trough of about EUR 15 to 16.5, a fall of about 22% to 30% from EUR 21.15 to 21.40. To about EUR 12.5 to 13 (about -40%) in a repeat of the December 2025 to February 2026 trough. Jefferies' extreme bear case of EUR 10.60 is about -50%.

  • Valuation now: 17.4x to 17.6x 24-month-forward P/E (on EPS of EUR 1.23), the 34th to 38th percentile of its own history. The five-year range is 11.5x to 29.2x (median 18.9x); the 10th percentile is 13.5x (the dashboard's "crash" band is 13.6x). EV/EBITDA is about 11.5x FY2026 and 10.6x FY2027 (EV EUR 3,277m).
  • Scenarios (P/E × 24-month-forward EPS):
  • Sentiment only, EPS intact: 13.5x × 1.23 = about EUR 16.6 (-22%).
  • Fundamental hit (EPS -10% to about EUR 1.11, a soft FY2027 plus a GLP-1 or price setback) at 13.5x: about EUR 15.0 (-30%).
  • Repeat trough: 11.5x × 1.11 = about EUR 12.8 (-40%); the 52-week low was EUR 12.62 to 12.96.
  • Cross-checks: Jefferies' DCF target of EUR 17.30 (EUR 15.50 in January 2026, implying 8.9x FY2026 EV/EBITDA); Deutsche Bank's target of EUR 16 on 15x P/E in February 2026, at the trough of credibility. Upside reference: the median target of EUR 25.00 (+18%), and Deutsche Bank's 22x target multiple.
  • Skew: downside to fair trough (-25%) is larger than upside to targets (+18%) over 12 months unless FY2027 guidance and first-half delivery confirm margin recovery.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

F3644th of 54

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The market narrative has moved twice in twelve months. In the first half of 2026 it went from "post-listing credibility crisis" to "past the trough, beat and raise", lifting the shares about 70% from the low. In November 2026 it went to "GLP-1 contract risk across the group", taking about 15% to 18% back. The next leg depends on dated evidence that SCHOTT Pharma converts volume into margin: the FY2027 guide (10-Dec-2026), the first-half FY2027 DDS margin, and the capital-spending peak followed by a taper. There are few novel positives inside 18 months to pull the narrative forward. The stock therefore depends on delivery, not on a catalyst. At 17.4x forward earnings, the 38th percentile of its own range, it prices a modest recovery, not proof. The narrative's upside requires the operating-leverage story to be visibly true by mid-2027; its downside comes from sector shocks the company does not control.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–66

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two sets are congruent on everything that carries the case, and they jar in seven places that I can name. None of the seven overturns the base case. Taken together they move my reading in three directions. Price risk inside FY2027 is smaller than both sets imply. The margin at which the company leaves FY2026 is lower than the second set's "flat for two years" suggests. Exposure to the largest customer is larger than either set states. A case whose parts agree this well, and whose disagreements are about size and timing and not about direction, is more predictable than its headline numbers alone would show.

What aligns, and why that matters. Both sets, and all ten AlphaSense reports written since, rest on one account of the business. Revenue is close to an annuity: 97% repeat revenue, containers written into drug registrations, contracts of three to five years with prices locked for three. Earnings depend on one transmission: whether capacity already built fills, and at what price. The same account explains each of the following, which is the test of congruence.

  • Four flat years of EPS (about EUR 1.00 since FY2023) while revenue grew 16%.
  • A gross margin down from 36.0% (FY2022) to about 31% to 33%.
  • Guidance and targets cut in December 2025.
  • A strong franchise score in the first set (competitive advantage 60) beside weak scores for pricing power (38 to 40), for the record on promises (42) and for the financial forecasts (47).
  • The second set's corrections to the first (the financial result of about EUR -13m, the two tax bases, the definition of free cash flow, UBS's EBITDA basis). They changed the build of the model but not the conclusion: both sets arrive at FY2027 EPS of EUR 1.04 to 1.07 and FY2028 EPS of EUR 1.15 to 1.20.

The seven places where the sets jar, and what I conclude at each.

  1. "The underlying margin has been flat for two years" (second set) against the path inside FY2026. The full-year average of about 27.7% hides a fall. First half FY2026 without the write-down: 29.1% (and the first quarter was helped by "supplemental orders and extra factory shifts"). Third quarter: 26.8%, with no one-offs. Fourth quarter without the compensation: about 26% (Deutsche Bank's estimate, and what the mid-point of guidance implies). So the second half ran at about 26.3%, against the usual pattern of a stronger second half. The FY2027 input of 27.9% therefore needs about 160 basis points more than the rate at which the company leaves FY2026. Management says the production-optimisation costs that weighed on the third quarter end with the fiscal year (Jefferies, 12-Aug-2026), but it has not given their size. The second set called the third quarter "the most important single datum" and then modelled it as a trough. That is a reasonable call, and the informed brokers make the same one. But it is the base case's most exposed assumption, and "flat for two years" understates it.

  2. "Consensus misses the FY2027 capital-spending peak" (both sets) against the two-year totals. Consensus has EUR 156m (FY2026) and EUR 149m (FY2027), a total of EUR 305m. The second set has EUR 145m and EUR 165m, a total of EUR 310m. The disagreement is about the order of the two years, not the amount. Nine-month spending of EUR 82.5m makes consensus FY2026 too high. The consequence is a sequence. FY2026 free cash flow should meet or beat consensus (EUR 69m) on 10-Dec-2026, on the same day that the FY2027 capital-spending guide comes in above consensus. The cut to FY2027 consensus free cash flow (EUR 95m against our EUR 54m to 62m) is real, but about half of it is operating cash flow (joint-venture income not received in cash, and working capital), not capital spending.

  3. "Stevanato's freed capacity means sharper syringe tenders in 2027" (both sets) against the newer evidence. The customer that cut Stevanato's Nexa syringes was, with high confidence, Novo Nordisk: about 300m units for 2027, because Novo lost share to Eli Lilly and is moving to multi-dose pens (Deutsche Bank, 4-Nov and 11-Nov-2026). Stevanato has backfilled with other biologics, not with GLP-1 volume taken from rivals. Experts report no reallocation tenders, because a supplier change on a registered drug needs stability testing and refiling. Validation of a new programme takes 30 to 36 months. So freed capacity presses on awards for new programmes, which reach revenue from about FY2029. It does not press on FY2027 prices. The second set's FY2027 price input (+0.5%) is safer than its own text suggests. And about two-thirds of SCHOTT Pharma's GLP-1 revenue is with Eli Lilly, the originator that gained the share Novo lost. The sets treated the Stevanato event only as a negative read-across; for the next 18 months it is closer to neutral for SCHOTT Pharma's fundamentals, while remaining negative for sentiment.

  4. "No customer above 10% of sales" (first set, C-79) against the company's own disclosure. One customer was 10.3% of first-half FY2026 sales (interim report, 13-May-2026), about EUR 50m in the half. It is not named. The first set sized Eli Lilly at "about 8% of group sales", but that is Lilly's GLP-1 business only (two-thirds of a low-teens share). Lilly also buys insulin cartridges and vials. If the 10.3% customer is Lilly, the single-customer exposure is about a quarter larger than the figure used in the first set's risk arithmetic (C-81). The annual report on 10-Dec-2026 should show whether a customer exceeds 10% for the year.

  5. "Hedged across formats" (first set, C-03 and C-08; second set, P-05) against the split of SCHOTT Pharma's own GLP-1 revenue. The only estimate in the files is that about 70% of its GLP-1 revenue is glass syringes and about 30% ready-to-use cartridges (Deutsche Bank; the estimate dates from November 2023 and has not been updated). A monthly multi-dose cartridge at about US$0.05 to 0.06 replaces four single-dose syringes at about US$0.30 to 0.35 each. A shift of a patient from syringes to a multi-dose pen therefore keeps the customer but removes roughly 95% of the container revenue for that patient. The hedge protects the relationship, not the revenue. Two facts limit the risk inside 18 months. United States prescriptions still favour single-dose autoinjectors (Jefferies, October 2026). And the syringe volumes sit under a EUR 700m lifetime contract with minimum volumes.

  6. "Jefferies' working capital of about 32% of sales is a definitional outlier" (first set, C-52) against FactSet's own year-end figure. On the wider definition that FactSet and, evidently, Jefferies use, working capital was already 29.1% of revenue at 30-Sep-2025. Jefferies' 32% by FY2027 is therefore a forecast of a rise of about three points on a like-for-like definition, not a quirk. The second set's path has a rise of under one point. If Jefferies is nearer right, FY2026 and FY2027 free cash flow together are EUR 20m to 30m lower than our base. I keep the second set's path, because the company's own measure and the finance director's cash-linked pay argue against a large build, but this is a known downside to the cash numbers.

  7. "Polymer lines are under-used and will be refilled" (both sets) against the capacity still arriving. The programme to triple polymer syringe capacity at Müllheim runs to 2027 and has not been cancelled; the 2025 annual report still describes capital spending there as growth investment. RBC estimated that polymer demand fell about 30% in 2025. Qualifying a non-mRNA use takes two to three years. The second set credits polymer with EUR 2m to 3m a year of recovery from FY2027. The risk it does not carry is that under-absorption deepens before it eases, as the rest of the capacity is commissioned. The third quarter's better DDS growth (+5%, with "increasing dynamics from non-mRNA") is the evidence against that risk; it is one quarter.

Two smaller points. (a) The second set says the FY2027 guide is "likely to be beatable". On our own base (5.5% growth on the reported base) we sit in the lower half of the 5% to 7% range we expect the company to give. On growth the guide is one to be met, not beaten; it is beatable on margin only (I-01). (b) One AlphaSense report (file 06) says the FY2027 capital-spending peak is "driven by" the US$371m North Carolina plant. That is wrong. The fuller report on the plant (file 09) and the finance director's own words say it remains in the planning phase with no final investment decision. The second set is right to exclude it.

Net. The earlier answers support one another on direction everywhere, and on size nearly everywhere. The frictions are small and they partly offset: less price risk in FY2027, more margin risk at the starting point, more customer exposure. I leave the base-case forecasts where the second set put them.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

C+5514th of 54

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the dynamics that decide the next two quarters are well articulated, mostly by two houses (Deutsche Bank and Jefferies): the compensation in the FY2026 base, the "temporary" third-quarter margin, the concentration with Eli Lilly, the GLP-1 format and oral-drug debate, and the illiquid float. The share price shows that investors know the GLP-1 risk too, and currently weigh it more heavily than the sell-side does. What is not in the narrative falls into two groups. The positives that are missing are dated late: the free-cash-flow rise from FY2028 and net cash from FY2029, and the largely mechanical nature of the EPS recovery. The negatives that are missing are small or unlikely: the low margin at which FY2026 ends, joint-venture income that is in EBITDA but not in cash, falling ready-to-use vial prices, a customer above 10% of sales, the December 2027 parent contracts. On investor commentary I have no direct evidence: the files hold no social-media or fund commentary on this stock, and I infer investors' views from the share price, trading and ratings.

Each dynamic, and how well it is known.

Dynamic Sell-side Investors (inferred from price and positioning) Against our assessment
The EUR 15m compensation and the EUR 12.25m write-down: opposite effects on FY2027 growth and EBITDA Well known and prominent since July 2026 (Deutsche Bank expects a 5% to 7% guide; Jefferies calls it non-recurring) Known Fully reflected. A 5% to 7% guide should not surprise those who read the research; it may still surprise on the day, since FactSet's revenue consensus implies 6.0%
The third-quarter margin of 26.8% as a trough Widely discussed and accepted as temporary (three temporary factors, per Deutsche Bank) Accepted through October; unknown since The narrative and our base agree. What is not articulated is that the fourth quarter without the compensation is about 26%, so the second half ran at about 26.3%. A weak underlying fourth quarter on 10-Dec-2026 is a small unpriced negative
The quarterly shape of FY2027 (strong reported figures in May and August 2027, weak in December 2027) The base effect is known; the quarterly shape is not set out Not known A source of false signals in both directions

Synchronised syringe capacity; price pressure after Stevanato's cut Deutsche Bank calls Stevanato's cut idiosyncratic. The forecast of 20% excess syringe capacity by 2030 lives in expert calls, not in broker models. No broker models falling prices Feared: the shares fell 15% to 18% with Stevanato Investors are more worried than the sell-side. For FY2027 our view is closer to the sell-side's (D-01, point 3). For 2028 to 2030 it is closer to the investors'

The fall in vial prices comes from an expert call (27-Oct-2026) and is not in research Not known A small unpriced negative for DCS pricing, on which the company has just tripled its United States capacity
GLP-1 format shifts and oral drugs Widely debated for the sector (Deutsche Bank, Morgan Stanley, Jefferies' monthly prescription notes, Bernstein's 50% oral share by 2028). SCHOTT Pharma's own split by format has not been updated since 2023 Known and feared Reflected. A known unknown, not a hidden one
Concentration with Eli Lilly Well known (two-thirds of GLP-1 revenue, per Deutsche Bank), and now presented as protection, because Stevanato's cut came from Novo Nordisk Mixed The narrative has not joined two facts: Lilly is gaining share, and Lilly owns the most advanced oral drug. And a customer at 10.3% of first-half sales has gone unremarked
North Carolina (US$371m) Has fallen out of 2026 research Not known Neutral inside 18 months. A swing factor for free cash flow beyond
December 2027: the tubing agreement, the EUR 412m facilities, the 1.5% royalty A non-topic Not known An unpriced tail of low probability (about 10%)
The float and capital allocation Well known (Deutsche Bank: the stock is close to un-investable for many institutions; the parent "urgently needs to address" it). No plan from the parent Known; it is why few institutions can own it Reflected in the multiple. What net cash from FY2029 will be used for is not yet a topic
The basis of the tax rate Not discussed Not known Immaterial

Who carries the narrative. Ten brokers contribute estimates. The detailed argument sits with Deutsche Bank and Jefferies, with UBS and RBC adding recent notes. Coverage this thin means that a dynamic one house has not written about is, in practice, unknown. It also means one house's change of view moves the stock: Deutsche Bank's upgrade (July 2026) and downgrade (October 2026) marked the two turns of the year.

What the price and positioning say investors believe. The stock trades at 17.4x 24-month-forward consensus EPS, the 38th percentile of its own history, after peaking at 20.8x on 22-Oct-2026. It is about 11% below its 50-day average and 13% above its 200-day average, with a three-month return of -2.5% and a six-month return of +33%. The narrative has moved three times in a year: "credibility crisis" (December 2025), "past the trough" (July 2026), "GLP-1 contract risk across the group" (November 2026). Investors hold a recovery story that they have just marked down for an industry risk. They are not yet paying for proof of operating leverage.

The gap between narrative and our assessment.

  • Next 6 months. The sell-side's numbers are a little above ours. The investors' fear about GLP-1 contracts is a little worse than our reading of the next 18 months' fundamentals. The two roughly cancel. No positive that we hold and the market lacks falls due in this period.
  • Months 6 to 18. Reported figures will look better than the trend (May and August 2027) and then worse (December 2027). The missing positive, the fall in capital spending and the rise in free cash flow, becomes visible with the FY2028 guide in December 2027.
  • 18 months to three years. The narrative has not priced net cash from FY2029 and free cash flow of EUR 125m to 150m a year. It has priced, through fear and not through numbers, the over-capacity and oral-drug debates, which our forecasts carry only as a 25% bear case.

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 11 November 2026, 20:03 to 20:23 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
SCHOTT Pharma (1SXP-DE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-11-11 for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model. Share price EUR 21.15 to 21.40 (11-Nov-26); market value about EUR 3.1 billion; fiscal year ends 30 September; next results 10-Dec-2026, when the company reports FY2026 and gives its first FY2027 guidance.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Notes for the Consumer before the answers: the source check, the traps in the numbers and the underlying base

Source check. The latest Earnings Review does cover the period ending June 2026: it reviews the third quarter and first nine months of FY2026 (to 30-Jun-2026), pre-released on 8-Jul-2026 and reported in full on 12-Aug-2026. That matches the last period in the FactSet block (06/2026). The earlier review covers the second quarter (to 31-Mar-2026, reported 13-May-2026).

Seven traps the model must handle.

  1. Three EBITDA bases. The company's EBITDA carries no adjustments (Deutsche Bank) and matches the brokers' basis: FY2025 EUR 280.3m (28.4%) against brokers' EUR 278m. FactSet's "operating" EBITDA is EUR 25m to 35m a year lower (FY2025 EUR 255m, 25.9%; third quarter FY2026 EUR 67.0m against the company's EUR 75.5m). Most of the gap is the profit share of the equity-accounted joint ventures in India and Italy, which the company counts inside EBITDA although they add no revenue (with some other operating income, for example a EUR 1.6m United States tariff refund in FY2026). Model the joint-venture income as its own line: it lifts the reported margin by roughly two percentage points, and it will grow as the Indian joint venture adds syringe capacity. Model on the company basis, because consensus is on that basis.
  2. FY2026 holds two one-offs that almost cancel at EBITDA and do not cancel at revenue. A EUR 12.3m inventory write-down on one customer's glass syringes hit cost of sales in the first half (EUR 12.25m per the interim report; Jefferies put the second-quarter part at about EUR 7m). A compensation payment of about EUR 15m from the same customer (July 2026 agreement) is booked as revenue in the fourth quarter, at close to 100% margin. Net effect: about +EUR 2.7m on EBITDA but +EUR 15m (about 1.5 percentage points of growth) on revenue.
  3. FactSet's quarterly debt reads zero. The peer table's "net cash (0.1x)" is an artefact. Use the company's net debt of EUR 106m at 30-Jun-2026 (EUR 122m to 127m at 30-Sep-2025), against gross financial liabilities to the parent of EUR 231m (30-Jun-2026). The parent's revolving facilities total EUR 412m and terminate on 31-Dec-2027. Lease liabilities (EUR 24.9m) sit outside the company's net debt.
  4. FactSet's quarterly working capital is unusable (year-end spikes to 29% of revenue, other quarters 7% to 16%). Use the company's 20% to 22% of revenue (20.2% FY2025, 21.8% at Q3 FY2026).
  5. The segment split is 55/45, not 73/27. FY2025: Drug Containment Solutions (DCS) EUR 547m, Drug Delivery Systems (DDS) EUR 439m. In the nine months to June 2026, DCS was EUR 445.4m (+9.2% at constant currency) and DDS EUR 325.1m (-1.7%). The DDS segment EBITDA was EUR 95.7m (29.4% margin, -16.3%). That leaves DCS at about 25% (derived; corporate costs are not given separately).
  6. The FY2024 EBITDA margin was 26.9% as reported. The "record 27.8%" was at constant currency.
  7. Fiscal years. FY2027 runs from October 2026 to September 2027, so "the next 18 months" covers FY2027 and the first half of FY2028.

The underlying base (my derivation from consensus and the disclosed one-offs; tax at 22%).

Revenue (EURm) EBITDA (EURm, margin) EPS (EUR)
FY2025 actual 986 280 (28.4%) 0.97
FY2026 consensus as reported 1,041 285 (27.4%) 0.98
FY2026 without the compensation about 1,026 about 270 (26.3%) about 0.90
FY2026 without both one-offs about 1,026 about 282 (27.5%) about 0.97
FY2027 consensus 1,104 (+6.0% reported, about +7.6% underlying) 310 (28.1%; about +10% underlying) 1.10 (+12% reported, about +14% underlying)

So the base effect that the market worries about bites at revenue, not at EBITDA. The real FY2027 question is different: can EBITDA grow about 10% and the margin rise about 60 basis points on an underlying basis in the year when capital spending peaks?

The single most important judgement in this set. SCHOTT Pharma's franchise is strong and getting stronger in containment (vials, cartridges, ready-to-use formats). Its earnings power, though, has stalled for four years. Gross margin fell from 36.0% (FY2022) to 31.3% (nine months FY2026). EPS has been flat at about EUR 1.00 since FY2023, while revenue grew 16% and more than EUR 460m went into capital spending. Recent capital has earned close to nothing so far. The investment case for the next four years therefore rests on one transmission mechanism: whether capacity already built (Hungarian syringes and cartridges, Swiss cartridges, Lebanon ready-to-use vials, Müllheim polymer and specialty lines, and possibly North Carolina) fills at today's prices. That has to happen in an industry where the three leaders are adding capacity together, syringe utilisation has already fallen to about 90%, and customers have just shown they can cut volumes (SCHOTT Pharma in late 2025, Stevanato on 3-Nov-2026). The franchise is not in question. What is in question is the utilisation-to-margin transmission and the price of new volume.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company: Competitive advantage · Group: Company: Economic strength · Group: Company: Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Steer for the Consumer: what the first set implies for the operating and financial model

  1. Revenue: model DCS at about 7% to 9% organic and DDS at about 3% to 6% organic for FY2027 to FY2029 (GLP-1 about 10% to 15% growth; polymer flat to slightly up from FY2027; vaccines -5% to -10%). Group organic about 6.5% to 7.5% underlying, minus about 1.5 points of reported growth in FY2027 for the EUR 15m base. FX: ±3% band, slight headwind bias.
  2. Price: like-for-like price 0% to +1% in FY2027, 0% in FY2028 and FY2029 in the base case; a bear case of -2% a year from 2028.
  3. Margins (company EBITDA basis, including joint ventures): FY2026 about 27.5% to 28% reported (27.5% without both one-offs); FY2027 about 27.0% to 27.6%; FY2028 about 28.3%; FY2029 about 29%. Contribution on volume through existing plants about 45% to 55%; price drop-through close to 100%.
  4. Below EBITDA: depreciation 8.5% to 9% of sales; net financial charge about EUR 6m to 10m; tax 22%; share count 150.6m; minorities negligible; joint-venture income about EUR 20m to 30m inside EBITDA and growing.
  5. Cash: capital spending EUR 140m to 150m (FY2026), EUR 155m to 170m (FY2027 peak), EUR 130m to 140m (FY2028), EUR 120m to 135m (FY2029). Working capital 21% to 23% of revenue. Dividend payout about 18%. No buybacks, no M&A.
  6. Balance sheet: gross parent debt about EUR 230m, net debt about EUR 100m to 130m at FY2026 year-end, falling from FY2028; parent facilities due 31-Dec-2027 (assume rolled).
  7. Resulting EPS (my central case): about EUR 0.98 (FY2026 reported), EUR 1.04 to 1.07 (FY2027), EUR 1.15 to 1.20 (FY2028), EUR 1.27 to 1.32 (FY2029), against consensus of 0.98, 1.10, 1.22 and 1.32.
Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: SCHOTT Pharma AG & Co. KGaA (1SXP-DE), a German maker of primary packaging for injectable drugs (glass vials, ampoules, cartridges, and glass and polymer prefillable syringes, increasingly as sterile ready-to-use formats), reporting two segments: Drug Containment Solutions (vials, ampoules, cartridges) and Drug Delivery Systems (glass and polymer prefillable syringes); 77% owned by SCHOTT AG (Carl Zeiss Foundation), with fiscal year ending 30 September.

Request: T-P1

Peers: Stevanato Group (closest listed rival in glass syringes, cartridges and ready-to-use formats, with a new Fishers, Indiana plant still ramping); Gerresheimer (listed rival in vials and syringes, running the gto transformation programme and closing Chicago Heights); West Pharmaceutical Services (high-value elastomer components, a long precedent of margin expansion through mix with full plants); Becton Dickinson Pharmaceutical Systems (leading prefillable syringe maker, scale-cost precedent); Sartorius Stedim Biotech (bioprocess consumables, a precedent for margins through the 2023 to 2024 destocking and recovery); GVS (medtech consumables, footprint consolidation and Puerto Rico closure); Nolato (medical pens and components, start-up cost precedent in US plants); SGD Pharma (private bulk glass vial maker, cost behaviour under falling bulk prices). Inputs: What share of SCHOTT Pharma's cost base is fixed and what share is variable (glass tubing bought from SCHOTT AG, other materials, energy, labour, depreciation), and how did EBITDA move with volume from FY2021 to FY2026, including the Drug Delivery Systems EBITDA falling 16.3% to EUR 95.7m in the nine months to June 2026 (company Q3 FY2026 interim report, 12-Aug-2026) and the third-quarter FY2026 group EBITDA margin of 26.8% against 32.3% a year earlier? How large are the start-up costs at new sites (Lukácsháza in Hungary for syringes and a new ready-to-use cartridge plant, St. Gallen cartridges, Jagodina in Serbia for ampoules, Lebanon in Pennsylvania for ready-to-use vials, Müllheim polymer and specialty lines), how long do they last, and what is the current status and expected timing of the North Carolina prefillable syringe plant announced in 2024? How much fixed cost sits in the polymer syringe lines left under-used after mRNA vaccine volumes fell, and what are the options and costs to redeploy or close them? What are the content, phasing and savings delivered so far of the efficiency programme confirmed by finance director Reinhard Mayer (a double-digit million euro amount, mainly digitalisation)? How will depreciation evolve as capital spending peaks (management said investments peak in fiscal 2027; Q3 FY2026 call, 12-Aug-2026) and capital intensity falls (Deutsche Bank expects low 10s % of revenue)? Are the 1.5% brand royalty to SCHOTT AG and the tubing framework agreement (fixed until December 2027) reducible? For each peer, how long did new sterile or syringe capacity take to reach full utilisation, and what did margins do meanwhile (Stevanato Fishers and Latina; Gerresheimer US and Mexico expansions)? What did peer cost programmes promise against what they delivered? Focus: How much of SCHOTT Pharma's recently built capacity will convert into margin as volumes rise, and how quickly: the incremental margin on volume through existing plants, set against start-up costs and rising depreciation. Peer precedents for the time from commissioning to full utilisation would most help to set the 2027 to 2030 margin path.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Stevanato Group (closest rival in glass syringes and ready-to-use cartridges, lost GLP-1 syringe volume in November 2026); Gerresheimer (vials and syringes, under financial pressure and seen by some buyers as less trustworthy); West Pharmaceutical Services (premium elastomer components with a stated annual pricing practice); Becton Dickinson (syringe scale leader, priced below specialist rivals); SGD Pharma and Nipro (bulk glass vials, the commodity end of the market); Corning (Valor glass and tubing, an alternative glass supplier); Nolato and Ypsomed (pen and autoinjector suppliers facing expected device price falls). Operating conditions: a supply shortage (2020 to 2022 pandemic period); post-pandemic destocking and overcapacity in bulk glass (2023 to 2025); industry-wide capacity additions in glass syringes and ready-to-use formats with utilisation falling (2026 to 2030); multi-year contract renewals and take-or-pay renegotiations; sudden volume cuts by a single large customer; input cost inflation in energy and labour, including a strong Swiss franc; per-dose price deflation in GLP-1 drugs as generics and lower US list prices arrive; regulatory-driven format change (EU GMP Annex 1 and the US FDA's August 2026 draft container-closure guidance). Inputs: What prices has SCHOTT Pharma achieved and held, by product (bulk vials, ampoules, ready-to-use vials such as adaptiQ, cartridges including cartriQ, glass prefillable syringes, polymer syringes), from 2020 to 2026, and where has it given price back? How do its contracts work in practice: three-year locked prices with minimum volumes, glass cost indexation every six months and labour and overhead adjustments each February (expert call, Associate Director MSD, 7-Nov-2025), and volume-based pricing within take-or-pay terms? What were the terms and the price effects of the July 2026 agreement with a glass syringe customer, which included a compensation payment of about EUR 15m (Deutsche Bank, 9-Oct-2026)? How widespread are renewal concessions: customers demanding 5.0% to 7.5% on multi-year renewals (former SGD Pharma procurement director), glass makers conceding 5% to 10% (former SGD Pharma director, 19-Dec-2025), and standard 6ml ready-to-use vial prices falling from EUR 0.75 to EUR 0.35 to 0.40 per unit in 2026 (Gerresheimer executive)? What premium does SCHOTT Pharma command over Stevanato, BD and Gerresheimer by product, given that an Eli Lilly engineering adviser described SCHOTT Pharma and Stevanato syringes as functionally equivalent (expert call, 29-May-2026)? How do West's stated 2% to 3% annual price reviews and GVS's disclosed price contributions compare? Did the pandemic price rises of 25% to 40% in glass hold or get clawed back, and how fast? How will SCHOTT AG price tubing at the December 2027 renewal? Does the FDA draft container-closure guidance strengthen incumbents' pricing? Focus: Whether SCHOTT Pharma has demonstrable, real pricing power product by product, rather than index pass-through. How like-for-like prices on new and renewed contracts are likely to behave from 2027 to 2030 as syringe and ready-to-use capacity rises across the industry.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: The answers show the case turns on whether capacity filling, customer concentration and industry-wide capacity additions reinforce one another (filling capacity lifts margins and funds more conversion) or work against each other (idle capacity at one supplier drives price competition for all). Peers: Stevanato Group (its November 2026 GLP-1 customer cut and backfilling of freed capacity); Gerresheimer (capacity closures and loss of customer trust, a possible share donor); West Pharmaceutical Services (a long high-value mix loop with full plants); Becton Dickinson (scale pricing in syringes); Lonza and Thermo Fisher (sterile fill-finish capacity build-outs and the pandemic vaccine-capacity overhang precedent); Sartorius Stedim Biotech (the 2023 to 2024 bioprocess destocking and recovery loop). Inputs: Capacity filling: as new lines in Hungary, Switzerland and the United States fill, does absorption lift margins and fund further ready-to-use conversion, and what stalls it? Synchronised capacity: industry experts project 10% to 20% excess syringe capacity within three to five years and 20% overcapacity by 2030, with glass syringe utilisation falling from 100% to 90% in 2026 (Aptar Group executives); does lower utilisation lead to price competition and then slower capital spending, and how long did similar cycles last? Customer cut and backfill: after Stevanato's GLP-1 customer cut (shares down 28%, 3-Nov-2026), does the freed capacity bid for other volume and press prices across suppliers? Regulatory lock-in: does each new drug registered in a supplier's containment build an annuity that strengthens the supplier's position year by year, and how fast is that installed base growing? Ready-to-use adoption under Annex 1: does more ready-to-use capacity lower ready-to-use prices and so speed adoption, or erode the premium? GLP-1 price deflation: do lower per-dose prices and generics raise patient numbers and container volumes while shifting demand to cheaper formats (vials, multi-dose pens)? Tubing integration: does SCHOTT AG's tubing position give SCHOTT Pharma an advantage only in shortages? Focus: Which loop will dominate SCHOTT Pharma's margins from 2027 to 2030: the capacity-filling reinforcing loop or the overcapacity and price balancing loop. What are the earliest dated signs of which one is winning?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: The answers show earnings per share has stayed flat since FY2023 despite revenue growth, because items below EBITDA and outside revenue absorbed the growth, so the FY2027 to FY2029 bridge depends on several inputs the company discloses only partly. Peers: Stevanato Group (capital-heavy growth with a similar depreciation and financing profile); Gerresheimer (leverage and interest cost precedent); West Pharmaceutical Services (capital-spending taper and free cash flow precedent); GVS (equity, tax and financing items in a family-controlled medtech consumables company); Nolato (medical consumables with heavy capital spending in new plants). Inputs: The equity-accounted joint ventures (SCHOTT Pharma India, renamed from SCHOTT Poonawalla in October 2026 and 35% owned by TPG, and the Italian joint venture): how much profit they contribute inside reported EBITDA and how fast it can grow as Indian syringe capacity is added. The gap between the company's EBITDA and FactSet's operating EBITDA, and what explains it. Depreciation as new plants are commissioned. The net financial result after the intra-group financing restructuring in the first quarter of FY2026, and the terms of the SCHOTT AG revolving facilities (EUR 412m, ending 31-Dec-2027; company disclosures). The tax rate path (23.5% in FY2025 against about 20% before). Currency effects: the US dollar at about 30% of sales, a net short Swiss franc position of CHF 135m at 30-Sep-2025, no hedge accounting and twelve-month hedges. The FY2026 one-offs (an inventory impairment of EUR 12.25m in the first half; a compensation payment of about EUR 15m in the fourth quarter). Capital spending (FY2026 guidance EUR 140m to 160m; peak in fiscal 2027 per the finance director) and working capital as ready-to-use formats grow. The dividend payout policy of 10% to 20%. The brand royalty of 1.5% of sales. Any plan by SCHOTT AG regarding the free float. Focus: For each input, what the company has guided, what the sell-side assumes (including UBS, Deutsche Bank, Jefferies, RBC and Stifel forecasts in autumn 2026) and how far apart they are. What would move each input materially between FY2027 and FY2029.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 12 November 2026, 04:21 to 04:37 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
SCHOTT Pharma (1SXP-DE): second set of questions, the financial forecasts

Written 2026-11-12 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Share price EUR 21.15 to 21.40 (11-Nov-26). Fiscal year ends 30 September. FY2026 results and the first FY2027 guidance come on 10-Dec-2026. "The next 18 months" covers FY2027 and the first quarter of FY2028 (October 2026 to December 2027).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

  1. Five corrections to the numbers the first set used. They change how the model must be built.

  1. The FY2025 financial result was about EUR -13m on the company basis, not EUR -19.7m. FactSet's pre-tax profit (EUR 174m) leaves out the EUR 14.0m of joint-venture net income (India EUR 11.98m, Italy EUR 2.06m; annual report 2025). FactSet's operating EBIT (EUR 176m) also leaves out about EUR 11m of other operating income. Rebuilt on the company basis: EBITDA EUR 280.3m, less depreciation EUR 79.4m, gives EBIT of about EUR 201m. Pre-tax profit including the joint ventures was about EUR 188m, so the financial result was about EUR -13m. That fits the company's disclosed interest expense of EUR 12.3m, of which EUR 12.2m was paid to SCHOTT AG. The nine-month FY2026 financial result was EUR -6.2m, EUR 3.4m better than a year earlier.
  2. Two tax rates are in circulation. The 23.5% FY2025 rate is tax divided by pre-tax profit excluding the joint ventures (EUR 40.9m on EUR 174m). On pre-tax profit including them, which is the basis a model has to use when EBITDA includes joint-venture income, the rate was about 21.8% in FY2025. It was about 22.9% in the nine months of FY2026 (derived: EUR 103.0m of net income on about EUR 133.6m of pre-tax profit). Mixing the two bases misstates EPS by about 2%.
  3. FactSet's free cash flow line here deducts dividends. FY2025: operating cash flow EUR 180m, less capital spending EUR 144m, less dividends EUR 24m, gives FactSet's EUR 11.5m. The company's EUR 36.8m is operating cash flow less capital spending. On that basis FY2024 free cash flow was about EUR 80m, 53% of net income, against 25% in FY2025. Consensus free cash flow (EUR 69m, 95m, 125m, 129m) is roughly operating cash flow less capital spending, before dividends and leases.
  4. UBS's EBITDA is on a different basis, so it is not the bear signal the first set took it for. UBS's FY2026 EBITDA of EUR 272m on EUR 1,043m of revenue is a 26.1% margin. That is below the floor of the company's own 27% to 28% guidance, so UBS almost certainly excludes the joint-venture and other operating income, as FactSet does. One restricted broker shows the same, at EUR 272m on EUR 1,046m. So UBS's FY2027 EBITDA of EUR 281m says nothing about the company-basis median of EUR 310m. The real signal sits in EPS: UBS (EUR 1.04, 6-Nov-26) and Deutsche Bank (EUR 1.05, 8-Oct-26) are at the bottom of the EUR 1.04 to 1.16 range. Their EBITDA, put on the same basis, is close to everyone else's. The FY2027 consensus EPS gap is mostly below EBITDA: the financial result and depreciation.
  5. The EBITDA margin has been flat on an underlying basis. It has not been falling. FY2025's 28.4% was lifted by an exceptional third quarter, at 32.3% (EUR 82.7m). Had that quarter run at the year's other-quarter average, FY2025 would have been about 27.4%. In FY2026, the first half's underlying margin, without the EUR 12.25m write-down, was about 29.1%, against 27.1% a year earlier. The nine months were 28.3%, and the full year without both one-offs comes to about 27.7%. So the underlying EBITDA margin has been about 27.5% to 28% for two years. The real erosion is at gross margin and EBIT, where depreciation rose from 6.7% to 8.5% of sales. The most important single datum is the third quarter of FY2026, at 26.8% with no one-offs: the lowest underlying quarter of the year, weighed down by "process-optimisation costs" and under-used polymer lines in DDS. The FY2027 margin depends on whether that quarter is a trough or a run-rate.

  1. The single most important judgement in this set

Operating leverage at SCHOTT Pharma is real, but most of it has been, and will keep being, absorbed by the capacity the company keeps adding. Since FY2022 the realised incremental EBITDA margin has been about 32% (EBITDA up about EUR 65m on about EUR 206m of extra underlying revenue, FY2022 to FY2026). That is well below the 45% to 55% marginal contribution of volume through plants already built, because growth has needed new sites, new staff and start-up costs. At EBIT the leverage was close to zero: depreciation rose about 14% a year. The forecast question is therefore not "does operating leverage exist" but "how much of FY2027 to FY2030 growth runs through capacity already built, and how much needs new fixed cost". My base case uses a blended incremental margin of about 30% in FY2027 and 36% to 37% in FY2028 to FY2030. That lifts the EBITDA margin 40 to 50 basis points a year, to 29.3% in FY2030, about a year behind the company's "towards 30% by FY2029". Because depreciation growth slows only from FY2029, the EBIT margin regains its FY2025 level (20.4%) only in FY2029. EPS growth then comes increasingly from below the operating line (a falling financial charge) and from the capital-spending taper (free cash flow). My EPS is 5% to 6% below consensus in FY2027 and FY2028. My free cash flow is well below consensus in FY2027 and in line from FY2029. The FY2027 shortfall is about EUR 0.06 of EPS, and the largest part of it sits below EBITDA.

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's closing notes

Steer for the Consumer: what to test first in the third and fourth sets

  1. Test the third quarter of FY2026 as trough or run-rate. The first-quarter FY2027 group margin (February 2027) against 26.8%, and the DDS margin against 29.4%. Above 28% and 31% respectively supports consensus. Below 27% and 29% supports my bear case.
  2. Read the FY2027 guide on 10-Dec-2026 on two bases. Organic growth on the reported FY2026 base (which includes about EUR 15m of compensation) against my about 5.5%. The EBITDA margin against my 27.9%. A guide of "around 28%" is in line with my base. "27% to 28%" is in line but would read soft. "28% to 29%" would be a bull signal.
  3. Treat the consensus EPS gap as mainly below EBITDA. Check each broker's financial result and depreciation assumptions. Do not read UBS's EUR 281m FY2027 EBITDA as like-for-like (note 2.4).
  4. Watch capital spending against free cash flow. The FY2027 capital-spending guide (my EUR 165m against consensus EUR 149m) and any decision on North Carolina. These two items move FY2027 to FY2030 free cash flow by more than any operating variable.
  5. Watch the December 2027 dates. The parent tubing agreement and the EUR 412m facilities both reach their terms in the last month of the 18-month window.
  6. Track the pricing signals. Syringe and ready-to-use vial tender pricing after Stevanato's backfill (Stevanato fourth-quarter 2026 results, about March 2027). Ready-to-use cartridge tightness past 2027.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports two segments, with revenue and EBITDA for each. Corporate costs are not shown separately: in the nine months of FY2026, segment EBITDA (DCS EUR 109.7m plus DDS EUR 95.7m) equals group EBITDA (EUR 205.3m). All joint-venture income sits in DCS. My segment figures are approximate and add up to the group figures within about EUR 1m to 2m.

Drug Containment Solutions (vials, ampoules, cartridges): revenue, EURm (growth) 547 about 592 (+8.3%) about 640 (+8.0%) about 688 (+7.5%) about 736 (+7.0%) about 784 (+6.5%)
DCS EBITDA margin (includes all joint-venture income, about 2.5 points) about 24% about 24.5% about 25.0% about 25.3% about 25.6% about 25.9%
Drug Delivery Systems (glass and polymer prefillable syringes): revenue, EURm (growth) 439 about 450 (+2.5%; about 435 and -1% without the EUR 15m compensation) about 459 (+2.0%; +5.5% underlying) about 483 (+5.2%) about 510 (+5.6%) about 538 (+5.5%)

Nine-month FY2026 actuals: DCS revenue EUR 445.4m (+9.2% at constant currency), EBITDA EUR 109.7m (24.6%, against 24.1%). DDS revenue EUR 325.1m (-1.7%), EBITDA EUR 95.7m (29.4% to 29.5%, against 35.1%; -16.3%), including the EUR 12.25m syringe inventory write-down. I assume the fourth-quarter compensation (about EUR 15m) is booked in DDS revenue.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue. Underlying organic growth (constant currency, without the compensation) of about 4.5% in FY2026, 7.0% in FY2027, 6.5% in FY2028 and FY2029, and 6.0% in FY2030. FY2027 reported growth (5.5%) is about 1.5 points lower because the FY2026 base contains about EUR 15m of compensation. Drivers: the injectables packaging market grows about 9% a year. The drag from vaccines and mRNA polymer fades: mRNA fell from about 8% to about 5% of sales in FY2026, about -3 points of growth, and from FY2027 it plateaus. GLP-1 revenue (about EUR 125m to 135m in FY2026) grows 12% to 15% a year. The high-value share rises from 57% to about 60% by FY2029. Like-for-like price is about +0.5% in FY2027 and 0% thereafter. Currency is assumed neutral (spot euro-dollar about 1.12 would add about 0.5 to 1 point in the first half of FY2027). North Carolina adds no revenue inside the window.
  • EBITDA margin (company basis, including joint-venture income). The FY2026 underlying base is about 27.7% (reported 27.5%, with the EUR 12.25m write-down and the EUR 15m compensation netting to about EUR +2m). Since FY2022 the realised incremental margin on extra revenue has been about 32%; marginal contribution through plants already built is 45% to 55%. I use about 30% in FY2027 (the third-quarter FY2026 exit rate of 26.8%, start-up costs at the new Hungarian ready-to-use cartridge plant, syringe tender pressure after Stevanato's November 2026 customer cut), then 36% to 37% in FY2028 to FY2030 as Hungarian syringes, St. Gallen cartridges and Lebanon vials fill. The efficiency programme adds about EUR 10m to 13m net by FY2029. Joint-venture income grows from about EUR 15m to about EUR 21m. Wage inflation is half recovered through February labour indexation.
  • Quarterly phasing (Part 3). The fourth quarter of FY2026 is revenue of about EUR 272m (+10%; about +4% without the compensation) and EBITDA of about EUR 81.5m (30.0%; about 26% without it). In FY2027, the first quarter laps a quarter flattered by extra shifts. The second laps the trough (DDS -9.8% organic) and about EUR 7m of write-down. The third laps the 8.3% organic beat but also the 26.8% margin. The fourth laps the compensation. Reported growth and margin will therefore look strong in the second and third quarters of FY2027 and weak in the fourth, with no change in the underlying trend.
  • Depreciation. Nine months of FY2026 EUR 65.5m (+14%); the third quarter about EUR 22.2m. It rises with plants commissioned through FY2027 and then slows as capital spending tapers. It is 8.5% to 8.8% of sales.
  • Net financial items. These are on the company basis: interest on parent cash-pool debt, pension and lease interest, facility fees and currency-derivative results. Joint-venture income is excluded, because it sits in EBITDA. Nine months of FY2026 EUR -6.2m; FY2025 about EUR -13m (derived). The charge falls as free cash flow turns net debt (about EUR 104m at September 2026) into net cash in FY2029. The parent facilities (EUR 412m, ending 31-Dec-2027) are assumed rolled on similar terms.
  • Tax. The rate applies to pre-tax profit including joint-venture income: about 21.8% in FY2025 and about 22.9% in the nine months of FY2026 (derived). Pillar Two sets a floor. The 23.5% the company quotes for FY2025 is measured on pre-tax profit excluding the joint ventures, and must not be used with an EBITDA that includes them.
  • Dividend. A 10% to 20% payout; about 18% assumed. EUR 0.18 for FY2026 (EPS flat), then rising with EPS. Cash is paid in February of the following fiscal year.
  • Share count. Constant at 150.6m. Long-term incentives are virtual shares settled in cash. No buyback is expected, given the KGaA form, 77% parent voting control and the thin free float.
  • Capital spending. FY2026 guidance EUR 140m to 160m; nine months EUR 82.5m. The fourth quarter is back-loaded, as it was in FY2025, giving about EUR 145m. The finance director says investment peaks in FY2027 (EUR 165m assumed; Jefferies EUR 163m) and then tapers to about 10% to 11% of sales. The US$371m North Carolina syringe plant "remains in the planning phase" (finance director) and is not included. Committing it would add EUR 40m to 60m a year from about FY2028.
  • Working capital. On the company basis, 20.2% at September 2025 and 21.8% at June 2026. FactSet's quarterly working capital is not usable. It rises slowly with the ready-to-use mix (longer production cycles), and the collections focus behind the finance director's cash incentive offsets part of that.
  • Lease payments. Lease liabilities are EUR 24.9m, outside the company's net debt. The annual principal payment is estimated at EUR 7.5m to 8.5m.
